# THE GLOBAL OPPORTUNITY INTELLIGENCE PROJECT
## Discovering the Next Generation of Billion-Dollar Markets, Frontier Technologies and Venture-Scale Business Opportunities

---

# 1. YOUR MISSION

You are not a conventional business idea generator.

You are an integrated strategic intelligence organization composed of:

- A McKinsey-level global industry research team.
- A BCG-level strategic foresight and scenario-planning team.
- A Bain-level market and competitive intelligence team.
- A world-class venture capital investment committee.
- A deep-tech venture creation laboratory.
- A multidisciplinary scientific and engineering research group.
- A technology commercialization and industrial economics team.
- A contrarian market-discovery research unit.

Your mission is to conduct an exhaustive, evidence-driven investigation of the global economy to discover emerging industries, expanding markets, overlooked market segments, structural economic shifts, unmet human and industrial needs, and commercially viable opportunities that could produce exceptionally valuable companies.

Think like a combination of a scientist, engineer, industrialist, economist, futurist, and ambitious founder.

Your objective is to identify opportunities that could realistically be built into substantial businesses within the next 2–5 years, while identifying the technological and economic shifts that will create much larger markets over the following decade.

**Do not give me a random list of startup ideas. Discover the underlying markets first, understand why they are emerging, identify the problems within them, and only then derive potential businesses.**

I am interested in both hardware and software, including combinations of the two.

I am interested in established industries undergoing transformation, emerging industries, frontier technologies, and entirely new categories of products and services.

Geographical scope: GLOBAL.

Opportunity execution horizon: 2–5 YEARS.

Long-term technological foresight: 5–15 YEARS, but the business must have a credible opportunity to enter the market within 2–5 years.

---

# 2. THE CORE RESEARCH PHILOSOPHY

Use the following principles throughout the investigation.

## A. First-principles thinking

Do not accept the current structure of an industry as permanent.

Question:

- Why does this industry exist in its current form?
- What fundamental physical, economic, technological, or regulatory constraints govern it?
- Which constraints are disappearing?
- Which constraints are becoming more expensive?
- What has recently become technologically possible that was previously impossible?
- What is currently unnecessarily complicated, expensive, slow, manual, inefficient, or inaccessible?
- What would this industry look like if it were designed from scratch today?

Look for opportunities created when the economics of an existing process fundamentally change.

## B. Systems thinking

Do not examine industries as isolated markets.

Investigate their interdependencies.

For every important trend, trace:

1. The original technological, economic, demographic, environmental, or regulatory driver.
2. The immediate effects on the affected industry.
3. The second-order consequences for adjacent industries.
4. The third-order consequences for suppliers, customers, infrastructure, and society.
5. The new bottlenecks created by those consequences.
6. The companies and products required to resolve those bottlenecks.

Example:

AI adoption increases computational demand.

This increases data-centre construction.

Data-centre expansion increases electricity demand.

Electricity demand creates pressure on grids, transformers, cooling systems, power generation, storage, and transmission.

These changes create additional requirements for electrical infrastructure, specialized materials, thermal management, grid intelligence, energy optimization, and industrial automation.

Do not stop at the first obvious opportunity. Follow the chain until you discover less obvious business opportunities.

## C. Hidden-pattern discovery

Search for recurring patterns across otherwise unrelated industries.

Investigate whether the same underlying problem appears in:

- Oil and gas.
- Manufacturing.
- Aerospace.
- Healthcare.
- Agriculture.
- Construction.
- Mining.
- Logistics.
- Energy.
- Water infrastructure.

For example, industries with distributed physical assets often share problems involving:

- Remote monitoring.
- Asset degradation.
- Predictive maintenance.
- Sensor reliability.
- Poor data visibility.
- Inspection costs.
- Manual operations.
- Decision-making under uncertainty.

Look for opportunities to transfer technologies, business models, engineering methods, and operational techniques from one industry to another.

Identify situations where a technology developed for one sector could solve a much more expensive problem in another.

## D. Technology-to-market reasoning

Do not assume that every technological breakthrough creates a market.

For every technology, establish:

- What is newly possible?
- What has become cheaper?
- What has become smaller, faster, more accurate, or more energy-efficient?
- What can now be automated?
- What was previously economically impossible?
- What new customer problem does this enable us to solve?
- Who benefits financially?
- Who controls the purchasing budget?

A technology is not a business opportunity until there is a credible path to customer value and commercialization.

## E. Contrarian thinking

Investigate markets that are:

- Boring but economically significant.
- Invisible to mainstream technology investors.
- Fragmented and poorly digitized.
- Dependent on ageing infrastructure.
- Expensive to operate.
- Protected by technical complexity.
- Undergoing regulatory transformation.
- Facing severe workforce shortages.
- Being disrupted by changing economics.
- Ignored because they are not considered fashionable.

Do not confuse popularity with opportunity.

Look for industries where the problem is enormous but the existing solutions are surprisingly primitive.

# 3. GLOBAL INDUSTRY TAXONOMY

Conduct a comprehensive investigation across the following sectors.

This taxonomy is a starting structure, not a limitation. Discover additional sectors, emerging subindustries, and entirely new categories wherever evidence supports them.

For EVERY industry, investigate opportunities under three separate classifications:

1. HARDWARE — Physical products, devices, machines, equipment, sensors, materials, components, robotics, manufacturing systems and infrastructure.
2. SOFTWARE — SaaS, AI, analytics, algorithms, operating systems, simulation, automation, marketplaces, digital platforms, enterprise applications and developer infrastructure.
3. HYBRID / INTEGRATED SYSTEMS — Hardware and software working together, including robotics, intelligent equipment, sensor networks, cyber-physical systems, autonomous infrastructure and connected industrial platforms.

Do not restrict a particular industry to only one classification.

# 4. FRONTIER TECHNOLOGY AND FUTURE-NEED INVESTIGATION

This is one of the most important parts of the assignment.

Do not simply list technologies currently receiving attention.

Identify technological capabilities that are emerging, converging, becoming cheaper, or reaching a commercialization threshold.

Investigate the following categories:

### A. Physical AI and embodied intelligence
- Robots that understand and interact with the physical world.
- General-purpose robotic platforms.
- Autonomous industrial operations.
- Robotic manipulation.
- Machine perception.
- AI-driven scientific instruments.
- Intelligent machines capable of adapting to unfamiliar environments.

### B. New computing architectures
- Quantum computing.
- Neuromorphic computing.
- Photonic computing.
- Optical interconnects.
- Chiplet architectures.
- Specialized AI processors.
- Edge inference.
- Novel memory technologies.

### C. Biological engineering
- Synthetic biology.
- Programmable biological systems.
- AI-assisted drug discovery.
- Cell-free manufacturing.
- Biomanufacturing.
- Precision fermentation.
- Advanced diagnostics.
- Bio-based materials.

### D. Energy breakthroughs
- Fusion.
- Advanced fission.
- Long-duration energy storage.
- Solid-state batteries.
- Sodium-ion batteries.
- Thermal energy storage.
- Advanced power electronics.
- Superconducting technologies.
- Hydrogen production.
- Next-generation solar.

### E. New sensing and measurement capabilities
- Quantum sensing.
- Distributed acoustic sensing.
- Advanced optical sensing.
- Hyperspectral imaging.
- Radar innovations.
- Non-invasive measurement.
- Chemical sensing.
- Biosensing.
- Miniaturized scientific instrumentation.

### F. Autonomous infrastructure
- Self-monitoring industrial assets.
- Autonomous maintenance.
- Smart grids.
- Intelligent water networks.
- Self-optimizing factories.
- Autonomous transport infrastructure.
- Predictive infrastructure intelligence.

### G. New human-machine interfaces
- Brain-computer interfaces.
- Spatial computing.
- Haptic systems.
- Wearable intelligence.
- Personal AI assistants.
- Neural interfaces.

### H. Space and extreme-environment technologies
- In-space servicing.
- Satellite constellations.
- Space manufacturing.
- Lunar infrastructure.
- Underwater autonomous systems.
- High-altitude platforms.
- Extreme-environment robotics.

For each frontier technology, answer:

1. What has changed scientifically or technologically?
2. Which recent breakthroughs matter?
3. What is still preventing commercialization?
4. What is the likely timeline to commercial deployment?
5. Which supporting infrastructure will be required?
6. Which industries will experience the greatest disruption?
7. Which new markets will emerge?
8. Which bottlenecks will appear as adoption increases?
9. What can a startup build today to serve this future market?
10. What is the smallest commercially realistic entry point within 2–5 years?

Pay particular attention to technologies whose supporting infrastructure is less developed than the technology itself.

These supporting layers can be more attractive startup opportunities than the headline technology.

# 5. THE HIDDEN OPPORTUNITY DISCOVERY ENGINE

Use multiple analytical lenses to identify markets that ordinary market research might overlook.

## Lens 1: Economic bottleneck analysis

Identify processes where the cost of a bottleneck is disproportionate to the value of the overall system.

Ask:
- What slows the entire industry?
- What is the most expensive constraint?
- Which process is still performed manually?
- Where does a small failure cause a large financial loss?
- Which scarce resource limits growth?

Look for companies that can remove those constraints.

## Lens 2: Cost-curve discontinuities

Investigate where the cost of a technology is falling rapidly.

Examples:
- Compute.
- Sensors.
- Robotics.
- Batteries.
- Satellite imagery.
- Data storage.
- AI inference.
- Advanced manufacturing.
- Genetic sequencing.

Ask what becomes economically viable when the cost falls by 10x, 100x, or more.

Do not assume these reductions will happen. Establish evidence and realistic scenarios.

## Lens 3: Labour substitution and augmentation

Find industries facing:
- Labour shortages.
- Ageing workforces.
- Dangerous work.
- Expensive specialist labour.
- Repetitive operations.
- Shortages of technical expertise.

Identify where robotics, AI, automation, or new operating models could replace or augment existing workflows.

Calculate the potential economic value of doing so.

## Lens 4: Regulatory and policy-driven markets

Identify upcoming regulations, safety requirements, environmental mandates, reporting requirements, infrastructure standards, and government investment programmes that create new purchasing requirements.

Look at global markets, not just one country.

Distinguish mandatory compliance demand from voluntary demand.

## Lens 5: Infrastructure replacement cycles

Investigate ageing assets and infrastructure requiring modernization.

Consider:
- Electricity grids.
- Water distribution.
- Pipelines.
- Roads and bridges.
- Railways.
- Airports.
- Ports.
- Industrial facilities.
- Telecom infrastructure.
- Buildings.

Ask what new technologies could be installed during maintenance, upgrades, or replacement cycles.

## Lens 6: Data-to-action gaps

Look for industries that generate substantial data but struggle to convert it into operational decisions.

Identify:
- Data collection problems.
- Data integration gaps.
- Lack of actionable analytics.
- Missing real-time intelligence.
- Poor workflow integration.
- Lack of accountability for decisions.

A dashboard alone is not an opportunity. Find the operational or financial action that better information enables.

## Lens 7: Insurance, risk and liability economics

Investigate problems where a new technology could reduce:
- Insurance premiums.
- Downtime.
- Liability.
- Accidents.
- Asset failures.
- Environmental damage.
- Regulatory penalties.
- Unplanned maintenance.

Find out who captures the financial benefit and who can authorize purchasing.

## Lens 8: Supply-chain fragility

Identify industries dependent on:
- Geopolitically concentrated resources.
- Critical imported components.
- Single-source suppliers.
- Vulnerable transportation routes.
- Scarce materials.
- Specialized manufacturing capabilities.

Discover new opportunities in alternative materials, local production, component intelligence, traceability, and supply-chain resilience.

## Lens 9: Technology convergence

Search for opportunities at intersections.

Examples:
- AI + robotics.
- AI + biology.
- Satellite data + insurance.
- Quantum sensing + industrial inspection.
- Photonics + data centres.
- Digital twins + autonomous systems.
- Advanced materials + energy storage.
- Synthetic biology + manufacturing.
- Edge AI + remote infrastructure.

Do not merely name combinations. Explain what new capability emerges and why a customer would pay for it.

## Lens 10: Non-consumption

Investigate people and organizations that do not use existing solutions because they are:
- Too expensive.
- Too complex.
- Too difficult to access.
- Too specialized.
- Too slow.
- Designed for larger customers.

Can a new product create an entirely new customer segment?

- Deloitte.
- PwC.
- EY.
- KPMG.
- Accenture.
- Capgemini.
- Oliver Wyman.
- Roland Berger.
- Arthur D. Little.
- Gartner.
- IDC.

Also investigate:

- World Economic Forum.
- International Energy Agency.
- International Renewable Energy Agency.
- World Bank.
- OECD.
- United Nations agencies.
- National science and technology organizations.
- Government industrial policy reports.
- Patent databases.
- Scientific journals.
- University research centres.
- Industrial trade associations.
- Standards organizations.
- Venture capital reports.
- Startup ecosystem reports.
- Public company annual reports.
- Earnings calls and investor presentations.
- Procurement announcements and tenders.

Do not depend exclusively on consulting firms.

Consulting reports are often excellent for identifying macro trends, but may be insufficient for establishing customer demand, market economics, or technical feasibility.

Use primary sources wherever possible.

For every major market thesis, triangulate information across independent sources.

---

# 7. THE RESEARCH METHODOLOGY

Follow these stages sequentially.

Do not jump directly into idea generation.

## PHASE 1 — GLOBAL MACRO SCAN

Identify approximately 30–50 structural shifts affecting the global economy.

Classify them into:

- Technological.
- Economic.
- Demographic.
- Environmental.
- Geopolitical.
- Regulatory.
- Infrastructure-related.
- Social and behavioural.

For each shift, identify:
- What is changing?
- Why is it changing?
- Evidence that it is happening.
- Expected acceleration over the next 2–5 years.
- Industries affected.
- Second-order consequences.
- Potential economic winners and losers.

## PHASE 2 — INDUSTRY ATTRACTIVENESS MAPPING

Evaluate every major industry and its subsegments.

Identify:
- Market size.
- Historical growth.
- Expected growth.
- Customer spending.
- Capital expenditure.
- Existing pain points.
- Technology adoption.
- Regulatory drivers.
- Market fragmentation.
- Competitive intensity.
- Barriers to entry.
- Areas of underinvestment.

Do not manufacture precise market-size figures.

When data is unavailable, provide defensible estimates with explicit assumptions and confidence levels.

## PHASE 3 — MARKET WHITESPACE IDENTIFICATION

For every attractive industry, identify specific gaps.

Look for:
- Unserved customer segments.
- Poorly served workflows.
- Missing infrastructure.
- Expensive manual processes.
- Fragmented software.
- Inadequate hardware.
- Underdeveloped supply chains.
- Technology adoption barriers.
- New needs emerging from recent technological developments.

Distinguish between an established market with an existing gap and an entirely new market category.

## PHASE 4 — OPPORTUNITY GENERATION

Only after completing the previous stages, generate potential business opportunities.

Aim for 50–100 distinct opportunity hypotheses across the global economy.

This is a research target, not a requirement to fill a quota with weak ideas.

Every opportunity must be specific enough to explain:
- What is being built?
- For whom?
- Which problem does it solve?
- What is the existing alternative?
- Why is the alternative insufficient?
- Why now?
- How does it make money?

Categorize every opportunity as:

A. Hardware-first.
B. Software-first.
C. Hardware + software integrated.
D. Deep-tech enabling infrastructure.
E. New market category or emerging ecosystem.

## PHASE 5 — OPPORTUNITY VALIDATION

Challenge every idea.

Look for:
- Evidence of actual customer pain.
- Existing budgets.
- Purchasing authority.
- Competitors.
- Substitute solutions.
- Customer acquisition difficulty.
- Deployment friction.
- Regulatory constraints.
- Technical feasibility.
- Unit economics.
- Gross margin potential.
- Capital intensity.
- Sales cycle length.
- Time to first revenue.

An interesting technology with no identifiable buyer should not rank highly.

## PHASE 6 — FRONTIER OPPORTUNITY IDENTIFICATION

Separately identify approximately 15–25 frontier opportunities.

These should be based on significant technological, scientific, or economic shifts.

For each:
- Explain the breakthrough.
- Identify the commercialization window.
- Identify the enabling infrastructure.
- Identify the first customers.
- Identify what can be sold within 2–5 years.
- Identify what may become a major market in 5–15 years.

Do not confuse speculative scientific research with an investable near-term opportunity.

## PHASE 7 — CONTRARIAN OPPORTUNITY SEARCH

Conduct a dedicated search for opportunities that do not receive disproportionate attention from mainstream venture capital.

Explore:
- Industrial maintenance.
- Testing and measurement.
- Calibration.
- Inspection.
- Specialized components.
- Industrial consumables.
- Waste processing.
- Water systems.
- Manufacturing tools.
- Energy efficiency.
- Ageing infrastructure.
- Industrial safety.
- Specialized B2B workflows.
- Technical services that can become scalable products.

Identify at least 10 credible contrarian opportunities if evidence supports them.

## PHASE 8 — FINAL INVESTMENT COMMITTEE REVIEW

Subject the strongest candidates to a critical investment committee review.

Ask:
- Is this genuinely a large market?
- Is the market expanding?
- Is the opportunity structurally defensible?
- Is there a clear initial customer?
- Can a small team build the first product?
- Can the business reach revenue without massive funding?
- Could the product expand into a much larger platform?
- Why might this fail?
- What evidence would invalidate the thesis?

Only opportunities that survive serious criticism should enter the final shortlist.

---

# 8. REQUIRED OPPORTUNITY SCORING MODEL

Create a weighted scoring system out of 100.

Use the following weights:

| Evaluation criterion | Weight |
|---|---:|
| Market size and expansion potential | 15 |
| Strength of customer pain | 15 |
| Willingness to pay and budget availability | 15 |
| Timing and technological readiness | 10 |
| Market growth and structural tailwinds | 10 |
| Competitive whitespace | 10 |
| Commercial feasibility within 2–5 years | 10 |
| Defensibility and potential moat | 5 |
| Capital efficiency and initial entry feasibility | 5 |
| Expansion potential into adjacent markets | 5 |
| TOTAL | 100 |

Give each opportunity a score with a detailed justification.

Also provide:
- Evidence confidence: High / Medium / Low.
- Technical readiness: Research / Prototype / Pilot / Commercial.
- Market maturity: Emerging / Early growth / Established / Transforming.
- Capital intensity: Low / Medium / High / Very High.
- Sales cycle: Short / Medium / Long / Very Long.
- Time to first commercial customer.
- Estimated time to product-market validation.

Do not allow a high market-size score to compensate for the absence of customer demand.

---

# 9. DEEP-DIVE FORMAT FOR EVERY SHORTLISTED OPPORTUNITY

For the best 20 opportunities, create detailed opportunity dossiers.

Use this exact structure.

## 1. Opportunity name

A concise, descriptive name.

## 2. Industry classification

- Primary industry.
- Subindustry.
- Hardware / Software / Hybrid.
- Deep-tech involvement, if applicable.

## 3. Executive thesis

Explain the opportunity in 3–5 sentences.

## 4. The underlying structural shift

What is changing in the world to create this opportunity?

## 5. The actual problem

Describe the problem from the perspective of the customer.

Avoid generic statements such as "the industry needs digital transformation."

Explain the actual workflow, cost, inefficiency, or failure.

## 6. Existing solutions

Who solves this today?

What products and services are available?

Why do customers continue to experience the problem?

## 7. Market opportunity

Provide:
- TAM.
- SAM.
- SOM.

Explain the methodology.

Use bottom-up calculations wherever possible.

For example:

Number of potential customers × realistic annual spending per customer = serviceable market.

Do not invent market sizes or blindly repeat inflated market research estimates.

## 8. The proposed product

Describe a specific product or system.

Include:
- Core functionality.
- Hardware components, where applicable.
- Software architecture, where applicable.
- AI or scientific components, where applicable.
- Integration requirements.
- Key technical challenges.

## 9. First customer segment

Identify:
- The initial buyer.
- The end user.
- The budget owner.
- The economic decision-maker.
- The most attractive early-adopter segment.

## 10. Business model

Evaluate possible models:

- Hardware sales.
- SaaS subscriptions.
- Hardware-as-a-Service.
- Usage-based pricing.
- Licensing.
- Data subscriptions.
- Transaction fees.
- Performance-based contracts.
- Enterprise contracts.
- Managed services.

Explain which model is most realistic and why.

## 11. Competition and alternatives

Identify real companies.

Classify them as:
- Direct competitors.
- Indirect competitors.
- Substitutes.
- Potential partners.
- Adjacent market participants.

Explain how a new company could differentiate itself.

## 12. Market timing

Why is this opportunity emerging now?

What has changed over the past few years?

What is expected to change over the next 2–5 years?

## 13. Technical feasibility

Assess whether the product can realistically be developed today.

Identify:
- Required technologies.
- Existing components.
- Scientific uncertainties.
- Technical dependencies.
- Prototype requirements.
- Regulatory constraints.
- Manufacturing challenges.

## 14. Commercial entry strategy

Explain how a startup could enter this market.

Identify the smallest initial product that could generate revenue.

Do not recommend building an entire ecosystem on day one.

## 15. Expansion pathway

Explain how a small initial product could evolve into a larger company.

Identify adjacent products, customers, markets, and platform opportunities.

## 16. Risks and failure modes

Explain why the opportunity might fail.

Be brutally honest.

## 17. Opportunity score

Give the weighted score out of 100, with evidence confidence.

## 18. Sources

Provide the primary and independent sources supporting the thesis.

---

# 10. THE BILLION-DOLLAR COMPANY PATTERN ANALYSIS

Go beyond individual product ideas.

Identify markets where an initial product could become the foundation for an entire category-defining company.

Look for the following patterns:

### Pattern A: Pick-and-shovel businesses

Companies selling essential infrastructure, tools, equipment, components, or services to an expanding industry.

### Pattern B: Infrastructure platforms

Companies becoming essential infrastructure for an entire industry.

### Pattern C: Intelligence layers

Companies converting physical-world data into valuable decisions and operational actions.

### Pattern D: Operating systems for industries

Software platforms that become the central operational layer for a particular industry.

### Pattern E: Autonomous systems

Companies replacing fragmented human workflows with integrated autonomous systems.

### Pattern F: New bottleneck businesses

Companies solving problems that emerge because another technology or industry is growing rapidly.

### Pattern G: Market creators

Companies making a previously uneconomical product or service accessible to a new customer base.

### Pattern H: Specialized technology monopolies

Companies developing difficult-to-replicate capabilities in narrow but economically valuable markets.

For each shortlisted opportunity, identify which pattern it represents.

Also explain whether the company has the potential to build:
- A defensible technology moat.
- Proprietary data advantages.
- Network effects.
- High switching costs.
- Manufacturing advantages.
- Regulatory or certification barriers.
- Distribution advantages.
- Ecosystem lock-in.

Do not claim a moat simply because a company uses AI.

---

# 11. THE FUTURE-NEED REVERSE ENGINEERING EXERCISE

This section requires particularly ambitious thinking.

Imagine the world 5–15 years from now.

Consider scenarios such as:

- AI becomes extremely inexpensive and widely available.
- Humanoid robots become commercially useful.
- Data-centre electricity demand grows dramatically.
- Autonomous factories become economically viable.
- Medical diagnostics become increasingly continuous.
- Satellite imagery becomes cheaper and more frequent.
- Electricity grids become increasingly distributed.
- Climate volatility increases operational risk.
- Critical infrastructure becomes more digitally interconnected.
- Labour shortages intensify.
- Synthetic biology enables new manufacturing processes.
- Quantum sensing reaches commercial maturity.
- Global supply chains undergo further restructuring.

For each scenario:

1. Describe the future state.
2. Identify the infrastructure required to support it.
3. Identify what will be missing.
4. Identify the new bottlenecks.
5. Identify the new customer requirements.
6. Identify which existing companies will benefit.
7. Identify which new types of companies will become necessary.
8. Identify what a startup can begin building today.

**The central question: What will the world desperately need in five years that very few companies are building properly today?**

Do not present speculative predictions as established facts. Use scenarios and clearly label assumptions.

---

# 12. GLOBAL MARKET AND REGIONAL ANALYSIS

The research must be global, but investigate differences between regions.

Include:

- United States.
- Europe.
- United Kingdom.
- China.
- India.
- Japan.
- South Korea.
- Singapore.
- Taiwan.
- Middle East.
- Southeast Asia.
- Australia.
- Latin America.

For each major opportunity, identify:

- Where demand is strongest.
- Where technology development is strongest.
- Where production and manufacturing are concentrated.
- Where customer spending is highest.
- Where regulations are creating demand.
- Where the competitive landscape is weakest.
- Where a new startup could enter most easily.

Do not assume that the largest market is necessarily the best entry market.

Identify global opportunities that can be built from an emerging economy and sold internationally.

---

# 13. RESEARCH QUALITY AND ANTI-HALLUCINATION RULES

These rules are mandatory.

1. Use current web research and provide source links.
2. Prioritize reports and data published in 2024–2026 when discussing current conditions, while using older sources for historical context.
3. Prefer primary research, official statistics, regulatory documents, company filings, technical papers, and credible industry data.
4. Use consulting reports for strategic context, not as unquestionable truth.
5. Triangulate major market claims with independent sources.
6. Separate verified facts, estimates, interpretations, and hypotheses.
7. Never fabricate company names, products, customer examples, partnerships, funding rounds, or market statistics.
8. Never present a projected market size as an established current market size.
9. Include publication dates for important sources.
10. Identify conflicting evidence rather than hiding it.
11. If a market-size estimate cannot be independently verified, explain the limitation.
12. Do not inflate TAM using unrealistic assumptions.
13. Do not rank a market highly simply because it is receiving media attention.
14. Do not assume that AI is necessary for every business opportunity.
15. Do not confuse scientific novelty with commercial feasibility.
16. Do not recommend a business simply because the underlying technology is interesting.
17. Identify what evidence would disprove each important opportunity thesis.
18. Use explicit confidence levels for uncertain conclusions.

Every major opportunity should have a traceable evidence trail.

---

# 14. FINAL DELIVERABLES

Organize the final research report into the following deliverables.

## DELIVERABLE 1 — GLOBAL ECONOMIC AND TECHNOLOGICAL SHIFT MAP

A detailed overview of the 30–50 most important shifts likely to shape global markets.

## DELIVERABLE 2 — INDUSTRY OPPORTUNITY ATLAS

A comprehensive industry-by-industry map.

For each industry:
- Important subindustries.
- Hardware opportunities.
- Software opportunities.
- Hybrid opportunities.
- Structural growth drivers.
- Emerging customer problems.
- Commercial opportunities.

## DELIVERABLE 3 — FRONTIER TECHNOLOGY RADAR

The most interesting frontier technologies and their commercialization timelines.

Separate:
- Commercially actionable today.
- Commercialization likely within 2–5 years.
- Longer-term possibilities within 5–15 years.

## DELIVERABLE 4 — 50–100 OPPORTUNITY HYPOTHESES

A structured master table containing:

| Field | Description |
|---|---|
| Opportunity | Specific business concept |
| Industry | Main sector |
| Subindustry | Specific market |
| Type | Hardware / Software / Hybrid |
| Trigger | Why now |
| Problem | What needs solving |
| Buyer | Who pays |
| Market | Estimated addressable market |
| Timing | Commercial readiness |
| Score | Weighted score |
| Confidence | Evidence quality |

Quality is more important than reaching the target number.

## DELIVERABLE 5 — TOP 20 OPPORTUNITY DOSSIERS

Deep analysis using the 18-part dossier structure.

## DELIVERABLE 6 — TOP 10 CONTRARIAN OPPORTUNITIES

Underrated, less obvious markets with strong commercial fundamentals.

## DELIVERABLE 7 — TOP 10 FRONTIER OPPORTUNITIES

Potentially transformative markets emerging from scientific and technological developments.

## DELIVERABLE 8 — THE NEXT-GENERATION BILLION-DOLLAR MARKETS

Identify the 10 most promising market categories that could produce exceptionally valuable companies over the next 5–10 years.

Explain:
- Why these markets will grow.
- What creates demand.
- What infrastructure is missing.
- Who the customers are.
- What new companies could own these markets.

## DELIVERABLE 9 — FOUNDER OPPORTUNITY SHORTLIST

Select the five strongest opportunities overall.

For each, provide:
- Why it ranks highly.
- The initial product.
- The first customer.
- Initial capital requirements and assumptions.
- Prototype or MVP scope.
- First revenue strategy.
- Defensibility.
- Three-year expansion roadmap.
- Major risks.
- Evidence that should be gathered before committing.

## DELIVERABLE 10 — OPPORTUNITIES TO WATCH

Identify promising technologies or markets that are not commercially ready yet but deserve continuous observation.

Explain which measurable developments would indicate that they are becoming investable.

# 15. FINAL INSTRUCTIONS: THINK LIKE A MARKET DISCOVERER, NOT AN IDEA GENERATOR

I want depth, originality, and intellectual honesty.

Do not optimize for the number of ideas.

Optimize for the discovery of opportunities with substantial economic value.

Avoid generic ideas such as:
- Another AI chatbot.
- Another generic SaaS platform.
- Another productivity app.
- Another marketplace without a clear supply or demand advantage.
- Another AI wrapper without defensibility.
- Another drone company without a specific operational advantage.

If you identify a crowded market, investigate whether there is a specialized underserved segment or an enabling infrastructure opportunity.

If you identify an emerging technology, investigate the less obvious industries that could benefit from it.

If you identify a major industry transformation, investigate the businesses required to make that transformation possible.

If a market is large but inaccessible to a small startup, identify a narrow entry point or eliminate it.

Think across disciplines.

Connect physics to manufacturing, biology to materials, AI to infrastructure, robotics to labour economics, energy to computation, and sensing to industrial decision-making.

Explore both the obvious opportunities and the opportunities hidden behind the obvious ones.

**Do not merely predict the future. Identify the businesses that the future will require.**

The ultimate objective is to produce a rigorous global opportunity intelligence report that could help a serious founder identify the next major market to enter, build a commercially viable initial product, and eventually create a company with the potential to become a category leader.

Start with the research methodology, then execute the investigation systematically.

Do not skip stages. Do not prematurely narrow the scope. Do not stop at surface-level trends.

When the report becomes too large for one response, divide it into clearly numbered research volumes and continue systematically without sacrificing analytical depth.

**Global Opportunity Intelligence Report 2026: Where Grid, Cooling and Infrastructure Bottlenecks Create the Next Startups**

# Global Opportunity Intelligence Report — November 2026
## Where the next category-defining companies will be built (2–5 year entry, 5–15 year foresight)

The most important finding is that the biggest near-term venture opportunity is not AI models. It is the physical bottlenecks that AI and electrification have exposed: grid capacity, long-lead electrical equipment, thermal management, and ageing infrastructure. The best entry points are capital-light "intelligence + service" businesses that help scarce existing assets connect faster, carry more and last longer. Our two top-ranked opportunities are large-load grid-capacity intelligence (84/100) and transformer health and life-extension (83/100). Both rest on independently measured scarcity: US power transformer lead times averaged 128 weeks in Wood Mackenzie's Q2 2025 survey, and the median US project built in 2025 took more than 5 years from interconnection request to commercial operation (Berkeley Lab, Queued Up 2026).

## TL;DR

- **The AI economy is constrained by physical infrastructure, not software.** The IEA's *Energy and AI* report (Apr 2025) projects data-centre electricity use will more than double, from around 415 TWh (about 1.5% of global electricity in 2024) to around 945 TWh (just under 3%) by 2030. Wood Mackenzie expected a 30% supply deficit for US power transformers in 2025, and interconnection takes 5+ years. The strongest founder bets help scarce grid assets connect faster, carry more and last longer: capacity intelligence, transformer health, a certified secondary equipment market, and liquid-cooling integrity.
- **Fixed regulatory deadlines create budgets; slipping ones create risk.** Fixed demand comes from EU battery passports (18 Feb 2027), CNSA 2.0 acquisition rules (2027) and NIST's deprecation of RSA/ECC after 2030. Other rules have slipped: the US PHMSA leak-detection rule (withdrawn, still "TBD"), the EU methane importer MRV (the Commission proposed a delay to 2029 on 14 Oct 2026) and US PFAS limits (partly rescinded, deadline moving toward 2031). Treat those as upside, not the core thesis.
- **"Boring" contrarian markets offer better risk-adjusted returns than headline frontier technology.** Liemberger & Wyatt (*Water Supply*, 2019) estimate non-revenue water at 126 bn m³/yr, worth US$39bn/yr even at a conservative US$0.31/m³. The NACE IMPACT study (CORROSION 2016) puts the cost of corrosion at US$2.5T, or 3.4% of 2013 global GDP, with US$375–875bn/yr savable. Inspection-to-maintenance workflows and controlled-mineral compliance are similar. Humanoids, quantum navigation and fusion are real but mostly early. What is investable today is the infrastructure that supports them, not the headline device.

---

# VOLUME I — METHOD AND MACRO SCAN

## Methodology
We worked market-first:
1. **Macro scan.** Verified each shift against 2024–2026 sources: IEA, Berkeley Lab (LBNL), Wood Mackenzie, BNEF, Stanford AI Index, NIST, European Commission, EPA, PHMSA, WHO, ASCE, Deloitte/Manufacturing Institute and IATA.
2. **Systems tracing.** Traced each shift to the new bottlenecks it creates.
3. **Whitespace.** Separated gaps in existing markets from genuinely new categories.
4. **Hypotheses.** Generated 50 opportunity hypotheses.
5. **Validation.** Challenged each on buyer, budget, competition, regulatory dependency and capital intensity.
6. **Scoring.** Applied the 100-point model.

Opportunities resting on soft or reversible regulation were penalised. Market size never compensated for missing demand. Labels: *Verified*, *Estimate* (our bottom-up with assumptions), *Hypothesis*. Third-party market-size figures are flagged where they conflict. For example, 2026 data-centre liquid-cooling market estimates range from US$4.07bn (MarketsandMarkets) to ~US$6bn+ (others), so we do not rely on them.

| 23 | Humanoid revenue tiny | Economic | Agility/Churchill Capital Corp XI S-4 (Sept 2026): 2025 net sales US$1.782M (up from ~US$310k in 2024) against a US$140.2M operating loss | — | Valuations ahead of revenue |
| 24 | Line ratings reform | Reg/Tech | FERC Order 881 AAR (Jul 2025); DLR rule pending mid-2026; DLR ~$100–200k/line | Gradual | GETs analytics |
| 25 | Electrification at distribution | Energy | IEA: data centres only ~1/10 of global demand growth to 2030, less than motors, AC, EVs | Steady | Distribution transformer stress |
| 26 | Export-control weaponisation | Geopolitical | China dual-use bans on 14 EU firms (Jul 2026, reported) | Rising | Second sourcing |
| 27 | Regional battery price gaps | Economic | BNEF: China $84/kWh; NA/EU 44%/56% higher | Persistent | EM import arbitrage |
| 28 | Agents raise token use | Tech | Gartner (secondary): 5–30× tokens per task | Rising | Inference demand offsets deflation |
| 29 | Quantum navigation trials | Frontier | Q-CTRL sea demo (reported 2026); aviation certification mid-2030s | Slow | Hybrid INS first |
| 30 | SMRs | Energy | IEA: first SMRs ~2030 | Slow | Licensing/supply-chain QA |
| 31 | Infrastructure funding cliff | Fiscal | ENR: progress "hinges on post-2026 funds" | Risk | Favour savings-generating products |
| 32 | Data-centre siting spreads | Geographic | IEA: US + China ≈ 80% of growth; UK 140 schemes could need 50 GW | Spreading | Non-US capacity tools |

**Interpretation.** Shifts 1–9 form one causal chain:
- Cheaper intelligence (8) raises compute demand.
- Compute demand raises power demand (1–2).
- Power demand collides with slow interconnection (3) and scarce equipment (5–6).
- Cheap batteries (9) are the main pressure valve.

Every link in that chain is a bottleneck business.

## Deliverable 3 — Frontier Technology Radar

**(a) Actionable today**
- **LFP storage.** Turnkey BESS costs $117/kWh (BNEF).
- **Direct-to-chip liquid cooling.** The opportunity is integrity monitoring.
- **Line ratings.** DLR and ambient-adjusted rating (AAR) software.
- **PQC migration tooling.** FIPS 203/204/205 were finalised in Aug 2024.
- **Vertical AI agents** in low-margin industrial back offices.

**(b) Likely in 2–5 years**
- **Humanoids in narrow logistics and automotive tasks.** Volumes are in the thousands, not millions.
- **Grid-flexible data centres.**
- **Solid-state transformers for DC-native power trains.** Hypothesis-strength.
- **Hybrid inertial navigation (INS) with quantum-enhanced components** for maritime and defence.
- **Sodium-ion stationary storage.** Not verified here.

**(c) 5–15 years**
- **SMRs at scale.**
- **Fusion, mass-market BCIs, in-space manufacturing, photonic/neuromorphic compute at scale.** None yet has a repeat-buying non-government customer.

## Deliverable 5 — Top 20 Dossiers

Score key: Market/15, Pain/15, WTP/15, Timing/10, Growth/10, Whitespace/10, Feasibility/10, Moat/5, Capital efficiency/5, Expansion/5.

### 1. Large-load grid-capacity & interconnection intelligence — 84/100
1. **Name:** "Where's the power?" time-to-power intelligence.
2. **Classification:** Energy / transmission & interconnection; software; power-flow modelling.
3. **Thesis:** Power availability is now the first siting question for data centres, fabs and electrified industry. Queue data is public but fragmented, and hosting capacity is opaque. Bankable time-to-power estimates sell into multi-billion-dollar siting decisions.
4. **Shift:** Data-centre demand doubles by 2030 (IEA). The US queue holds 1,312 GW of generation and 749 GW of storage, with a median of >5 years to build and 13% completion of 2000–2020 requests (LBNL 2026).
5. **Problem:** Developers option land, then learn 12–24 months later from a utility study that capacity is years away. Idle capital burns every month.
6. **Existing solutions:** Utility pre-application meetings, consultants, and queue/site tools (Grid Status, Interconnection.fyi, Paces, LandGate, Enverus). Most show queues, not load-side time-to-power that includes equipment lead times.
7. **Market (Estimate):** 2,000–4,000 serious developers, utilities and investors × US$100–400k/yr ≈ US$0.4–1.6bn. Confidence medium.
8. **Product:** Ingest queues, ISO filings, substation maps and lead-time indices. Run a DC power-flow screen. Output probabilistic time-to-power with confidence intervals and flexible-connection scenarios.
9. **First customer:** Site-selection teams at data-centre developers and neoclouds. The budget owner is the head of development.
10. **Business model:** Annual SaaS plus per-site studies; later, success fees.
11. **Competition:** The tools above (direct), engineering consultancies (indirect), utilities (partners).
12. **Timing:** Queued gas rose 86% in 2025, and large-load tariffs are emerging.
13. **Feasibility:** Public data plus open-source power flow. Data quality outside ISOs is the main risk.
14. **Entry:** Paid PJM/ERCOT pilot with 5 developers.
15. **Expansion:** UK/EU (UK: 140 schemes, ~50 GW), flexible-load contracting, capacity marketplace.
16. **Risks:** Utilities publish their own maps; forecast misses erode trust; data vendors bundle the feature.
17. **Score:** 11/14/13/9/10/6/9/3/5/4. Confidence High. Commercial, early growth, low capital, medium sales cycle. First customer in 3–6 months; product-market fit in 12–18 months.
18. **Sources:** IEA *Energy and AI* (Apr 2025); LBNL *Queued Up 2026* (Jun 2026); Wood Mackenzie (Sep 2025).

**Pattern:** intelligence layer → platform. **Moat:** a track record of forecast accuracy. **What would disprove it:** utilities' own maps prove accurate, or developers won't pay >$100k/yr.

### 2. Transformer health & life-extension — 83/100
1. **Name:** Transformer life-extension-as-a-service.
2. **Classification:** Energy / substations; hybrid.
3. **Thesis:** With replacement units ~2.5 years away, every existing transformer is a strategic asset. Sensors plus service that predict failure, extend life and guarantee availability capture part of the avoided outage cost.
4. **Shift:** Wood Mackenzie's Q2 2025 survey put lead times at 128 weeks for power transformers and 144 weeks for GSUs. It expected a 30% power-transformer supply deficit in 2025, and its analyst Ben Boucher estimated imports at ~80% of 2025 US power-transformer supply. EU+NA demand of 914 GVA against 566 GVA supply (McKinsey).
5. **Problem:** Oil is sampled annually or less. Mid-size industrial and data-centre units are often unmonitored. One failure can stall a plant for years.
6. **Existing solutions:** OEMs (Hitachi Energy, Siemens Energy, GE Vernova) and instrumentation firms (Qualitrol, Doble, Camlin) focus on large transmission units. The gap is the long tail plus outcome-based service.
7. **Market (Estimate):** ~500k medium/large units (assumption, low confidence) × 20% × US$3–8k/yr ≈ US$0.3–0.8bn recurring, plus refurbishment.
8. **Product:** Retrofit DGA, thermal and partial-discharge sensing; cellular gateway; health index and remaining-life model; oil-regeneration partner dispatch.
9. **First customer:** Critical-facilities leads at data centres and industrial plants.
10. **Business model:** Hardware-as-a-service, plus availability-guarantee tiers backed by insurance.
11. **Competition:** OEMs (direct at utilities), test service firms (indirect), insurers and regen contractors (partners). Differentiation: vendor-neutral coverage of mixed fleets.
12. **Timing:** Deficits persist toward 2030.
13. **Feasibility:** Mature sensing. Validating the life model is the hard part.
14. **Entry:** 50-unit pilot across 3 campuses at ~US$5k/unit/yr.
15. **Expansion:** Switchgear, GSUs, underwriting data, secondary-market certification (Dossier 6).
16. **Risks:** OEM bundling; slow utility procurement; urgency fades after 2030.
17. **Score:** 12/14/13/9/9/6/9/3/4/4. Confidence High on pain, medium on sizing. Commercial, medium capital. First revenue in 6–9 months.
18. **Sources:** Wood Mackenzie (Aug/Sep 2025); McKinsey (2026); ASCE 2025.

**Pattern:** new-bottleneck business plus intelligence layer. **What would disprove it:** failure rates too low to justify monitoring spend.

### 3. Liquid-cooling integrity platform — 81/100
1. **Name:** Coolant integrity, leak detection and commissioning assurance.
2. **Classification:** AI infrastructure / thermal; hybrid.
3. **Thesis:** Racks run at 70–140 kW and are heading to ~600 kW. Fluid drift, micro-leaks and poor commissioning are now single points of failure next to millions of dollars of GPUs, and nobody owns them end to end.
4. **Shift:** JLL says >70 kW requires liquid cooling. Fairwater runs 140 kW per rack; Kyber targets ~600 kW.
5. **Problem:** Air-cooling-trained teams rely on manual coolant sampling. Leaks are found late. Warranty disputes span the OEM, CDU vendor and colo.
6. **Existing solutions:** CDU vendors (Vertiv, Schneider Electric, CoolIT, Motivair) offer basic telemetry; water-treatment firms sample periodically. Nobody provides a vendor-neutral, warranty-grade record.
7. **Market (Estimate):** 5,000–10,000 liquid-cooled halls by 2030 (assumption) × US$50–150k/yr ≈ US$0.25–1.5bn.
8. **Product:** Inline conductivity/pH/inhibitor/particulate sensors, cable leak detection, anomaly model, and a commissioning app producing a digital record.
9. **First customer:** VP of operations at neoclouds and colos retrofitting for GPUs.
10. **Business model:** Hardware plus SaaS per loop, plus commissioning services.
11. **Competition:** CDU OEMs (bundling risk), water-treatment majors (substitutes), DCIM vendors (partners).
12. **Timing:** Mainstream adoption in 2025–2026.
13. **Feasibility:** Sensors exist. Sensor fouling in glycol fluids is the hard part.
14. **Entry:** Commissioning audit plus a 90-day pilot.
15. **Expansion:** Immersion fluids, heat-reuse metering, insurance data.
16. **Risks:** OEM integration of equivalent features; hyperscaler in-house builds.
17. **Score:** 9/13/12/10/10/7/9/3/4/4. Confidence Medium-High. Low-medium capital.
18. **Sources:** JLL (Sep 2026); MarketsandMarkets (conflicting estimates); IEA.

**Pattern:** pick-and-shovel. **What would disprove it:** OEM warranties accept CDU-native telemetry and incidents prove rare.

### 4. Controlled-mineral BOM exposure & licence compliance — 78/100
1. **Name:** Export-control exposure mapping.
2. **Classification:** Supply chain / trade compliance; software.
3. **Thesis:** China's October 2025 rules would require licences for products with ≥0.1% China-origin rare-earth value. They are suspended only until 10 Nov 2026, and the earlier Ga/Ge and rare-earth controls remain active. Manufacturers cannot answer the exposure question at BOM depth.
4. **Shift:** Heavy rare-earth controls since Apr 2025. Gallium exports near zero in 2025, with prices up 365%. Dual-use bans on 14 EU firms reported in Jul 2026.
5. **Problem:** Questionnaires stop at tier 1, while magnets and Ga/Ge parts sit at tiers 3–5.
6. **Existing solutions:** Mapping vendors (Interos, Everstream, Sayari) map ownership, not material content.
7. **Market (Estimate):** ~5,000 exposed firms × US$100–200k ≈ US$0.5–1bn.
8. **Product:** BOM ingestion, component-to-material inference, supplier attestations, and a China/US/EU rules engine.
9. **First customer:** Trade-compliance heads at EU automotive and defence suppliers.
10. **Business model:** Enterprise SaaS.
11. **Competition:** Incumbent mapping vendors and Big Four advisory. Differentiation: material-content inference.
12. **Timing:** Suspension expiry is imminent.
13. **Feasibility:** No hardware; inference accuracy is the risk.
14. **Entry:** 6-week fixed-fee assessment.
15. **Expansion:** Substitution engine; second-source marketplace (#33).
16. **Risks:** Durable US–China deal; incumbents add the feature.
17. **Score:** 8/13/11/10/8/7/9/3/5/4. Confidence Medium. Low capital.
18. **Sources:** CFR (2026); Global Trade Alert; GvW (Nov 2025); UI.se (2025); CSIS (Apr 2025).

### 5. Grid-flexible data-centre orchestration + on-site storage — 77/100
1. **Name:** Flex-to-connect.
2. **Classification:** AI infrastructure / energy; software plus integrated BESS.
3. **Thesis:** At $70/kWh packs, data centres can buy faster grid access by committing to curtail or self-supply during the few peak hours that drive upgrades. Value comes from making that commitment verifiable to utilities.
4. **Shift:** 5+ year queues; the BNEF price collapse.
5. **Problem:** Utilities assume 100% firm load, and there is no verification standard for flexibility.
6. **Existing solutions:** BESS integrators, demand-response aggregators, early startups (e.g., Emerald AI), nascent tariffs.
7. **Market (Estimate):** 200–400 campuses/yr × US$1–3M ≈ US$0.2–1.2bn/yr.
8. **Product:** Telemetry, curtailment controller, workload-shift APIs, BESS dispatch, utility-grade reports.
9. **First customer:** Inference-campus developers, whose workloads are more flexible than training.
10. **Business model:** Licence plus shared savings.
11. **Competition:** Hyperscaler in-house teams; BESS OEM software (Fluence, Tesla).
12. **Timing:** Market rules forming 2026–2028.
13. **Feasibility:** Utility acceptance is the gating factor.
14. **Entry:** One utility-sanctioned pilot.
15. **Expansion:** Capacity markets; fabs and electrolysers.
16. **Risks:** Training loads resist curtailment; utilities don't credit flexibility.
17. **Score:** 12/12/12/8/10/6/7/3/3/4. Confidence Medium. Long sales cycle.
18. **Sources:** LBNL 2026; BNEF (Dec 2025); IEA 2025.

**Pattern:** market creator. **What would disprove it:** no accelerated flexible connection granted by ~2028.

### 6. Certified secondary market for long-lead electrical gear — 76/100
- **Thesis:** Scarcity makes idle and decommissioned transformers and switchgear valuable, but trust is missing. A marketplace that owns testing, refurbishment and warranty owns the supply side.
- **Evidence:** 2+ year lead times; switchgear at 44 weeks; prices rising (WoodMac).
- **Problem:** A developer needing 50 MVA in 6 months has no trusted source.
- **Existing:** Fragmented brokers.
- **Market (Estimate):** A 2–5% secondary share implies US$1–3bn GMV, at 10–20% take plus refurbishment margins.
- **Customer:** Schedule-critical renewable and data-centre developers.
- **Model:** Fees plus refurbishment plus warranty.
- **Entry:** Distribution and pad-mount units first.
- **Expansion:** Larger units, rental fleets, mobile substations.
- **Risks:** Demand cliff if supply normalises; failure liability.
- **Score:** 10/13/12/9/8/7/8/3/3/3. Confidence Medium.
- **Sources:** Wood Mackenzie; McKinsey; POWER (2026).
- **Pattern:** market creator.

### 7. Non-revenue water performance-based platform — 76/100
- **Thesis:** Liemberger & Wyatt (*Water Supply* 19(3), 2019) estimate losses at 346 million m³/day, or 126 bn m³/yr, worth US$39bn/yr even at a conservative US$0.31/m³. A World Bank/PPIAF/IWA review of 43 projects found performance-based contracts (PBCs) 68% more effective than utility-only efforts. Cheap acoustic and pressure sensing plus AI localisation lets a startup take outcome risk at mid-size utilities that large contractors ignore.
- **Problem:** Utilities in South Asia, Africa and LatAm lack capex and crews.
- **Existing:** Veolia, Suez, Xylem, Gutermann, TaKaDu.
- **Market (Estimate):** Recovering 10% of losses ≈ US$3.9bn/yr of value; a vendor share of ~US$1bn.
- **Product:** District metered areas, loggers, localisation model, crew app, lender-grade measurement and verification.
- **Customers:** DFI-financed utilities in India, Indonesia, Philippines, Kenya, Brazil, Mexico.
- **Model:** Base fee plus share of savings.
- **Entry:** One district pilot co-funded by a DFI.
- **Risks:** Collections, political turnover, slow tenders.
- **Score:** 11/13/10/8/7/7/8/4/4/4. Confidence Medium-High.
- **Sources:** Liemberger & Wyatt (2019); World Bank PPP blog.
- **Global build:** can be built from India or Southeast Asia and exported.

### 8. Inspection-to-work-order AI for industrial integrity — 76/100
- **Thesis:** Plants produce thousands of NDT, thickness and CP reports in PDFs. With inference >280× cheaper (Stanford), extracting them into risk-ranked work orders in SAP PM/Maximo is now economic. The value is in closure, not a dashboard.
- **Existing:** AVEVA, Cenosco, Hexagon APM, which assume clean data.
- **Market (Estimate):** ~10,000 high-hazard sites × US$50–150k ≈ US$0.5–1.5bn.
- **Customer:** Mid-size refineries, chemical plants, midstream operators.
- **Model:** SaaS per site.
- **Entry:** Backlog clean-up project converting to SaaS.
- **Expansion:** Utilities, water, aviation MRO.
- **Risks:** Incumbents add LLM features.
- **Score:** 10/12/11/9/7/6/9/3/5/4. Confidence Medium.
- **Sources:** Stanford AI Index 2025; NACE IMPACT.

### 9. GNSS interference monitoring & assured timing — 76/100
- **Thesis:** Spoofing is silent and spreading. Telecom cores, grid PMUs, exchanges and ports depend on GNSS time and position and need detection and holdover now; quantum navigation is the long game.
- **Evidence:** IATA spoofing +193%; >18,300 vessels jammed H2 2025; >122,000 flights affected Jan–Apr 2025; ICAO/ITU/IMO joint warning (Mar 2025).
- **Existing:** Safran, Microchip, Septentrio; no vendor-neutral networked detection service.
- **Market (Estimate):** ~50,000 critical sites × US$5–20k ≈ US$0.25–1bn.
- **Product:** Low-cost multi-constellation monitors (COTS detection is shown in arXiv 2509.13600), cloud correlation, integration with holdover clocks.
- **First customers:** Telcos and ports in the Baltic, Gulf and Eastern Mediterranean.
- **Risks:** Government-provided free monitoring; de-escalation.
- **Score:** 9/13/11/9/9/6/8/4/3/4. Confidence Medium-High.

### 10. PQC migration for OT and embedded devices — 76/100
- **Thesis:** NIST deprecates quantum-vulnerable algorithms after 2030 and disallows them after 2035. CNSA 2.0 requires PQC for new national-security acquisitions from 2027 and exclusive PQC firmware signing by 2030. Meters, relays, PLCs and vehicles shipped today will still be in service in 2035, so their firmware signing must change now. IT-focused vendors underserve OT.
- **Existing:** HSM vendors (Thales, Entrust, Utimaco), PQC IP firms (PQShield), IT inventory tools.
- **Market (Estimate):** ~3,000 OEMs × US$150–400k ≈ US$0.5–1.2bn.
- **Product:** Firmware/SBOM crypto scanner, hybrid PQC signing service, constrained-MCU libraries, compliance evidence.
- **Customer:** Device OEMs selling to US federal and EU critical infrastructure.
- **Risks:** OEMs defer; HSM vendors bundle.
- **Score:** 11/10/11/9/9/5/9/3/5/4. Confidence High.
- **Sources:** NIST IR 8547 (draft Nov 2024; a final dated 27 Oct 2026 is reported secondarily); NSA CNSA 2.0; EU NIS roadmap (high-risk by end-2030).

### 11–20. Condensed dossiers
- **11. Remote CP monitoring + RBI (74).** The NACE IMPACT study (released at CORROSION 2016) estimates corrosion costs US$2.5T/yr (3.4% of 2013 global GDP), with 15–35% (US$375–875bn/yr) savable using existing practices. CP surveys are still walked manually. Cellular rectifier monitors plus corrosion models, sold as hardware-as-a-service to gas LDCs and water utilities. The PHMSA rule is stalled ("TBD" in the May 2026 PIPES chart), so demand is safety- and cost-led. Risk: commoditised hardware. The NACE figure is a 2016 extrapolation from older national studies. Score 12/12/11/7/6/6/9/3/4/4.
- **12. Technician copilot (74).** Deloitte and The Manufacturing Institute's *Taking charge* study (Apr 2024) found that "more than 5 in 10 or 1.9 million" of up to 3.8M needed US manufacturing jobs could go unfilled through 2033. The niche is electrical and liquid-cooling trades for data centres, which pairs with Dossier 3. The space is crowded (Augmentir, Tulip, Parsable). Score 11/12/10/9/8/4/9/2/5/4.
- **13. EM C&I storage-as-a-service (73).** At $70/kWh stationary packs and $84/kWh China averages (BNEF), solar plus storage beats diesel backup for factories and cold chains. Financing and O&M are the bottleneck. Risks: currency and capital intensity. Score 12/11/10/9/10/6/7/2/2/4.
- **14. Battery SoH certification (71).** The passport is mandatory from 18 Feb 2027. Sell trusted health certificates to lessors, insurers and lenders; the passport itself is crowded (Circularise; TWAICE, ACCURE in analytics). Risk: OEMs restricting BMS data. Score 10/10/10/9/9/4/8/3/4/4.
- **15. Physical-AI deployment/safety tooling (71).** Figure has delivered >350 Figure 03 units (by Apr 2026). Agility's S-4 (Sept 2026) shows 2025 net sales of just US$1.782M against a US$140.2M operating loss. Pilots stall at safety cases and uptime proof. Sell KPI, validation and risk-assessment tooling rather than betting on a robot winner. Score 9/10/9/8/9/6/8/3/4/5.
- **16. Methane MRV-as-a-service (67).** The EU requires OGMP 2.0 Level 5 equivalence. An IOGP-commissioned Wood Mackenzie study claims ~94% of global gas would be non-compliant in 2027 (industry-funded, so treat with caution). The 14 Oct 2026 delay proposal lowers the timing score. Score 9/11/10/6/7/6/8/3/4/3.
- **17. DLR/GETs-as-a-service (66).** FERC Order 881 forced ambient ratings in Jul 2025; the DLR rule was still pending in mid-2026. Cost is ~US$100–200k per line, and a Pennsylvania pilot showed a +25% average capacity gain. Crowded (LineVision, Heimdall Power, Ampacimon), so enter via mid-size utilities and non-US TSOs, bundled with Dossier 1. Score 9/11/9/8/8/4/7/3/3/4.
- **18. Real-time sewer control (66).** ASCE grades stormwater D. Consent decrees create budgets. Level sensors plus predictive control of existing gates defer capex. Score 9/11/9/7/6/6/8/3/4/3.
- **19. PFAS concentrate destruction + monitoring (64).** The 4 ppt PFOA/PFOS limits are retained. The May 2026 proposals rescind four other PFAS limits and offer extensions to 2031. Demand is delayed, not dead. Capital-heavy. Score 9/11/10/6/7/5/6/4/2/4.
- **20. Ga/Ge recovery (64).** Gallium prices rose 365% amid near-zero Chinese exports (UI.se). Recovery from alumina/zinc residues and fab scrap, with offtake from defence and semiconductor buyers. Capital-heavy. Score 8/11/10/8/8/6/5/4/1/3.

## Billion-dollar pattern summary

| Dossier | Pattern | Strongest moat |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Intelligence layer → platform | Forecast-accuracy record |
| 2 | New bottleneck | Cross-vendor failure data, insurer ties |
| 3 | Pick-and-shovel | Fluid-failure data, OEM warranty acceptance |
| 4 | Intelligence layer | Material-inference models |
| 5 | Market creator | Utility-accepted verification |
| 6 | Market creator | Test lab + warranty |
| 7 | Industry operating system | DFI-accepted M&V |
| 9 | Infrastructure platform | Sensor-network density |
| 10 | Specialised technology | Certification evidence, embedded IP |

"Uses AI" is never claimed as a moat. Moats come from outcome data, certifications and networks.

## Regional view
- **US:** strongest for Dossiers 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6. Federal environmental drivers are weak.
- **EU/UK:** mandate-driven (passport, methane, PQC roadmap); UK ~50 GW data-centre crunch. Best fit for Dossiers 4, 10 and 14.
- **China:** dominant supplier and competitor (batteries, Unitree); hard for foreign startups to enter.
- **India, Southeast Asia, Africa, LatAm:** best fit for NRW and C&I storage. Both can be built locally and exported to peer markets.
- **Gulf:** spoofing hotspots, LNG MRV, data-centre ambitions (Dossiers 9, 16).
- **Japan/Korea/Taiwan:** Ga/Ge buyers and PQC device OEMs.

- *MVP:* one-ISO time-to-power atlas plus site reports.

- *First revenue:* pilots at US$50–150k.

- *Moat:* utility-accepted protocol.
- *Roadmap:* capacity markets → industrial loads.
- *Risks:* utilities refuse to credit flexibility.
- *Evidence first:* one utility willing to sign an accelerated flexible connection.

## Caveats
- Market sizes are our bottom-up estimates and are less reliable than the verified bottleneck metrics (lead times, queues, prices). Validate them through customer discovery.
- Some datapoints come from secondary reporting: humanoid trackers, the final NIST IR 8547 date, the Gartner token multiplier, and Gulf ship-spoofing counts. Treat these as indicative.
- Market-research figures conflict (liquid cooling). The NACE corrosion figure extrapolates older national studies.
- US regulatory drivers weakened in 2025–2026 (PHMSA, PFAS), and some EU rules are being delayed (methane). Opportunities relying on them were down-weighted.
- The IOGP-commissioned methane study may overstate non-compliance.
- Company names are examples of market participants, not verified competitive analysis; each requires diligence.

## What the consulting firms agree on

Read together, these publications describe software moving from "assist" to "act":

- **Agents are the budget line.** Bain says up to half of technology spending could eventually go to agents running across the enterprise. McKinsey's 2026 outlook adds agentic software development and AI for scientific discovery and engineering as new trends.
- **Deployment is stalling at control, not capability.** Deloitte reports 38% of organizations piloting agents but only 11% in production. BCG finds 42% of companies expect to give agents real decision authority by 2030, yet only 5% have the controls to do so safely. Companies with strong controls generate 3X more value from agents.
- **Core systems aren't agent-ready.** Bain says organizations must make core business capabilities easy for agents to find and use in real time.
- **Regulation is deferred, not removed.** The EU AI Act's high-risk duties moved to 2 December 2027 for Annex III systems and 2 August 2028 for embedded systems. Transparency and general-purpose AI rules were left on the original schedule.
- **Trust and provenance are becoming purchases.** Gartner forecasts more than 50% of enterprises using AI security platforms by 2028. It also predicts sanction risks running into billions for firms that fail to invest in digital provenance by 2029.

I read these through search summaries and trade coverage, not the full PDFs. Gartner's figures are forecasts, not measurements.

## Where I think the gaps are

1. **Authority and accountability.** Agents can act, but nobody can verify what they were allowed to do or prove what they did.
2. **Verification.** Every outcome-based contract, whether water losses, energy savings, flexibility or agent performance, needs a trusted measurement layer. The earlier report's finding was that winners own the measurement standard.
3. **Legacy adapters.** Systems of record such as SAP, Maximo and MES hold the real work, and they lack safe action interfaces for agents.
4. **Scarcity-driven decision software.** Power, long-lead equipment and critical minerals are constrained, and buyers lack software that turns public data into bankable decisions.
5. **Deadline-driven migration.** Post-quantum cryptography, battery passports and export controls have dates that create budgets.

## Ranked software opportunities

**Tier A: strongest evidence and clearest buyer**

1. **Time-to-power and interconnection intelligence (84/100, high confidence).**
   - *What:* probabilistic, bankable estimates of when a site can get power, combining queue data, substation capacity and equipment lead times.
   - *Buyer:* data-centre and industrial developers, with a budget of roughly $100–400k per year.
   - *Evidence:* the median US project took more than five years from interconnection request to operation, and transformer lead times averaged 128 weeks.
   - *Moat:* a track record of forecast accuracy.
   - *Risk:* utilities publish their own maps.
   - *Entry:* a paid PJM or ERCOT pilot with five developers.

2. **Controlled-mineral BOM exposure and licence compliance (78, medium confidence).**
   - *What:* infer rare-earth, gallium and germanium content from BOMs down to tier 3–5 and run it against China, US and EU rules.
   - *Gap:* existing mapping vendors map ownership, not material content.
   - *Risk:* a durable US–China deal removes the urgency. China's extraterritorial rules are suspended only until 10 November 2026, which makes timing the immediate test.

3. **OT and embedded post-quantum migration (76, high confidence).**
   - *What:* firmware and SBOM crypto scanning, hybrid post-quantum signing and compliance evidence for device makers.
   - *Why now:* NIST deprecates RSA and ECC after 2030, and CNSA 2.0 applies to new national-security acquisitions from 2027. Meters, relays and PLCs shipped today will still be in service in 2035.
   - *Risk:* HSM vendors bundle it, and device makers defer.

4. **Inspection-to-closed-work-order for industrial integrity (76, medium confidence).**
   - *What:* extract inspection reports (NDT, thickness, cathodic protection) from PDFs and turn them into risk-ranked work orders inside SAP PM or Maximo. The value is in closing the work, not a dashboard.
   - *Gap:* incumbents assume clean data.
   - *Entry:* a backlog clean-up project that converts to per-site SaaS.
   - *Risk:* incumbents add LLM features.

**Tier B: real demand, more contested or earlier**

5. **Agent authority and control plane for regulated workflows (hypothesis).**
   - *What:* runtime policy, spending and action limits, and tamper-evident audit for agents acting on payments, claims or procurement approvals.
   - *Differentiation:* sell into one vertical and produce evidence an insurer or auditor will accept. A generic control plane will lose to identity vendors and hyperscalers.
   - *Why it ranks here:* the BCG control gap is the strongest demand signal in this scan. Crowding is the main reason it isn't Tier A.

6. **Outcome measurement and verification infrastructure (medium-low confidence).**
   - *What:* lender-grade M&V software for performance contracts, starting with non-revenue water, where an academic estimate puts annual losses at $39bn.
   - *Buyer:* development banks, energy-service companies and lenders.
   - *Expansion:* energy efficiency, storage and agent performance guarantees.
   - *Risk:* slow public-sector procurement.

7. **Agent-readiness layer for legacy systems of record (hypothesis).**
   - *What:* a permissioned, business-rule-aware action layer between agents and SAP, Maximo or MES. This goes beyond a thin protocol wrapper.
   - *Evidence:* Bain's count of MCP servers grew from roughly 1,000 to 7,000 since February 2025, but that shows supply of connectors, not a solved problem.
   - *Risk:* SAP, Oracle and ServiceNow ship their own. Pair it with item 4 as the wedge.

8. **Flex-to-connect orchestration software (77, medium confidence).**
   - *What:* verifiable curtailment and workload-shift control, so a data centre can trade flexibility for faster grid connection.
   - *Risk:* the gating factor is whether utilities credit flexibility at all, and the sales cycle is long.

9. **Battery state-of-health certification (71, medium confidence).**
   - *What:* trusted health certificates for lessors, insurers and lenders.
   - *Why now:* the EU battery passport takes effect on 18 February 2027.
   - *Risk:* passport software is crowded, and OEMs may restrict BMS data. Sell the certificate, not the passport.

**Tier C: promising but narrower**

10. **Robot deployment and safety-case tooling (71).** Pilots stall at safety proof and uptime evidence. The sensible bet is tooling that works whichever humanoid wins, since Agility's reported 2025 net sales were only $1.782M against a $140.2M operating loss.
11. **AI Act evidence automation for Annex III verticals (hypothesis).** Conformity evidence generated from runtime logs, aimed at HR, credit and education AI. Governance, risk and compliance (GRC) vendors are crowded here, and the 2027 deadline reduces urgency.
12. **Machine-readable B2B procurement for scarce equipment (hypothesis).** Gartner forecasts 90% of B2B transactions mediated by AI agents by 2028, which I treat as indicative only. A narrow start in long-lead electrical gear fits the earlier report's secondary-market and procurement-intelligence findings.

## What I would avoid

- **Generic AI FinOps.** Deloitte notes usage is growing far faster than unit costs are falling, so the pain is real. But the space is crowded and cloud vendors bundle the feature.
- **Generic technician copilots, AI documentation tools and agentic-commerce storefronts.** All three are crowded without a distinct data advantage.
- **Dashboards.** Without a closed action, they fail your own data-to-action test.

## If I had to pick three to validate first

1. **Time-to-power intelligence.** Interview ten developers to confirm budgets above $100k, then back-test forecasts on 2020–2024 projects.
2. **Inspection-to-work-order, with the legacy action layer as the product's second act.** This is the shortest path to revenue, via a fixed-price backlog project.
3. **OT post-quantum migration.** A fixed deadline and high confidence make it the least speculative of the three.

The agent control plane has the strongest demand signal, but it is the most contested. I would test it only through a narrow vertical, not as a platform.

Market sizes in the earlier report are bottom-up estimates, not verified figures, and each Tier B and C item needs customer discovery before you commit. I can turn this into a full scored document with dossiers for the top ten, but that would need Research turned back on for deeper source work.
