Your report on post-quantum migration for OT and embedded systems is ready. Its main finding is that the hardest problem isn't choosing algorithms. It's renewing roots of trust in devices that are already installed. Most field equipment bought today will still be running when NIST disallows RSA and ECC in 2035, and the main risk is forged firmware and device identity rather than decrypted traffic.

The report covers:
- **Rules and deadlines:** the policy timeline, including the final NIST IR 8547, CNSA 2.0, and the EU, UK and Australian targets.
- **Protocol readiness:** OPC UA, IEC 62351, DNP3-SA, CIP Security and AUTOSAR have no published PQC profiles yet, which is the real bottleneck.
- **Benchmarks:** measured Cortex-M results show compute is mostly solved. The binding limits are signature size, signing memory and side-channel protection.
- **Ideas and market:** 22 scored innovation hypotheses, plus a bottom-up market estimate. Published market estimates disagree fourfold, so the report estimates OT spend directly at low single-digit billions per year by the early 2030s.
- **Product blueprint:** an "Industrial Trust Plane," with hash-based multi-root boot, managed custody for stateful firmware-signing keys, gateways that block fallback to classical crypto, and symmetric key distribution for devices that can never run PQC. It comes with an MVP and validation plan.

Some recent policy details come mainly from a secondary source, and some benchmarks are vendor-measured. The caveats section flags these and says what to check against primary documents.

**Reinventing Post-Quantum Migration for OT and Embedded Infrastructure: From Algorithm Swaps to a Managed Trust Plane**

# Reinventing Post-Quantum Migration for OT and Embedded Infrastructure: From Algorithm Swaps to a Managed Trust Plane

The most important finding: in OT, the post-quantum problem is mainly about who is allowed to change a device's firmware and identity, not about who can read its traffic. It cannot be fixed one endpoint at a time. Most field devices installed today will still be running when NIST disallows RSA and ECC in 2035. Their cryptography is frozen into silicon, bootloaders, protocol parsers and safety certifications. So the winning approach is to manage trust renewal as an external, continuously operated layer, built on hash-based roots, gateways and symmetric keys. Swapping ML-KEM and ML-DSA into every PLC is not that approach.

## TL;DR

- **The deadline is set.** NIST published the final IR 8547 on 27 October 2026. It deprecates every quantum-vulnerable public-key algorithm after 2030 and disallows them after 2035, regardless of key size. The EU wants critical infrastructure's high-risk use cases migrated by the end of 2030, and Australia wants classical public-key cryptography gone by the end of 2030. OT assets bought today have 15–30-year lives, so most will outlive these dates.
- **The real threats are forgery and frozen roots, not decryption.** The big OT exposures are forged firmware and forged device identity, plus trust anchors that cannot be changed once deployed. Harvest-now-decrypt-later matters for long-lived keys, engineering data and remote-access sessions, but much less for sensor telemetry. Stateful hash-based signatures (LMS/XMSS) are the strongest near-term tool for firmware roots. CNSA 2.0 already required them to be "supported and preferred" by 2025, with exclusive use by 2030.
- **The opportunity is a "trust plane", not another scanner.** That means a continuously operated layer for managing cryptographic trust: hash-based root anchors with remote renewal, signature-free KEM-based handshakes for constrained links, gateways that block downgrades, binary-level crypto discovery, and symmetric key distribution for devices that can never run PQC. Industrial protocol standards are the binding constraint. OPC UA, IEC 62351, DNP3-SA, CIP Security and AUTOSAR SecOC have no published PQC profiles yet, so for most of this decade the only control an asset owner can deploy alone is wrapping at the boundary.

## Key Findings

1. **The policy clock has hardened.** The final NIST IR 8547 (27 Oct 2026) dropped the draft's carve-out that let RSA-3072, P-256 and Ed25519 run to 2035. All approved quantum-vulnerable public-key schemes are now deprecated after 2030 and disallowed after 2035. Executive Order 14412 (22 June 2026) and OMB M-26-15 (24 June 2026) require federal high-value and high-impact systems to use PQC for key establishment by 31 Dec 2030 and for signatures by 31 Dec 2031. A FAR rule for contractors is due around 19 Dec 2026. CISA's minimum elements for a cryptographic bill of materials (CBOM) are due around 19 March 2027.
2. **Hybrid now has a validation path; hybrid signatures remain unsettled.** IR 8547 states that a hybrid key-establishment scheme "can be FIPS 140-validated" if at least one input comes from an approved scheme. For hybrid signatures, NIST says "the security properties of hybrid signatures are complex and need to be carefully analyzed." The signature half of the migration, which is the half that matters most for OT, is therefore the slower half.
3. **Industrial protocols are the bottleneck, not algorithms.** As of November 2026 there is no OPC UA PQC SecurityPolicy (its newest asymmetric policies are ECC, from v1.05). IEC 62351-9:2023 says that "at this time being no specific measures are provided" for PQC. DNP3-SAv6 is only "designed to accommodate" PQC and is still in draft. ODVA's October 2026 CIP Security announcements mention no PQC. AUTOSAR states that "current TLS standards available don't provide post quantum ready asymmetric cryptography yet." OEMs cannot ship compliant PQC firmware for protocols whose standards do not yet define it.
4. **Microcontroller compute is mostly solved; bytes, stack and side channels are not.** On Cortex-M4, ML-KEM-768 decapsulation takes 707,827 cycles (pqm4 m4fspeed), about 30 ms at 24 MHz. ML-DSA-44 verification takes 1.42 M cycles with 8.9 KB of stack. The hard parts are elsewhere: ML-DSA-44 signing needs about 44.8 KB of stack, and signatures are 2.4–4.6 KB against 64 bytes for ECDSA. A first-order masked ML-KEM-768 decapsulation costs about 3 M cycles, a 3.5× overhead. And radio frames are tiny: an ML-KEM-768 exchange needs 18 IEEE 802.15.4 frames.
5. **Secure boot with PQC is already practical on mid-range parts.** wolfBoot verified an ML-DSA-87 image on an NXP i.MX95 Cortex-M7 in 5.25 ms (vendor-measured). Its bootloader code was 940 bytes smaller than the ECDSA P-256 build, but each image header grew by about 7 KB. Infineon's OPTIGA TPM SLB 9672, announced in February 2022, has a firmware update mechanism protected by XMSS signatures, so the TPM "can still be updated if the standard algorithms are no longer trusted." The cost moves out of compute and into image headers, slot sizes and key-state management.
6. **The deepest unsolved problem is renewing roots of trust in fielded devices.** Siemens' S7-1200/1500 hardcoded-key case (CVE-2022-38465) showed what happens when a trust anchor sits in a secure element that cannot be patched. Every device shipping today with a single classical root key is a future replacement liability.
7. **Market numbers are unreliable.** Published 2025 "PQC market" estimates range from USD 0.42 B (MarketsandMarkets) to USD 1.6 B (Grand View Research), a fourfold spread in the base year alone, and none models OT specifically. My bottom-up estimate puts addressable OT and embedded trust-lifecycle spend in the low single-digit billions per year by the early 2030s.

---

# Part I — The Big Picture

## What the threat actually is

Shor's algorithm solves integer factorisation and discrete logarithms (finite-field and elliptic-curve) in polynomial time on a fault-tolerant quantum computer. That breaks RSA, DH, ECDH, ECDSA and EdDSA completely: no key-size increase helps. Grover's algorithm gives only a quadratic speed-up against symmetric ciphers and hashes, and it parallelises poorly. AES-128 keeps meaningful security, and AES-256, SHA-384 and SHA-512 are comfortable. **In short: every public-key operation must change, and symmetric primitives mostly need not.** That asymmetry is the single most under-exploited fact in OT migration planning.

How far away a cryptographically relevant quantum computer is remains a moving estimate. In May 2025 Craig Gidney (Google Quantum AI) estimated that RSA-2048 could be factored "in less than a week by a quantum computer with less than a million noisy qubits," a 20-fold reduction from his 2019 estimate with Ekerå of 20 million qubits. It is a resource estimate, not a demonstrated attack. Later 2026 preprints claim further reductions; treat them as unverified until peer-reviewed. The direction matters more than any single number: estimated attack costs keep falling faster than hardware is improving.

## Why OT is different (and harder)

Mosca's inequality frames the risk: if the time data must stay secure (x) plus the time to migrate (y) exceeds the time until a quantum attack is possible (z), you are already exposed. In IT, x is often short and y is 3–5 years. In OT, three things change:

- **Asset life is far longer.** The Thales OTCEP 2026 deck for Singapore's CSA gives typical OT lifetimes of 7–10 years for ICS gateways, 15–20 years for RTUs and protection relays, and 25–30 years for safety PLCs and turbine control. A safety PLC commissioned in 2026 runs about 21 years past the 2035 disallowance date.
- **Migration takes much longer.** OEM firmware, IEC 61508/62443 recertification, outage windows and vendor-standard dependencies stretch migration to a decade or more. CISA's October 2024 guidance calls OT PQC migration "a significant and enduring challenge."
- **The asset that matters is authority, not secrecy.** A forged firmware signature or device certificate gives an attacker persistent control of a physical process; a decrypted Modbus reading usually reveals little. Thales calls the relevant variant "HNFL", harvest-now-forge-later: capture a vendor's public firmware-signing key today, derive its private key once a quantum computer exists.

## The core reframing

The usual question is "how do we put ML-KEM and ML-DSA into every device?" The better question is how a 30-year physical system can **renew its own cryptographic authority** several times during its life, without being physically touched and without recertifying its safety function each time. NIST's own final report expects hybrid to lead to "a second transition to post-quantum-only cryptography." The SIKE and Rainbow breaks (both 2022) show that newly standardised families can fail suddenly. A 30-year asset should therefore expect two or three cryptographic transitions in its life, not one.

---

# Part II — The Existing System: A Layer-by-Layer Map

| Layer | What it does today | Typical crypto | Why it exists | PQC limitation | What could replace or improve it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Silicon RoT (ROM bootloader, OTP fuses, secure element, PUF) | Anchors the first verification step | ECDSA P-256 or RSA-2048 public-key hash in fuses; SE such as ATECC608 | Makes trust immutable so malware cannot change it | Fused classical keys cannot be rotated; SE firmware is often unpatchable | Multiple fused roots of different types (classical + LMS/XMSS + SLH-DSA); hash-only ROM verifiers |
| TPM 2.0 / DevID (IEEE 802.1AR IDevID/LDevID) | Device identity, attestation, measured boot | RSA-2048 or ECC P-256 keys | Manufacturer-backed identity | TPM PQC is only on roadmaps; IDevID certificates issued at manufacture for life | PQC-capable TPMs (Infineon roadmap); LDevID re-enrolment via KEM-based protocols |
| Bootloader / update (MCUboot, wolfBoot, U-Boot, vendor) | Verifies images; A/B slots, rollback | ECDSA/RSA + SHA-256 | Blocks malicious firmware | Fixed header and TLV sizes, flash slot sizing, verification RAM | LMS/XMSS or ML-DSA verifiers (wolfBoot ships both); SUIT manifests |
| RTOS / embedded Linux crypto (mbedTLS/PSA, wolfSSL, OpenSSL) | TLS, DTLS, signatures | ECDHE + ECDSA | Secure channels | Static buffers, ASN.1 parsers with hard size limits | PSA Crypto with PQC drivers; size-agnostic parsers |
| Field protocols (Modbus/TCP, DNP3-SA, IEC 61850 GOOSE/SV with IEC 62351-6, OPC UA, CIP Security, PROFINET Security, BACnet/SC) | Process data and commands | None (Modbus) or HMAC/AES with RSA/ECC key setup | Real-time determinism | No PQC profiles published (Part IV) | Keep symmetric data path; move PQC into key distribution and management |
| Constrained wireless (802.15.4/6LoWPAN, Zigbee, WirelessHART, LoRaWAN, BLE) | Sensor networks | AES-CCM with network or pre-shared keys; LoRaWAN AES root keys | Energy and bandwidth limits | 127-byte frames; LoRa payloads as small as 51 bytes | Symmetric data paths already quantum-resistant; PQC only at join servers and back ends |
| Automotive (CAN/CAN-FD, SecOC, automotive Ethernet) | ECU messaging | AES-128 CMAC (e.g., SecOC Profile 2: 24-bit CMAC) | 8–64-byte frames | Data path quantum-safe; key management and backend PKI are not | PQC backend PKI and OTA signing |
| Gateways, remote access, IDMZ | Segmentation, translation, vendor access | IPsec/IKEv2, TLS, SSH | Boundary control | Software-updatable, so easiest place to start | Hybrid IKEv2 (X25519 + ML-KEM-768), already demonstrated |
| Enterprise PKI, HSMs, code signing | Issue certificates, sign firmware | RSA/ECDSA CAs, HSMs | Centralised trust | HSM upgrades; stateful HBS state management | PQC HSMs; HBS state-managed signing services |
| Rail ERTMS/ETCS | Train-to-trackside keys | Classical PKI over TLS (UNISIG SUBSET-137, 2015) | Interoperable safety signalling | No public PQC plan found | Symmetric KMC continuity plus PQC online key management |

**What each component hides.** Across the stack, **cryptographic sizes are hard-coded in places nobody calls "crypto"**: buffer sizes in protocol parsers, fixed certificate fields in engineering tools, TLV length fields in image headers, flash partition maps, EEPROM layouts for key storage. This is the "crypto is 2% of the code but 100% of the blocker" problem. The algorithm is a library call; the sizes are architecture.

**A cautionary case study.** In 2022, Claroty's Team82 extracted a hardcoded global private key protecting Siemens SIMATIC S7-1200/1500 PLC families (CVE-2022-38465). Siemens' fix was a new PKI with per-device keys in TIA Portal V17. A 20 October 2022 CPO Magazine report argues Siemens "opted to hardcode the credentials to save users and integrators from the complexities of key management systems, which did not exist at the time for industrial systems." That sentence explains much of OT cryptography: **key management was the missing infrastructure, and hardcoding was the workaround.** PQC migration will repeat this failure unless key management is supplied as infrastructure.

---

# Part III — The Fundamental Problems

## Root causes (why the problems exist)

1. **Immutability was the security goal.** Roots of trust are fused into hardware so attackers cannot change them, and that same immutability makes cryptographic renewal impossible. The trade-off was rational when RSA and ECC were expected to last indefinitely.
2. **Safety certification freezes the whole binary.** Under IEC 61508 and its sector derivatives, changing a safety-relevant binary triggers impact analysis and often re-assessment. If the crypto library is linked into the safety image, an algorithm swap is a safety change. Thales notes TÜV/SIL recertification "adds years per family."
3. **The protocol standards come first, and they have not moved.** Thales: "OEMs cannot ship compliant firmware before the standards move."
4. **The economics are misaligned.** The asset owner bears the risk, the OEM controls the firmware, the integrator holds the configuration, and the standards body controls interoperability. Nobody owns the whole trust chain, so nobody pays for its renewal.
5. **Real-time determinism rules out large handshakes in the data path.** GOOSE trip messages must arrive within a few milliseconds (Thales cites GOOSE < 4 ms). Thales' conclusion: "Deterministic protection traffic (GOOSE / SV) is not the migration target — PQC protects key distribution and boundary trust only."
6. **Physics and radio limits.** An IEEE 802.15.4 frame is 127 bytes; a single ML-DSA-65 signature (3,309 bytes) spans dozens of frames, and every extra frame raises the chance a handshake fails on a lossy link.

## The second- and third-order cascade

Moving from ECDSA (64-byte signature) to ML-DSA-65 (3,309-byte signature, 1,952-byte public key) or ML-DSA-87 (4,627/2,592 bytes) sets off a chain:

- **Larger image headers:** roughly 7 KB more per signature for ML-DSA-87 (wolfBoot i.MX95); hybrid dual signatures add more.
- **Slot and memory layout:** header growth can push an A/B layout past a sector boundary; verification RAM rises (ML-DSA-44 verify needs 8.9 KB of stack in pqm4's fast variant) and can collide with statically allocated RTOS memory.
- **Partition maps and bricking risk:** changing the partition map usually needs a bootloader update, the riskiest field operation, requiring a maintenance window.
- **Delivery over slow links:** larger bundles and certificate chains take longer over narrowband backhaul, meaning longer exposure windows and more failed transfers.
- **Certification and budget:** a firmware change on a safety function triggers recertification, budgeted per product family; missing the operator's capex cycle slips migration by years.
- **Engineering tools:** workstations and configuration tools with fixed certificate buffers must change too.
- **Hybrid adds downgrade risk:** Thales warns that "unalarmed fallback to classical is an adversary-selectable downgrade," so every hybrid deployment needs downgrade detection.

## Industry misconceptions

| Common belief | Reality |
|---|---|
| "PQC is too slow for microcontrollers." | Mostly false for ML-KEM and ML-DSA verification on Cortex-M4 and above. The binding limits are signing stack, signature size on the wire, and side-channel protection. |
| "We're air-gapped, so quantum doesn't matter." | Thales: "Air-gapped does not mean crypto-free … trust still crosses via: updates, backups, vendor tools, engineering access, signed firmware." |
| "HNDL is the main risk." | For telemetry, usually low-value. For firmware-signing roots, vendor remote-access sessions, engineering project files and long-lived keys, high. |
| "Crypto-agility means supporting multiple algorithms." | Real agility needs headroom in sizes, buffers, slots and RAM, plus a renewable root. Thales: "Agility ends at vendor microcode: Class C never becomes agile." |
| "Hybrid is always safer." | Hybrid key exchange is cheap insurance. Hybrid signatures double sizes, have complex security semantics (per NIST), and invite downgrades. ASD does not recommend hybrid long-term. |
| "QKD solves the problem." | QKD does not authenticate the parties. ASD states QKD is not supported for secure communications; NSA and NCSC prioritise PQC. |

## What an experienced OT engineer would reject that a cryptographer finds reasonable

- **Fail-closed on a failed handshake.** For a protection relay, a lost connection can be more dangerous than an unauthenticated one. Thales lists "Fail-open/closed per boundary; alarm on fallback."
- **Frequent re-keying that forces reconnects.** Reconnect storms interact with failsafe timers; Thales asks for "P95 latency vs failsafe timers."
- **Active scanning for crypto discovery.** Thales: "no active scanning at lower OT levels."
- **"Just update the firmware."** Outage windows may come once every few years.

## What a cryptographer sees that OT practitioners often miss

- **Stateful signature misuse.** Reusing an LMS/XMSS one-time key, e.g., after restoring an HSM from backup or a cloned build server, can enable forgery. State management is an operational discipline.
- **Entropy at first boot.** Devices generating keys before the TRNG has accumulated entropy have historically produced shared or weak keys; ML-KEM encapsulation and masking add randomness demand.
- **Side channels in fielded devices.** Masking ML-KEM costs about 3.5× in cycles at first order; masking AES is comparatively cheap. Physically exposed devices need protected implementations.
- **Downgrade and negotiation attacks** in mixed fleets.
- **Long-term secrets inside "non-confidential" OT data:** engineering files, network maps and embedded credentials stay valuable for decades.

---

# Part IV — The State of the Art

## Standards and policy status (as of 5 November 2026)

| Item | Status | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| FIPS 203 ML-KEM, FIPS 204 ML-DSA, FIPS 205 SLH-DSA | Final, 13 Aug 2024 | ML-KEM-768 named recommended default in IR 8547 final |
| FIPS 206 FN-DSA (Falcon) | Not final; NIST "is developing a FIPS" | Draft submitted for approval 28 Aug 2025; sources conflict on whether the public draft is out; CAs will not ship until final |
| HQC (expected FIPS 207) | Selected 11 Mar 2025; final planned for 2027 | NIST presented "FIPS 207: HQC-KEM" Sept 2025; leaning toward seed-only private keys; no confirmed public draft found |
| NIST IR 8547 | **Final, 27 Oct 2026** | Deprecated after 2030, disallowed after 2035 at all key sizes; Ascon (SP 800-232) approved; hybrid KEMs validatable |
| NIST CSWP 39 (crypto agility) | Update final 29 June 2026 | |
| NIST SP 800-208 (LMS/XMSS) | Final 2020 | Basis for CNSA 2.0 firmware signing |
| NSA CNSA 2.0 | Firmware/software signing: prefer by 2025, exclusive by 2030; networking exclusive by 2030; OS and web/cloud by 2033; all NSS by 2035 | ML-KEM-1024, ML-DSA-87, LMS/XMSS (LMS SHA-256/192 recommended) |
| US EO 14412 / OMB M-26-15 | June 2026 | HVAs: PQC key establishment by 31 Dec 2030, signatures by 31 Dec 2031 |
| EU Coordinated Implementation Roadmap | 23 June 2025 | Start by end-2026; high-risk use cases by end-2030; "as many systems as practically feasible" by 2035 |
| UK NCSC | Discovery by 2028; priority migration 2028–31; complete 2031–35 | |
| Australia ASD ISM | Cease traditional asymmetric crypto by end-2030 | Approves ML-KEM-768/1024, ML-DSA-65/87; hybrid not recommended long-term; QKD not supported |
| Canada | Non-classified federal IT by 2035 | |
| IETF | RFC 9881 (ML-DSA in X.509, Oct 2025); RFC 9909 (SLH-DSA in X.509, Dec 2025); composite signatures in RFC Editor queue; X25519MLKEM768 widely deployed | IKEv2 ML-KEM and EDHOC PQC work active |

## Industrial protocol readiness (the real bottleneck)

| Protocol/standard | PQC status (Nov 2026) | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| OPC UA | No PQC SecurityPolicy; newest asymmetric policies are ECC (v1.05) | OPC 10000-2 release 1.05.06 (22 Oct 2025) says only "new security policies are composed to support new algorithms"; PQC-OPC-UA work is academic (IACR ePrint 2021/1563, ProVerif-verified) |
| IEC 62351 (TC57 WG15) | No PQC measures | IEC 62351-9:2023: "at this time being no specific measures are provided"; annex B.11 discusses PQC |
| DNP3-SAv6 (IEEE 1815) | Draft "designed to accommodate" PQC in association handshake; sessions already symmetric | DNP Users Group workshop notes, 31 Oct 2024; "under final review before IEEE ballot" |
| ODVA CIP Security | No public PQC plans | 22 Oct 2026 release on TÜV certification to IEC 62443-4-2 SL-C 3 |
| AUTOSAR SecOC / TLS | Gap acknowledged; SecOC symmetric (CMAC) | AUTOSAR FO R24-11 Security Overview |
| ERTMS/ETCS | No public PQC plan found | UNISIG SUBSET-137 classical PKI over TLS |

**What this means.** The parts of OT that are already symmetric (DNP3-SAv6 sessions, SecOC, LoRaWAN, GOOSE with IEC 62351-6 MACs) are quantum-resistant in their data path; only their key distribution must change. That is a strong argument for migrating key management, not data paths.

## Measured performance (Cortex-M class)

| Operation | Implementation / platform | Cycles | Memory | Source type |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ML-KEM-768 keygen / encaps / decaps | pqm4 m4fspeed, Cortex-M4 | 642,096 / 658,754 / 707,827 | ~6.7 KB peak stack (hybrid X25519 build) | Measured (pqm4; ePrint 2026/1414) |
| ML-KEM-768 full handshake | Reference C, RP2040 Cortex-M0+ | 7,530k (56.6 ms) vs 4,163k on M4 | — | Measured (arXiv 2603.19340); M0+ only 1.81× slower |
| ML-DSA-44 verify | pqm4 m4f / m4fstack | 1,421,623 / 3,242,333 | 8,912 B / 2,712 B stack | Measured (pqm4) |
| ML-DSA-65 verify | pqm4 m4f | 2,415,944 | 9,888 B stack | Measured (pqm4) |
| ML-DSA-44 sign | pqm4 m4f | — | 44,816 B stack | Measured (pqm4) |
| ML-DSA-65 sign | Cortex-M4 | ~17.1 M | — | Measured (ePrint 2026/1414) |
| FN-DSA-512 keygen / sign / verify | Cortex-M4 | 75.1 M / 34.4 M / 6.5 M | — | Measured (ePrint 2026/1414) |
| SLH-DSA-SHA2-128s verify | pqm4 clean | 7,471,794 | 1,968 B stack | Measured (pqm4) |
| HQC (all three operations) | Cortex-M4 | 19.6–74.5 M total vs 3.7–8.8 M for lattice KEMs | — | Measured (ePrint 2026/1414) |
| First-order masked ML-KEM-768 decaps | Cortex-M4 | 2,978,441 | — | Measured (Heinz, Kannwischer et al.) |
| ML-DSA-87 secure boot verify | wolfBoot, i.MX95 Cortex-M7, 800 MHz | 4,201,179 (5.25 ms); 2.4× ECDSA P-256 | .text 21,608 B | Vendor-measured (wolfSSL) |
| LMS/XMSS verify | — | **Not in pqm4** | — | Gap: no neutral benchmark |

**Sizes (bytes):** RSA-2048 key and signature 256 each. ECDSA P-256 key 64–65, signature 64. ML-KEM-768 encapsulation key 1,184, ciphertext 1,088. ML-DSA-44 key 1,312, signature 2,420; ML-DSA-65 1,952/3,309; ML-DSA-87 2,592/4,627. SLH-DSA-128s key 32, signature 7,856. FN-DSA-512 signature about 666. LMS public keys about 48–56; a single-tree LMS SHA-256/192 signature with h=10, w=8 is about 900 bytes (my calculation from SP 800-208 formulas).

**Interpretation.** For firmware verification, LMS gives the smallest public key, a sub-kilobyte signature and hash-only verification, so a ROM needs only a hash engine most MCUs already have. That makes LMS the most natural root anchor for constrained devices, subject to signing-side state management. ML-DSA verification is fast but expensive in bytes. FN-DSA has the smallest lattice signatures, but floating-point Gaussian sampling makes signing hard to protect, and it is not final. SLH-DSA is the conservative stateless backup root. HQC is about 5–10× more expensive than ML-KEM on the M4: a backup, not an embedded default.

**Network effects.** On IEEE 802.15.4, an ML-KEM-768 exchange needs 18 frames. On LoRa with a 51-byte payload, ML-KEM-1024 needs 62 packets, and handshake success can fall toward 50% even with high per-packet delivery (MDPI *Future Internet*, 2026). ML-KEM-1024 cannot fit an unfragmented IKE_SA_INIT on a 1,500-byte path; Thales' lab demo used RFC 7383 IKEv2 fragmentation at MTU 1,280. When Chrome 124 enabled the hybrid key exchange (the pre-standard X25519Kyber768 draft, not final ML-KEM), the larger ClientHello split into two packets, and the Chromium Blog (23 May 2024) reported "a 4% median latency increase to all TLS handshakes in Chrome on desktop." On constrained links, **bytes, not cycles, are the binding constraint.**

**Signature-free handshakes are the key embedded insight.** In PQ-EDHOC tests on an nRF52840 over BLE, Pocero Fraile et al. ("Enabling Quantum-Resistant EDHOC") found ML-KEM-512 suites paired with HAWK1 and FALCON1 ran "19% and 54% faster" than secp256r1/ECDSA, at about 13% more RAM. A signature-free, KEM-authenticated EDHOC has also been proposed, both in the IEEE paper "Reinventing EDHOC for the Post-Quantum Era" and in IETF draft-ietf-lake-authkem-edhoc. Like KEMTLS: authenticate with KEMs, keep signatures for long-lived certificates and firmware.

## Production-ready vs experimental

- **Production-ready now:** hybrid X25519+ML-KEM-768 in TLS 1.3 and IKEv2 at gateways; ML-DSA and LMS/XMSS firmware verification (wolfBoot ships all three plus hybrid dual signing); XMSS-protected TPM firmware updates (Infineon OPTIGA TPM SLB 9672, at least 10-year availability); PQC-capable HSMs; ML-DSA certificates (RFC 9881); Ascon; CycloneDX 1.6 CBOM.
- **Maturing:** composite certificates; PQC TPMs (Infineon's next generation "embedding … ML-KEM and ML-DSA"); masked ML-KEM/ML-DSA on MCUs; PQ-EDHOC and Merkle-tree certificates.
- **Experimental:** PQC in OPC UA, IEC 62351, DNP3, CIP and SecOC profiles; FN-DSA in production; HQC on embedded; automated binary crypto discovery at OT scale; remote root-of-trust renewal.

## Leading players (what they solve, where they stop)

- **Silicon and IP** (Infineon, NXP, ST, Microchip, Silicon Labs, Rambus, Synopsys, Secure-IC, PQShield, PQSecure, Crypto Quantique): accelerators, masked cores, PQC-ready secure elements. *Gap:* cannot rescue the installed base.
- **HSM, PKI and code signing** (Thales, Entrust, Utimaco, Keyfactor, DigiCert, Venafi/CyberArk): Siemens demonstrated PQC software signing with Keyfactor (SPHINCS+, 2023). *Gap:* IT-centric; fleet-scale HBS state management is rare.
- **OT visibility** (Dragos, Claroty, Nozomi, Armis, Forescout): *Gap:* inventory devices, not crypto dependencies inside firmware.
- **Embedded TLS and boot** (wolfSSL/wolfBoot, mbedTLS/TF-M, MCUboot, Segger): *Gap:* no fleet-level lifecycle orchestration.
- **Open source:** pqm4, PQClean, liboqs/OQS, libcrux/HACL* (formally verified ML-KEM), SLOTHY (up to 32% ML-DSA and 27% ML-KEM speed-ups on Cortex-M7).

**Whitespace:** no one sells a vendor-neutral, safety-aware, offline-capable service for renewing trust anchors and keys across a mixed OT fleet.

---

# Part V — The Future

## 5 years (2026–2031): boundaries and roots

- **Gateways:** hybrid ML-KEM becomes mandatory at gateways, remote access, site links and IDMZs (Thales "Class A").
- **New products** ship with LMS/XMSS or ML-DSA verification, two or more independent root anchors, slots sized for 8–16 KB of signature metadata, and PQC-capable TPMs or secure elements.
- **Procurement is the lever:** "include PQC readiness and crypto-agility requirements in every OT RFP today" (Thales); in the US it flows down via the FAR rule and CISA CBOM elements.
- **Standards:** protocol bodies publish first PQC key-management profiles (my projection, not yet evidenced).
- **Becoming obsolete:** single-root classical secure boot; lifetime factory IDevIDs; PSK-only remote access; unalarmed hybrid fallback.

## 10 years (2031–2036): the hard middle

- **2035 disallowance** arrives; Class B and C devices need compensating architecture or replacement, and documented risk acceptance becomes routine audit work.
- **A second transition may begin:** hybrid retired toward pure PQC, or, if lattices weaken, migration to HQC, SLH-DSA or new signatures.
- **Mandatory capabilities:** remote root renewal; signed CBOMs as procurement evidence; continuous crypto-posture monitoring; HBS signing-state custody.

## 15–20 years (2036–2046): renewable trust as infrastructure

- Trust renewal becomes an operated utility, like time synchronisation or patch distribution.
- Devices carry a "cryptographic constitution": hash-anchored rules for transferring authority to new algorithms.
- **Becoming obsolete:** algorithms as product features; manual certificate management; per-vendor proprietary signing.

## The second migration

SIKE reached NIST's fourth round and was then broken by a classical key-recovery attack: Castryck and Decru (KU Leuven, IACR ePrint 2022/975, July 2022) report that their implementation "breaks SIKEp434… in about ten minutes on a single core." Rainbow fell the same year: Ward Beullens (IBM Research, "Breaking Rainbow Takes a Weekend on a Laptop," CRYPTO 2022) recovered the SL1 secret key "after on average 53 hours (one weekend) of computation time on a standard laptop." Lattices have far more cryptanalysis behind them, but hash-based signatures rest on the most conservative assumption available. **Design principle: anchor long-lived roots in hash-based schemes, use lattices for performance-sensitive sessions, and make the session layer replaceable under authority from the root.**

---

# Part VI — Radical Possibilities: 22 Innovation Hypotheses

Scores are my judgement on a 1–5 scale: N = novelty, P = technical plausibility, I = impact. **[challenges foundation]** marks direct challenges to standard assumptions.

**H1. Hash-anchored "trust constitution" in ROM [challenges foundation]** (N4 P4 I5)
- *Mechanism:* ROM holds hashes of several independent roots (classical, LMS, XMSS, SLH-DSA); a quorum can sign a "succession manifest" that installs a new root and retires an old one.
- *Addresses:* unchangeable fused anchors. *Must validate:* quorum semantics, rollback protection, partial-compromise behaviour. *Barriers:* the ROM verifier must be correct forever. *Value:* turns a replacement event into a software event for silicon vendors and OEMs.

**H2. Gateways as cryptographic proxies for frozen endpoints [challenges "every device implements PQC"]** (N2 P5 I5)
- *Mechanism:* bump-in-the-wire terminates PQC on the WAN side, speaks legacy protocols on a short protected segment. *Barriers:* the last metre stays classical. *Value:* deployable unilaterally by asset owners today.

**H3. Signature-free field devices [challenges "PKI everywhere"]** (N4 P4 I4)
- *Mechanism:* devices authenticate only with KEMs (KEMTLS/PQ-EDHOC style) and symmetric keys; only the root signs firmware. *Addresses:* signing stack (about 45 KB) and side-channel cost. *Barriers:* standards adoption.

**H4. Symmetric-key infrastructure (KDC/Kerberos-style) for closed OT cells [challenges "public key is required"]** (N3 P4 I4)
- *Mechanism:* a hardened, replicated KDC issues short-lived AES keys; PQC protects only the KDC's links and enrolment. Shor does not apply. *Barriers:* KDC is a single point of compromise, needing threshold or HSM protection. *Value:* plants, substations, rail; DNP3-SAv6's symmetric sessions already point this way.

**H5. Threshold/MPC custody for stateful HBS signing [challenges "HSM is enough"]** (N4 P3 I4)
- *Mechanism:* LMS state across separated HSMs, consensus before any one-time key index is used. *Addresses:* state reuse. *Value:* makes CNSA 2.0 firmware signing operationally safe.

**H6. Binary crypto lifting with ML/LLM assistance** (N3 P4 I4)
- *Mechanism:* decompile firmware; detect constants (S-boxes, curve parameters, Keccak round constants), call graphs and buffer sizes; output a CBOM with hard-coded size limits flagged. *Barriers:* obfuscation, encrypted images, hallucination (needs symbolic confirmation).

**H7. Cryptographic digital twin** (N3 P4 I4)
- *Mechanism:* a graph linking every key, certificate, root, protocol, buffer limit and outage window; simulates "what breaks if we swap X" and schedules migration within maintenance windows.

**H8. Separating cryptography from the safety function [challenges "crypto change means safety recert"]** (N3 P3 I5)
- *Mechanism:* crypto in an isolated partition or co-processor (TrustZone-M, separate core) with a freedom-from-interference argument, so crypto updates do not touch the SIL image. *Must validate:* assessor (TÜV, exida) acceptance. *Value:* perhaps the biggest single cost lever.

**H9. Delegated verification ("verify-once, attest-many")** (N3 P3 I3)
- A capable gateway verifies a large PQC signature and issues short symmetric MAC tokens for constrained nodes on 802.15.4 and LoRa.

**H10. Merkle-batched fleet updates** (N3 P4 I3)
- One root signature covers a Merkle tree of per-variant manifests; each device receives a log-size proof.

**H11. "Crypto headroom" as a design rule [challenges "agility = algorithm list"]** (N2 P5 I4)
- Specify maximum sizes, not algorithms: every parser, slot and buffer sized for, say, 16 KB signatures and 4 KB keys, tested with fuzzing. Cheap now, priceless later.

**H12. Continuous crypto posture as a managed service [challenges "migration is a project"]** (N3 P4 I5)
- A subscription that continuously inventories, scores, renews and evidences crypto, as EDR did for endpoints; recurring revenue matching recurring transitions.

**H13–H15. Physical-layer key agreement, QKD backbones, and QRNG entropy services** (N1–4 P2–4 I1–2)
- Supplements, not strategies. Channel-reciprocity keys are weak in static plants; QKD still needs PQC authentication and NSA, NCSC and ASD advise against relying on it; verified entropy injection at commissioning is useful hygiene, but any validated TRNG plus DRBG suffices.

**H16. Forward-secure, time-bounded device credentials [challenges "lifetime IDevID"]** (N3 P4 I3)
- Short-lived LDevIDs renewed via KEM-authenticated enrolment; algorithms can change at renewal.

**H17. Formally verified, size-agnostic parsers** (N3 P4 I3)
- ASN.1/CBOR/TLV parsers generated from verified specifications (EverParse-style), with no hard-coded sizes.

**H18. Insurance-priced crypto risk [challenges "compliance drives spend"]** (N3 P3 I4)
- Insurers price CBOM-derived quantum exposure, creating a market deadline independent of regulation.

**H19. Hybrid-strict policy enforcement with downgrade telemetry** (N2 P5 I3)
- Gateways refuse classical-only negotiation and alarm (as Thales demonstrated).

**H20. Cross-vendor "trust escrow" consortium [challenges "OEM owns the root"]** (N4 P2 I5)
- An independent escrow holds secondary roots and can authorise renewal if an OEM exits. *Barriers:* liability, governance, OEM resistance.

**H21. Ascon-based lightweight channels for 8/16-bit legacy MCUs** (N2 P4 I3)
- Retrofit Ascon AEAD plus pre-shared keys distributed by H4: quantum-resistant without public-key operations.

**H22. Deterministic, hash-only verification boot ROMs** (N2 P5 I3)
- ROMs perform only LMS/XMSS/SLH-DSA verification, which needs no secrets, avoiding RNG, floating-point and side-channel issues entirely.

**Ranking (composite):** H1, H12, H8, H2, H4, H3, H6, H7, H5, H20, H11, H16; the rest are supplements.

**What would disprove the core hypotheses:** H1 fails if formal analysis shows quorum succession creates unacceptable rollback or attack surface. H8 fails if assessors refuse freedom-from-interference arguments for crypto partitions. H4 fails if KDC availability cannot meet IEC 61508 diagnostic coverage targets. H6 fails if precision on stripped OT firmware stays below about 80% even with symbolic confirmation.

# Part VII — Commercial Opportunities

## Market sizing: the headline numbers do not agree

| Publisher | 2025 base | Forecast | Method note |
|---|---|---|---|
| MarketsandMarkets | USD 0.42 B | USD 2.84 B by 2030 (46.2% CAGR) | Top-down/bottom-up from vendor revenues |
| Mordor Intelligence | USD 0.88 B | USD 4.60 B by 2030 | Undisclosed in summary |
| IMARC | USD 470.2 M | USD 19.17 B by 2034 | Undisclosed in summary |
| Grand View Research | USD 1.6 B | USD 20.5 B by 2033 | Undisclosed in summary |

The fourfold disagreement on the 2025 base shows "PQC market" has no shared definition. None breaks out OT. Use them only as momentum signals.

## Bottom-up OT/embedded estimate (my model; assumptions explicit)

- **OEM firmware-signing and lifecycle platforms:** 1,000–3,000 OEMs shipping signed firmware into industrial, energy, building, medical and automotive-tier markets, at USD 150k–1M per year each: **USD 0.15–3 B per year**.
- **Asset-owner boundary controls and crypto posture:** 10,000–30,000 critical-infrastructure operators with meaningful OT estates, at USD 50k–300k per year: **USD 0.5–9 B per year**, realistically at the low end near-term.
- **Silicon content:** a few dollars per device across hundreds of millions of devices per year, captured by semiconductor incumbents.
- **Synthesis:** a realistic early-2030s OT/embedded software and services opportunity in the **low single-digit USD billions per year**. Disproof test: if the FAR rule, NIS2 transpositions and IEC 62443 revisions fail to reference PQC by 2028, the asset-owner figure collapses toward the low end.

## Who owns the problem, who pays, who influences

- **OEM product security and engineering:** owns firmware signing and roots; driven by CNSA 2.0, the EU Cyber Resilience Act and customer RFPs.
- **Asset owner CISO and OT engineering:** owns boundary and risk; influenced by regulators (NERC CIP, NIS2, TSA pipeline directives), insurers and integrators.
- **System integrators:** deployment gatekeepers. Partner, don't compete.
- **Most urgent sectors:** defence-industrial and federal-adjacent suppliers; electric grid; pipelines and water; rail (ERTMS key management); automotive (OTA signing for 15-year vehicles); medical implants (no update path).

## Product opportunities (software-first)

1. **HBS Signing Custody Service:** stateful LMS/XMSS signing with threshold state, audit trail and CNSA 2.0 evidence; my estimated pricing USD 50k–250k per year per product line. *Moat:* operational correctness.
2. **Boot and Update Kit:** MCUboot, wolfBoot and SUIT integrations with multi-root succession and slot-sizing tools; per-SKU plus royalties.
3. **OT Crypto Twin:** passive and binary discovery, CycloneDX 1.6 CBOM, dependency graph, outage-window scheduling; per site.
4. **Hybrid-Strict Gateway Policy Layer:** policy, downgrade telemetry and evidence on existing firewall and VPN vendors.
5. **Symmetric KDC for OT cells** for Class C fleets.
6. **Assurance evidence packs** for IEC 62443-4-1/4-2 and safety assessors.

**Barriers:** 12–24-month utility sales cycles; certification costs; incumbents bundling PQC "for free" with HSM or PKI renewals; OEM reluctance to share roots.

---

# Part VIII — The Blueprint: The Industrial Trust Plane

## Thesis

Build the **operating layer for cryptographic authority** in long-lived infrastructure: a vendor-neutral system that decides, proves and renews *who may change what* across a fleet, with algorithms as replaceable parts.

## Architecture (five planes)

1. **Root plane (in device):** multi-root, hash-anchored ROM or bootloader verifier (H1, H22) with classical, LMS and SLH-DSA roots and quorum-signed succession manifests; crypto headroom (H11). First on wolfBoot or MCUboot for Cortex-M33/M55 and i.MX-class parts.
2. **Authority plane (cloud/HSM):** threshold-custodied HBS signing (H5); Merkle-batched manifests (H10); succession policy.
3. **Session plane:** hybrid-strict gateways (H2, H19); KEM-authenticated enrolment and short-lived credentials (H3, H16); symmetric KDC for closed cells (H4).
4. **Knowledge plane:** binary crypto lifting plus a cryptographic digital twin (H6, H7) producing signed CBOMs.
5. **Assurance plane:** crypto-partition safety arguments and evidence packs (H8); downgrade and posture telemetry.

## Innovation principles

- Hash for roots, lattices for sessions, symmetric for data.
- Renewal is a feature; immutability belongs to the *rules*, not the keys.
- Never fail unsafe: availability policy is set per boundary.
- Evidence is a product: every action emits auditable proof.

## MVP (12–18 months)

- **For OEMs:** (a) HBS signing custody with state safety; (b) multi-root wolfBoot/MCUboot integration with a succession-manifest specification on two MCU families; (c) a slot and parser headroom analyser.
- **For one utility pilot:** (d) passive CBOM plus binary-lifting report for one substation design.

## Validation plan

1. Formal model of succession (Tamarin/ProVerif) and fuzzing of manifest parsers.
2. Fault-injection and power-loss testing during root rotation.
3. Benchmarks on Cortex-M0+/M4/M33/M7: boot time, flash, RAM.
4. Safety-assessor pre-assessment of the crypto-partition argument.
5. Two OEM design wins and one utility pilot as commercial proof.

## Moat

- Correctness of HBS state custody (failures are catastrophic, so trust compounds).
- An installed base of succession roots: once a device's ROM trusts our manifest format, renewal runs through us.
- Assessor-accepted safety arguments.
- A cross-vendor CBOM and firmware-knowledge graph.

---

# Part IX — The Strategic Verdict

## Genuinely important

Firmware-signing roots and device identity; remote access and gateway boundaries; protocol bodies adding PQC key-management profiles; HBS state management; crypto headroom in every new design.

## Overhyped

QKD for OT; "quantum-safe" telemetry encryption at Level 0/1; top-down market forecasts; treating MCU algorithm performance as the main barrier; one-time "migration projects" sold as if there will be no second transition.

## Deepest unresolved questions

1. How to renew a fused root of trust remotely and safely, with a formal proof.
2. Whether safety assessors will accept crypto isolation from SIL functions.
3. Long-term lattice security margin, and how fast backup families can be deployed.
4. Affordable higher-order side-channel protection for ML-KEM and ML-DSA on cheap MCUs.
5. Reliable binary-level crypto discovery at OT scale.
6. Governance of orphaned devices whose OEM has disappeared.

## Technical opportunity map

| Opportunity | Time to value | Defensibility | Who pays |
|---|---|---|---|
| HBS signing custody | 0–2 yrs | High | OEMs |
| Hybrid-strict gateway policy | 0–2 yrs | Low–medium | Asset owners |
| Crypto twin + binary CBOM | 1–3 yrs | Medium–high | Asset owners, insurers |
| Multi-root succession boot | 2–5 yrs | Very high | Silicon vendors, OEMs |
| Crypto/safety partition assurance | 2–5 yrs | High | Safety OEMs |
| OT symmetric KDC | 2–4 yrs | Medium | Utilities, rail |
| Trust escrow consortium | 5+ yrs | Very high | Industry, regulators |

## Breakthrough roadmap

- **Research first:** formal succession protocols; HBS threshold state; KEM-only industrial handshakes.
- **Prototype next:** multi-root wolfBoot/MCUboot; passive OT CBOM plus binary lifting; hybrid-strict gateway telemetry.
- **Test:** fault injection during rotation; LoRa and 802.15.4 handshake reliability; assessor pre-review.
- **Validate commercially:** OEM design wins tied to CNSA 2.0 and CRA; a utility pilot tied to EU 2030 high-risk deadlines.

## The ultimate strategic thesis

**The single most important unsolved problem is renewing roots of trust in devices that are already deployed.** The industry is solving "which algorithm," which is already answered. It has barely begun on "how does a 30-year device safely transfer authority to algorithms that do not exist yet?" The most compelling opportunity is to make that transfer routine: hash-anchored, quorum-governed root succession in the device, plus a vendor-neutral service that custodies, exercises and proves that authority across fleets. If this works, PQC migration and every later cryptographic migration become operations rather than replacement programmes.

## Caveats

- **Recent policy facts come partly from secondary sources.** Final IR 8547 contents and EO 14412 / M-26-15 details are drawn mainly from the Cloud Security Alliance research note of 30 Oct 2026; verify against primary documents.
- **FN-DSA and HQC status is contested.** Sources disagree on whether public drafts of FIPS 206 and the HQC standard are out.
- **Some benchmarks are vendor-measured.** The wolfBoot i.MX95 numbers are vendor-measured; pqm4 has no neutral LMS/XMSS benchmark.
- **Protocol-status findings are not proof of absence.** Members-only drafts (DNP3, IEC) may contain unseen PQC work; automotive and rail coverage was thin.
- **Market model inputs are mine.** OEM counts, operator counts and spend per entity are assumptions, not sourced data.
- **Quantum resource estimates beyond Gidney (2025) are unverified preprints.**

I've finished the revised report on post-quantum migration for OT and embedded systems. The main idea from the earlier report holds up: in OT, the post-quantum risk is mostly about who can change a device's firmware, keys and identity, not who can read its traffic. Two of its conclusions didn't survive the audit. Renewing a deployed root of trust isn't an unsolved problem, because quorum-based succession already runs in TUF/Uptane and RFC 5011, and the earlier market estimate was about ten times too high.

The report covers:
- **Audit:** a check of 29 earlier claims against sources. NIST's final IR 8547 is confirmed: RSA, ECC and DH are deprecated after 2030 and disallowed after 2035, whatever the key size. Two figures were corrected, the DNP3-SAv6 status and the 802.15.4 frame count.
- **Background:** the basics of quantum computing, Shor and Grover, and how the post-quantum algorithms work (ML-KEM, ML-DSA, FN-DSA, LMS/XMSS, SLH-DSA). Estimates of what it takes to break RSA and elliptic-curve keys fell sharply in 2025–26, but the hardware is still far off.
- **Protocols:** OT walkthroughs by sector and a protocol-by-protocol check. The real-time data path is mostly symmetric, but OPC UA, CIP Security, Zigbee Smart Energy and DLMS suites 1/2 need redesign, not just new key management.
- **Embedded numbers:** benchmarks, LoRa airtime costs, and new LMS verification estimates. The LMS figures are my own calculations, not measurements.
- **Root of trust:** what is still unsolved is narrower. It means putting verifiers from more than one algorithm family into silicon before it ships, revoking keys on weak devices, keeping time on offline devices, and governing orphaned fleets.
- **Market:** consolidation into incumbents, with Nozomi, Venafi/CyberArk and Dragos all acquired. A bottom-up model puts the share a standalone software vendor could win at about $100–600M a year.
- **Ideas and recommendations:** 42 hypotheses, ranked. The best commercial bet is custody and secure-boot tooling for stateful hash-based signatures, most likely ending in an acquisition. There are also recommendations for operators, device makers and builders, plus 20 open questions.

Some items rest on secondary sources only, including the FAR rule dates and the outcome of the October 2026 DNS root key rollover, which I couldn't confirm. The quantum estimates come from preprints and white papers, and the market spend levels are my own assumptions.

**Post-Quantum Migration for OT and Embedded Infrastructure: Audit, Foundations and a Revised Thesis (November 2026)**

# Post-Quantum Migration for OT and Embedded Infrastructure, Re-examined: Audit, Foundations and a Revised Thesis (November 2026)

The prior report got its central idea right: in operational technology (OT), the post-quantum problem is mostly about who may change a device's firmware, keys and identity, not who can read its traffic. Its claim that renewing a deployed root of trust is an unsolved frontier does not survive audit. Quorum-based root succession is already standardised and deployed in TUF/Uptane (automotive), automated trust-anchor rollover (RFC 5011) has run twice on the DNS root, and OpenTitan offers in-silicon ownership transfer. What remains unsolved is narrower and more physical:
- putting multiple independent post-quantum verifiers into silicon before it ships;
- revoking keys on devices too weak to process rotation metadata;
- validity and time on offline devices;
- governing orphaned fleets.

The prior commercial thesis, an "Industrial Trust Plane" worth low single-digit billions a year, is roughly an order of magnitude too optimistic for a standalone vendor.

## TL;DR

- **Policy verified.** NIST's final IR 8547 (27 Oct 2026) deprecates every quantum-vulnerable RSA/ECC/DH algorithm after 2030 and disallows them after 2035, regardless of key size. It lets hybrid key establishment be FIPS 140-validated if one input is approved. "Deprecated" still means usable with documented risk acceptance. The binding dates are federal (EO 14412, OMB M-26-15): PQC key establishment by 31 Dec 2030 and signatures by 31 Dec 2031 for high-value and high-impact systems.
- **The technical thesis survives, refined.** Protect authority first: immutable boot verifiers in new silicon, and the transport of long-lived symmetric keys (where "harvest now, decrypt later" becomes "forge later"). The real-time data path is mostly symmetric, but not everywhere. OPC UA, (D)TLS 1.2 profiles such as CIP Security, Zigbee Smart Energy and ECC-based metering need protocol redesign, not just new key management.
- **The commercial thesis shrinks.** Root-rotation protocols exist, and both 2026 mega-rotations (UEFI, DNSSEC) went to classical keys and took years. Value is being captured by PKI, HSM, silicon and OT-visibility incumbents. The best opportunities are a stateful-hash-signature custody and secure-boot toolkit, passive crypto-posture content for OT-visibility platforms, and services, most likely ending in acquisition.

## Key Findings

1. **IR 8547 is primary-confirmed.**
   - Tables 2 and 4 mark ECDSA, EdDSA, RSA, DH and MQV "Deprecated after 2030, Disallowed after 2035" at all approved strengths.
   - Hybrid key establishment "can be FIPS 140-validated, even if other inputs are disallowed".
   - "The security properties of hybrid signatures are complex and need to be carefully analyzed."
   - Three sentences the prior report missed matter more for OT:
     - devices that cannot update verification code "should be designed to require quantum-resistant signatures";
     - authentication "may continue to use quantum-vulnerable algorithms until" a cryptanalytically relevant quantum computer (CRQC) exists;
     - systems that cannot support PQC "should be identified and prioritized for replacement or decommissioning."
2. **Root succession is a solved protocol but an unsolved engineering problem.** Uptane requires Root metadata version N+1 to be signed by a threshold of version N keys and a threshold of version N+1 keys, which is the prior hypothesis H1 in all but name. Uptane also admits that partial-verification ECUs, which skip Root metadata, have "no truly secure way to invalidate a signing key".
3. **2026's largest trust-anchor rotations stayed classical.**
   - Microsoft's 2011 KEK expired 24 June 2026, the UEFI CA 27 June and the Windows PCA 19 October. The replacement is "Microsoft Corporation KEK 2K CA 2023".
   - The DNS root KSK rollover was scheduled for 11 October 2026 after 21 months of pre-publication. It is RSA-2048 to RSA-2048, and the next algorithm roll (expected around 2029) is to ECDSA.
4. **Resource estimates for breaking public-key cryptography collapsed in 2025–26; hardware remains far away.**
   - Estimates:
     - Gidney (May 2025): RSA-2048 with fewer than 1 million noisy qubits in under a week.
     - Google (Mar 2026): ECDLP-256 with about 1,200 logical qubits and 90M Toffoli gates, under 500k physical qubits, in minutes.
     - Iceberg's Pinnacle preprint: RSA-2048 with fewer than 100k physical qubits.
     - Alice & Bob (Oct 2026): 22,593 cat qubits, 3.14 days.
   - Hardware: at its November 2025 launch, Quantinuum said Helios ran "48 fully error-corrected logical qubits at a remarkable 2:1 encoding rate" (98 physical qubits). Its Apollo system, planned for 2029, targets hundreds of logical qubits.
   - Elliptic-curve cryptography, which dominates embedded systems, is now the cheaper target.
5. **No industrial protocol has a standardised PQC profile.** That covers OPC UA, IEC 62351, DNP3-SAv6, CIP Security, AUTOSAR and ERTMS. DNP3-SAv6 is unballoted, with its IEEE project reportedly extended to December 2027. AUTOSAR R25-11 still says TLS lacks post-quantum asymmetric cryptography. CIP Security is "standardized on TLS 1.2 and DTLS 1.2".
6. **Embedded PQC is real but uneven.**
   - wolfBoot supports ML-DSA, LMS/HSS and XMSS.
   - MCUboot added LMS only in a simulator-only pull request (May 2026); its imgtool offered no post-quantum key type as of 2 Nov 2026.
   - Thales Trusted Cyber Technologies says its Luna T7 module (FIPS 140-3 certificate #5450, firmware 7.15.1, August 2026) is "the world's first HSM to include all the NSA… CNSA Suite 2.0 PQC algorithms within a FIPS 140-3 validation". That covers ML-DSA, ML-KEM and LMS at Level 3.
7. **The market is consolidating into incumbents.**
   - Mitsubishi Electric paid USD 883 million for the 93% of Nozomi Networks it did not already own (9 Sep 2025 filing). The deal closed on 28 January 2026. Nozomi's net revenue was $74.7M in 2024 (about 11.8×) and $101.7M in 2025.
   - CyberArk bought Venafi for "approximately $1.54 billion" (about $1 billion cash and $540 million shares; SEC 6-K, 1 Oct 2024). Palo Alto Networks then agreed to buy CyberArk at "approximately $25 billion" equity value and closed on 11 February 2026 for $2.3 billion in cash and 112 million shares.
   - Accenture (newsroom, 18 Jun 2026) took a majority stake in Dragos and bought runZero and NetRise at a "combined enterprise value of approximately $4.175 billion". The three carry about $208M ARR, up 53% year on year. NetRise closed 31 Jul, Dragos 16 Sep and runZero 17 Sep 2026.
   - Keyfactor "exceeded $200 million in annual recurring revenue in the beginning of Q3 2026", growing more than 35% year over year (9 Sep 2026).

# PART I — Audit of the Previous Report

**Correct:**
- The authority framing.
- The "frozen cryptography" diagnosis: ROM algorithms, parser buffers, recertification.
- The preference for hash-based roots. NIST now names LMS/XMSS "for applications like software and firmware signing" and rates LMS SHA-256/192 Category 3, above ML-DSA-44's Category 2 (which drops to Category 1 with a weaker RNG).
- Scepticism toward QKD.

**Missed:**
1. Prior art for root succession: TUF/Uptane thresholds, RFC 5011 hold-downs, OpenTitan ownership transfer, the 2026 UEFI rotation.
2. **HNDL-to-authority.** Long-lived symmetric authority keys moved under RSA/ECDH can be decrypted later while still valid. Examples: DNP3 SAv5 asymmetric update-key change, OPC UA password encryption, ETCS KMC delivery. Harvest-now-decrypt-later then becomes forgery.
3. **The TLS 1.2 trap.** Hybrid groups are standardised for TLS 1.3, so TLS 1.2 profiles need a version migration first.
4. NIST's authentication carve-out (§3.1.2).
5. Rollover telemetry: DNSSEC's 2017 roll paused a year on RFC 8145 signals, and OT has no equivalent.
6. Sectors: medical, avionics/space, smart metering, rail key management, national standards.
7. No sourced market inputs.

**Over-optimistic conclusions:** "root renewal unsolved", "data path already symmetric", "billions per year", and H8 safety isolation as "the biggest lever" (no assessor evidence).

**Unasked questions:**
- Who signs configuration and logic downloads, as distinct from firmware?
- How many fielded devices verify any signature at all?
- What is the worst-case verification time against the watchdog?
- How do we learn device anchors without active scanning?

# PART II — Foundations

**Classical computing.** Bits are definitely 0 or 1, manipulated by deterministic gates. Cryptography uses problems that are easy one way and infeasible in reverse without a secret.

**Qubits.** A qubit's state is α|0⟩+β|1⟩, and measurement gives 0 with probability |α|².
- Superposition gives n qubits 2^n amplitudes, but you read only one outcome.
- Entanglement means joint states don't factor into per-qubit states.
- Interference is the real resource: algorithms arrange for wrong answers to cancel.
- A quantum computer is not "trying all answers"; it only helps on structured problems.

**Why RSA, DH and ECC fall.** Factoring N=pq and discrete logs (find x from g^x, or from xP on a curve) both reduce to finding the period of f(a)=g^a mod N.
- Shor's algorithm superposes over exponents, computes f (modular exponentiation is the costly arithmetic), applies the quantum Fourier transform so period information interferes into measurable peaks, then finishes classically.
- The cost is polynomial, so larger keys barely help.
- Grover's algorithm gives only a quadratic, poorly parallelisable speed-up on key search. NIST therefore keeps AES-128 at Category 1 and does "not expect to need to transition away" from symmetric standards.
- *Exercise:* why does doubling an AES key defeat Grover, while doubling an RSA key barely slows Shor?

**What a CRQC needs.**
- Physical qubits err about once per 10³ operations, so they must be encoded into logical qubits.
- The surface code repeatedly measures parity checks on a 2D grid, and a real-time classical decoder corrects errors. Below threshold, logical error falls exponentially with code distance (Google Willow, 2024).
- Quantinuum reported each distance step cutting logical errors 12× (Oct 2026).
- Non-Clifford gates (T/Toffoli) need magic-state distillation factories, so Toffoli counts drive cost.
- qLDPC codes (Pinnacle) and cat qubits (Alice & Bob) cut overhead.
- Today's best is tens to about 100 logical qubits. A CRQC needs roughly 1,000–1,500 logical qubits running for hours to days.

**Why it matters now.** Mosca's inequality: shelf-life + migration time > time-to-CRQC means you are already late. OT assets installed in 2026 run to 2041–2056, and a CRQC builder need not announce it.

**Dissent at full strength.** Cloudflare argues post-quantum authentication "is needed only after Q-Day risk materializes". OT practitioners note that attackers win via default passwords. Both are right for *online* authentication and wrong for verifiers frozen in silicon.

| Primitive | Role | OT example | Quantum status |
|---|---|---|---|
| AES | Encryption | GOOSE/SV options, LoRaWAN | Safe |
| HMAC/CMAC/GMAC | Integrity | DNP3-SA, SecOC, 62351-6 | Safe |
| SHA-2/3 | Fingerprint, Merkle trees | Image digests | Safe |
| DH/ECDH | Key agreement | TLS, IKEv2 | Broken by Shor |
| RSA/ECDSA/EdDSA | Signatures | Firmware, certificates | Broken by Shor |
| PKI | Binds identity to key | OPC UA, 62351 | Inherits its signature algorithm |
| DRBG | Randomness | Key generation | Safe if well seeded |

**Lattices.**
- LWE publishes b=A·s+e mod q with small noise e; recovering s is believed hard.
- Module-LWE uses polynomial rings (n=256, q=3329) for compactness. Module-SIS (find short z with Az=0) underpins ML-DSA.
- The Number-Theoretic Transform makes polynomial multiplication O(n log n). It is the hot loop.

**ML-KEM (FIPS 203).**
- Public key: (A, t=As+e).
- Encapsulation: u=Aᵀr+e₁, v=tᵀr+e₂+encode(m).
- Decapsulation: round v−sᵀu to recover m.
- The Fujisaki–Okamoto transform re-encrypts m and compares. Implicit rejection returns a pseudorandom key on mismatch.
- That comparison is where wolfSSL's 2026 flaw lay, so bugs there are key-recovery bugs.

**ML-DSA (FIPS 204), "Fiat–Shamir with aborts".**
- The signer commits w=Ay, hashes the message and the high bits of w into challenge c, and outputs z=y+cs.
- If z would leak s, the signer aborts and retries (rejection sampling). Signing time is therefore random: on RP2040, ML-DSA-44 signing averaged 158.9 ms, p99 584 ms, max 1,157 ms over 10,000 runs.
- The public key keeps only the high bits of t; a hint vector lets the verifier recover the high bits of w.
- Verification is deterministic and fast.

**FN-DSA (Falcon).** Hash-and-sign over NTRU lattices with discrete Gaussian sampling.
- Signature about 666 B.
- Floating-point signing is hard to make constant-time; verification is integer-only.
- Good for verify-only devices, hazardous for signers.

**Hash-based signatures.**
- A Winternitz one-time signature reveals hash-chain values; signing twice with the same key leaks enough to forge.
- Merkle trees authenticate many one-time keys under one root.
- LMS/XMSS (SP 800-208) are stateful: never reuse an index.
- SLH-DSA (FIPS 205) removes state using FORS plus a hypertree, at 7,856–49,856 B per signature.
- Security rests only on hash functions.
- **Statefulness changes disaster recovery:** restoring an HSM backup or failing over can reuse indices. NIST notes "strict operational controls to track signature indices across distributed systems."

**HQC** is a code-based backup KEM with larger sizes.

**Hybrid.**
- Key exchange: secrets combined in a KDF. SP 800-227 warns that combining only the secrets does not generally preserve CCA security; bind the ciphertexts too.
- Signatures: dual signatures. ML-DSA+ECDSA loses strong unforgeability because ECDSA is malleable.

*Thought experiment:* a transmitter and controller provisioned in the same factory never need public-key cryptography. PKC matters only for first contact and for renewing authority.

# PART III — The OT Ecosystem, End to End

```
[Vendor build HSM]-sign->[Firmware pkg]->[Portal/USB/Eng. workstation (TIA, Studio 5000, PCM600)]
L4 IT / vendor remote access <-DMZ-> L3 historian, OPC UA, jump host
L2 SCADA/DCS/HMI  -- IEC 104, DNP3, OPC UA, MMS --
L1 PLC/RTU/IED/safety PLC -- GOOSE/SV, PROFINET, EtherNet/IP, Modbus --
L0 sensors/actuators/WirelessHART/ISA100
Device: Boot ROM (key hash in OTP) -> bootloader -> RTOS/Linux -> app/safety image
```

The Purdue model is contested: cloud historians, IIoT gateways and remote access flatten it. Its durable lesson is that trust crosses levels via maintenance paths (updates, project downloads, backups, laptops), not control traffic.

| Authority | Holder | Algorithm today | Renewable? |
|---|---|---|---|
| ROM anchor (OTP hash) | Silicon vendor/OEM | RSA/ECDSA | Only with spare slots or revocation fuses |
| Firmware signing key | OEM HSM | RSA/ECDSA; LMS emerging | If the anchor allows |
| IDevID | OEM factory CA | ECDSA P-256 | Re-enrolment (EST/CMP/BRSKI) |
| Operational certificates | Operator CA/GDS | RSA/ECC | Yes |
| Logic/config download | Engineering tool + password | Often proprietary or none | Tool-dependent |
| Session/group keys | Master/KDC | Symmetric, sometimes RSA-transported | Yes |

**Walkthroughs:**

- **Substation IED or PLC.**
  - Lifecycle: ROM verifies against an OTP hash → OEM burns its signing-key hash and an IDevID → integrator loads SCL via the vendor tool (rarely signed) → optional TLS for MMS (62351-3/4) and GOOSE MACs with GDOI group keys (62351-9; most installed GOOSE is unauthenticated) → signed firmware pushed from the workstation, with safety retest.
  - When cryptography breaks: an ECDSA-only ROM can never verify a post-quantum signature, so the device must be replaced or have its delivery paths fenced. TLS can migrate if the device has RAM and flash headroom. GOOSE MACs survive, but GDOI must migrate.
  - Authority sits in the fuses and in whoever controls the workstation and vendor HSM.
- **Pipeline.**
  - Setup: RTUs and flow computers over VSAT (~600 ms latency) or cellular, carrying DNP3/Modbus/104 inside router VPNs, feeding leak detection.
  - IKEv2 PQC uses RFC 9370 intermediate exchanges plus RFC 7383 fragmentation, because ML-KEM-1024's 1,568 B key overflows an unfragmented 1,500 B IKE_SA_INIT.
  - Priorities: hybrid VPN, and replacing RTUs whose anchors cannot move. Mechanical relief and shutdown systems limit the consequence of forged firmware.
- **IEC 61850 station vs process bus.** The station bus carries MMS and GOOSE; the process bus carries Sampled Values at roughly 4,000–4,800 frames/s per stream. Public-key cryptography can never sit there. PQC work is entirely in GDOI and engineering authority, so the prior claim holds here.
- **Manufacturing.**
  - Authority sits in the engineering project. Siemens's per-device PLC certificates (TIA Portal V17) followed the hard-coded global key in CVE-2022-38465.
  - **OPC UA bites first:** asymmetric operations at every OpenSecureChannel, session signatures, and UserName passwords encrypted under the server's RSA key, so recorded sessions expose passwords later.
  - Mitigations now: certificate or issued-token user authentication, hybrid TLS 1.3 transport where available.
- **Small water utility.** CRS report R47315 counts "nearly 50,000 community water systems" as of June 2022, and "approximately 81% … serve fewer than 3,300 individuals." The rational plan:
  - hybrid-TLS/VPN clauses in hosting and remote-access contracts;
  - fixing PLCs that accept unsigned logic;
  - normal refresh.
  "Contract clauses only" is defensible here. *Thought experiment:* would $50k be better spent on PQC or on removing internet-exposed HMIs?
- **Rail ERTMS/ETCS.**
  - Euroradio derives session keys from KMACs distributed offline (SUBSET-114) or online (SUBSET-137, TLS with ECC PKI). Online delivery is exposed to HNDL-to-authority.
  - Domain knowledge, not re-verified: the Euroradio MAC is 3DES-based, a classical weakness.
  - Fix: TLS 1.3 hybrid, shorter KMAC lifetimes, cross-border KMC coordination.
- **Hospital devices.** OEM-signed images with regulatory change control. HL7/DICOM-over-TLS data has 50-year-plus confidentiality, so HNDL applies. The hospital's lever is procurement (crypto-agile updates, CBOM). Recertification, not size, is the constraint.
- **Satellites and avionics.** CCSDS link security is symmetric, with keys loaded pre-launch. Space already runs the "symmetric, physically provisioned" model; its exposure is ground PKI and asymmetric rekey. Avionics (ARINC 645 loadable software, DO-326A) shares the frozen-verifier problem with costlier recertification.

# PART IV — Technical Deep Dives

| Scheme | Public key (B) | Sig/ct (B) | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| ECDSA P-256 | 64–65 | 64 | Baseline |
| ML-KEM-768 / 1024 | 1,184 / 1,568 | 1,088 / 1,568 | NIST default / CNSA |
| ML-DSA-44/65/87 | 1,312/1,952/2,592 | 2,420/3,309/4,627 | Fast verify |
| FN-DSA-512 | 897 | ~666 | Signing hazardous |
| SLH-DSA-128s | 32 | 7,856 | Stateless |
| LMS SHA-256/192 H10 W8 | 48 | 900 | Cat 3, 1,024 sigs |
| LMS SHA-256/192 H10 W4 | 48 | 1,500 | ~8.7× fewer verify hashes |
| LMS SHA-256 H10 W8 | 56 | 1,452 | Cat 5 |

LMS sizes come from p=u+v, with u=⌈8n/w⌉, v=⌈(⌊log₂((2^w−1)u)⌋+1)/w⌉, and signature = 4+(4+n+pn)+4+hn.

**Filling the LMS/XMSS benchmark gap.**
- Campos et al. (Cortex-M4): LMS needs fewer cycles than XMSS, with at least 85% of time spent hashing.
- Rapidly-verifiable XMSS cut M4 verification from 13.85M to 6.56M cycles.
- Kampanakis et al.'s 0.049–0.173 Mcycles were measured on a ~2.2 GHz desktop. Do not transfer them to MCUs.
- Constructed estimate, assuming ~1,500–4,000 cycles per software SHA-256 block on M4:

| Parameter | Avg / worst hashes | Est. cycles | At 64 MHz |
|---|---|---|---|
| LMS H10 W8 | ~3,330 / ~6,640 | 5–13M avg | 80–210 ms; worst ~410 ms |
| LMS H10 W4 | ~395 / ~777 | 0.6–1.6M | 10–25 ms |
| ML-DSA-44 verify (pqm4, measured) | — | ~1.42–1.50M | ~23 ms |
| SLH-DSA-SHA2-128s verify (pqm4) | — | ~7.5M | ~117 ms |

W4 gives a bounded, secret-free, hardware-SHA-friendly verifier. W8 halves size but has a 2× worst-case spread, so size watchdogs for the worst case. *Experiment:* benchmark wolfBoot LMS on an STM32 with and without the HASH peripheral.

| Platform | Operation | Cycles | Stack |
|---|---|---|---|
| M4 (pqm4 m4f) | ML-DSA-44 sign / verify | ~3,017k / ~1,496k | 44,832 / 8,912 B |
| M4 (m4fstack) | ML-DSA-44 sign / verify | ~10,330k / ~3,242k | ~5,064 / 2,712 B |
| M0+ RP2040 (ref C) | ML-DSA-44 keygen/sign/verify | 5,297k / 21,135k / 5,849k | sign 50.6 KB |
| M0+ RP2040 | ML-DSA-65 / 87 sign peak | — | 77.6 / 119.6 KB |
| M7 (SLOTHY vs pqm4) | ML-DSA-44 sign | 7,633k vs 8,512k | — |

**Lessons.**
- Stack, not cycles, binds signing on small MCUs.
- Signing latency has a heavy tail.
- Verification fits any Cortex-M boot budget.
- Vendor claims such as PQShield's "ML-KEM in 5 KB RAM" are unverified; open alternatives are PQCA's mlkem-native and mldsa-native.

**LoRa** (51 B payload, ~2.5 s airtime, 1% duty ⇒ ~247 s per frame):
- ECDSA signature: 2 frames (~8 min).
- LMS W8: 18 frames (~1.2 h).
- ML-DSA-44: 48 frames (~3.3 h).
- SLH-DSA-128s: 155 frames (~10.6 h).
- ML-KEM-1024 exchange: 62 frames (~4.3 h, ~54% success without retries).

For a 100 KB image, even SLH-DSA adds under 8%. Size dominates only in interactive handshakes, which is why LoRaWAN's symmetric join is correct. In TLS, an X25519MLKEM768 ClientHello grows ~1.2 KB and may split, and some middleboxes reject that (tldr.fail). Test industrial firewalls and DPI first.

**Is the data path already symmetric?**

| Protocol | Asymmetric entry point | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| GOOSE/SV (62351-6) | GDOI group keys (62351-9) | **Holds** |
| MMS / IEC 104 (62351-3/4/5) | TLS handshake per session | Partly |
| DNP3 SAv5 | Optional RSA update-key change | Holds, but HNDL-to-authority |
| DNP3 SAv6/AMP | Elliptic-curve algorithms; unballoted | Holds in design |
| OPC UA | Every channel and session; RSA password encryption | **Fails** |
| CIP Security | (D)TLS 1.2 handshake | **Fails** until (D)TLS 1.3 |
| BACnet/SC | TLS 1.3 | Partly |
| LoRaWAN | None at device | **Holds strongly** |
| WirelessHART/ISA100 | Optional certificate join | Holds |
| Zigbee Smart Energy | ECC implicit certificates (CBKE) | **Fails** |
| AUTOSAR SecOC | Backend key provisioning | Holds; in-vehicle TLS/V2X fails |
| ETCS | SUBSET-137 ECC TLS | Data holds; KMAC delivery exposed |
| DLMS/COSEM | Suites 1/2 ECDH/ECDSA | **Fails** for suites 1/2 |
| MACsec / PTP | MKA (pre-shared CAK or EAP-TLS) / key management | Holds with pre-shared keys |
| Nuclear I&C | Maintenance paths; diodes | Crypto rarely the right control |

The prior claim holds on hard-real-time paths and fails as a generalisation.

**Stacks: what exists vs what is missing.**
- **MCUboot:** TLV-based image verification. LMS PR (TLV 0x26, "no platforms support this yet"); no ML-DSA or SLH-DSA; maintainers request a hybrid "double signature" mode.
- **wolfBoot:** ML-DSA, LMS/HSS, XMSS.
- **TF-PSA-Crypto:** LMS in extras/lms.c, reused by MCUboot. PSA ML-KEM/ML-DSA not verified.
- **U-Boot FIT and TF-A:** gaps.
- **wolfSSL:** full PQC; 2026 FO-check bug.
- **OpenSSL 3.5:** ML-KEM, ML-DSA, SLH-DSA; X25519MLKEM768 by default; provider model.
- **HACL\*/libcrux:** verified implementations. Bernstein's 2026 ML-DSA key-recovery-via-bugs work makes verified or tested code a requirement.
- **Keyfactor:** EJBCA has LMS, ML-DSA and ML-KEM from 9.1 and SLH-DSA from 9.3; SignServer 7.1.
- **Enrolment:** a KEM key cannot sign a CSR, so enrolment needs CMP-style KEM proof of possession. SCEP cannot do this, so retire it. BRSKI/cBRSKI handle zero-touch onboarding.
- **OpenTitan:** ROM verifies the Creator ROM_EXT, which supports ownership transfer. Documented owner keys are RSA-3072 and ECDSA-P256. Post-quantum ROM support in Caliptra/OpenTitan not verified.
- **DICE:** identities change automatically with firmware.
- **Matter:** a consortium root plus a ledger at consumer scale.
- **CycloneDX CBOM:** the de facto format.

**Genuinely new software needed:**
- (1) LMS state custody with index-reserving backup and multi-site failover;
- (2) MCUboot post-quantum TLVs, imgtool support and hybrid images;
- (3) passive OT crypto-posture extraction;
- (4) KEM replacement for asymmetric key transport in DNP3/62351-5 and the ETCS KMC;
- (5) OT conformance tests for large handshakes.

---

# PART V — Root Causes and Hidden Bottlenecks

1. **Signature growth → flash layout.**
   - ML-DSA-87 adds ~7 KB of TLV/header.
   - It can cross 4–128 KB erase sectors in both A/B slots.
   - Re-partitioning in the field is itself a bricking risk, so the change slips to the next hardware revision.
   - Fix: size headroom now (H11), or store only a key hash in the image.
2. **Statefulness → CI and DR.** A nightly CI that signs every build exhausts a 2^10 tree in about 3 years, which forces HSS multi-level trees or SLH-DSA. HSM restore becomes a hazard, and runbooks must reserve index ranges per site.
3. **Hybrid → test-matrix explosion** across modes, chains and peer versions. Interoperability plug-fests already strain; expect years of conformance cost.
4. **Handshakes → timeouts.** Larger handshakes over 600 ms links can trip application or failsafe timers. Operators may lengthen timeouts and weaken detection.
5. **Downgrade.** Unalarmed classical fallback can be selected by an adversary. Enforce hybrid-strict and log negotiated groups.
6. **Spares and version skew** silently reintroduce old anchors. Tag spares by crypto capability.
7. **Safety certification.** Crypto inside a SIL image triggers IEC 61508/61511 impact analysis. Isolation needs a freedom-from-interference argument, and there is no public evidence that assessors accept one.
8. **Entropy.** ML-DSA-44 falls to Category 1 with a random-bit generator under 192 bits. Marginal MCU TRNGs now matter.
9. **Time.** Certificates assume trusted time; OT often has unauthenticated NTP/PTP and no RTC battery. Uptane resets clocks when its time-server key rotates; OT has no analogue.
10. **Audit-gated budgets.** From 2031, each classical use needs a documented risk decision. "Boards fund obligations, not threats."
11. **The real root cause is a three-way deadlock.** OEMs wait for standards, standards move on 3–10-year cycles (SAv6: 2015 to at least 2027), and operators won't pay ahead of the standards. Only regulation and procurement break it.

---

# PART VI — The Root-of-Trust Investigation

**Physical reality.**
- The mask ROM reads key hashes from OTP/eFuse, verifies stage one and jumps to it.
- Anti-rollback counters are finite fuse bits.
- Some parts offer several key slots plus one-time revocation.
- Secure elements and TPMs hold keys separately.
- DICE derives per-boot identity from a unique device secret plus measurements.

**Rotation, succession and transfer** mean, respectively: a new key under the same authority; authority moving to a new key or algorithm; authority moving to a new owner.

| System | Mechanism | Proves | Does not solve |
|---|---|---|---|
| TUF/Uptane | Root N+1 signed by thresholds of old and new keys; roles; expiry | Deployed quorum succession | Partial-verification ECUs can't revoke; needs time |
| RFC 5011 / DNSSEC | New key signed by old; 30-day hold-down; revoke bit | Internet-scale automated rollover (2018; Oct 2026) | Stale VMs/containers fail; 2017 pause; still RSA, next roll to ECDSA |
| UEFI 2026 | KEK-signed db/dbx; 2011→2023 CAs | Fleet rotation if the PK stays trusted | OEM firmware dependence; revocation "cannot be reverted under Secure Boot"; RSA |
| OpenTitan | Creator keys in ROM; owner keys in ROM_EXT; owner endorses next owner; Creator cannot unlock a locked owner | Transfer without the manufacturer | Classical algorithms; Creator is a single root |
| Android Verified Boot | Rollback indexes, chained vbmeta | Consumer-scale anti-rollback | Single authority |
| CT / binary transparency | Append-only logs | Detects covert signing | Detection only |
| Matter DCL | Consortium root + ledger | Multi-vendor governance | Classical; consumer threat model |

**H1 verdict.** The succession logic is Uptane's, and the hold-down is RFC 5011's. What remains new:
- (a) multiple algorithm-family verifiers in ROM, so succession can switch families;
- (b) monotonic-counter hold-downs for offline devices;
- (c) fault-injection hardening;
- (d) formal proof.

That is engineering and standards work, not a new protocol.

**The bootstrap problem.** If the authorising root is itself broken, only four answers exist:
1. **Pre-commitment:** hashes of independent keys in other families, held by other custodians, committed before any break. This is the most valuable action today.
2. **Time:** hold-downs allow a veto, but offline devices cannot hear one.
3. **Physical presence:** a technician unlock.
4. **Replacement:** IR 8547's default.

*Thought experiment:* OEM gone, ECDSA root forgeable, device offline, and no pre-commitment. Only presence or replacement remain. The problem is solvable only prospectively.

**Attacks.**
- Fault injection skipping the compare branch; countered by redundant checks and sensors in silicon.
- Rollback of root epochs; counters must cover epochs.
- Freeze attacks; expiry reintroduces the time problem.
- Custodian collusion; thresholds need organisational and algorithmic independence.

I found no published Tamarin or ProVerif model of multi-family ROM succession. That is a clear research gap.
