# PART VII — Competitive Landscape

| Layer | Shipping (verified) | Unverified / announced | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| Silicon/IP | — | PQShield (customers reported to include AMD, Microchip, Collins Aerospace, Lattice), Infineon, NXP, ST, Microchip, Rambus, Synopsys, Secure-IC, Caliptra, OpenTitan | Multi-family ROM verifiers as a requirement |
| Bootloaders | wolfBoot | MCUboot LMS PR | MCUboot ML-DSA/SLH-DSA, hybrid images |
| HSM | Thales Luna T7 (FIPS 140-3 cert #5450, fw 7.15.1) | Entrust, Utimaco | Multi-site stateful-HBS custody |
| PKI/CLM | Keyfactor EJBCA/SignServer | DigiCert, AppViewX, Venafi (Palo Alto Networks), MTG | OT-protocol-aware issuance |
| OT visibility | Nozomi, Claroty, Dragos, Armis, Forescout | Crypto-inventory features | Passive PQ posture |
| Automation OEMs | No verified PQ shipments: Siemens, Schneider, Rockwell, ABB, Honeywell, Emerson, Yokogawa, GE Vernova, Hitachi Energy, SEL | — | PQ anchors in new PLCs/IEDs |
| Device management | Mender, Memfault, AWS, Azure, LwM2M | PQ signing unverified | Stateful signing integration |

**Deals.**
- Nozomi: USD 883 million for the 93% Mitsubishi Electric didn't own. The deal closed on 28 January 2026. Nozomi's net revenue was $74.7M in 2024 and $101.7M in 2025.
- Venafi: "approximately $1.54 billion" (≈$1B cash + ≈$540M shares; SEC 6-K, 1 Oct 2024).
- CyberArk: "approximately $25 billion" equity value. Closed 11 Feb 2026 for $2.3B cash and 112M Palo Alto Networks shares.
- Claroty: >$100M ARR (2023).
- Dragos+runZero+NetRise: ~$208M ARR, +53% (Accenture, 18 Jun 2026). Dragos closed 16 Sep 2026.
- Keyfactor: ">$200 million" ARR at the start of Q3 2026, growing ">35%". The $1B+ Summit Partners investment was announced 6 Jul 2026.

**Implication:** a post-quantum OT component is more likely to be bought than to displace these companies.

# PART VIII — Hypotheses H1–H22 Reassessed, and H23–H42

| H | Verdict |
|---|---|
| H1 Trust constitution | Keep, re-scoped (N2 P4 I5): multi-family ROM, counter hold-down, fault hardening, proofs |
| H2 Gateway proxies | Keep as compensating control; attest the proxy |
| H3 Signature-free devices | Keep for new designs; KEM-EDHOC immature |
| H4 KDC for closed cells | Keep; strong prior art (LoRaWAN join server, ISA100, KMC) |
| H5 Threshold stateful-HBS custody | Keep: top commercial wedge |
| H6 Binary crypto lifting | Keep, narrowed to CBOM; precision unproven |
| H7 Crypto digital twin | Merge into H12/H36 |
| H8 Crypto/safety separation | Keep as research priority; no assessor evidence |
| H9 Verify-once, attest-many | Weaken; shifts trust to the gateway |
| H10 Merkle-batched manifests | Minor; TUF/SUIT prior art |
| H11 Crypto headroom | Keep: cheapest high-impact rule |
| H12 Posture as a service | Feature for OT-visibility vendors |
| H13–15 PHY keys, QKD, QRNG | Kill as strategy |
| H16 Short-lived credentials | Keep; needs time and EST/CMP |
| H17 Verified parsers | Keep |
| H18 Insurance pricing | Market signal via 2031 deprecation records |
| H19 Hybrid-strict + telemetry | Keep; NIST logging advice |
| H20 Trust escrow | Keep, modelled on the Matter DCL; governance-heavy |
| H21 Ascon for 8/16-bit | Niche |
| H22 Hash-only ROMs | Merge into H1/H29 |

**New hypotheses:**
- **H23 Anchor telemetry.** RFC 8145-style key-tag reporting in IEC 62351-7 NSM objects or OPC UA diagnostics; makes rollovers measurable.
- **H24 Counter-based hold-down.** Boots or authenticated heartbeats replace the 30-day clock; needs a formal model against boot-spinning.
- **H25 Epoch-based validity.** Validity is counted in root epochs, not wall-clock time.
- **H26 KEM retrofit for DNP3-SA, IEC 62351-5 and KMC key transport.** Closes the most concrete HNDL-to-authority exposure.
- **H27 OPC UA over TLS 1.3 hybrid transport** plus non-password user tokens, as the fast path.
- **H28 Deadline-aware parameter choice.** LMS W4 or a hardware SHA engine, with a worst-case bound written into the safety case.
- **H29 "Hash engine first" ROMs.** SHA-accelerated LMS/SLH-DSA verification plus multiple key slots.
- **H30 Crypto partition as a pre-certified Safety Element out of Context (SEooC).**
- **H31 LMS index allocation logged** in an append-only, HSM-co-signed log; backups restore only unused ranges.
- **H32 Offline-verifiable firmware transparency** checked by the engineering workstation.
- **H33 Escrowed pre-signed succession manifests** for OEM exit.
- **H34 PQ conformance and fuzz suite for OT** ("tldr.fail for OT").
- **H35 Engineering workstation as PQ chokepoint.** PQ-signed download tokens.
- **H36 Passive CBOM** from ClientHello groups, UA policy URIs, DNP3-SA objects and certificate chains.
- **H37 Automated deprecation records** for each classical use from 2031.
- **H38 TESLA-style one-way key chains** for 8/16-bit devices.
- **H39 Spare-part crypto tagging.**
- **H40 DICE/SPDM-attested proxies.**
- **H41 Dual-family ROM requirement** in PSA Certified, IEC 62443-4-2 or procurement profiles.
- **H42 DER inverter fleets** (TLS-native IEEE 2030.5) as the first mass PQ OT target.

**Ranking:** H11 > H29/H41 > H5/H31 > H26 > H36 > H35 > H23 > H34 > H1 > H30/H8 > H27 > H42.

---

# PART IX — Commercial Opportunities

**Who's who.** Operators carry the risk; OEMs hold the signing keys; silicon vendors and OEMs control the anchors; budgets follow refresh cycles and contracts; OEMs and operators resist because of recertification and downtime.

**Sourced counts.**
- 3,405 EIA-861 filers for 2025, including 3,065 retail-serving utilities.
- 1,929 public-power utilities and 918 co-ops (2024).
- "Nearly 50,000" community water systems as of June 2022, about 81% serving fewer than 3,300 people (CRS R47315).
- ~55,000 US substations (weakly sourced).

| Segment | Count | Spend assumption (mine) | US annual |
|---|---|---|---|
| Large electric | ~200 | $250k–$1M | $50–200M |
| Mid electric | ~800 | $20–75k | $16–60M |
| Small electric | ~2,000 | ≤$10k | ≤$20M |
| Water >3,300 served | ~9,500 | $5–30k | $48–285M |

**Model.**
- US operator-side program spend: ~$0.13–0.57B a year at peak.
- Assuming the US is 25–35% of global spend, and that oil and gas, manufacturing, rail and health roughly double the base, global program spend is ~$0.7–3B a year in the early 2030s, mostly services and refresh hardware.
- The software-addressable share for a standalone vendor is plausibly $100–600M a year.
- Sanity check: three OT-visibility leaders together run at ~$0.4–0.5B recurring revenue after a decade.

| Option | Verdict |
|---|---|
| Full Industrial Trust Plane | No: too broad, incumbent-crowded |
| Stateful-HBS custody + secure-boot SDK for OEMs and silicon (H5/H31/H29) | Best wedge; exit to HSM/PKI/IP vendor |
| Passive PQ posture via OT-visibility partners (H36) | Good component |
| Services (inventory, RFP clauses, conformance H34) | Real revenue now, low moat |
| Safety assurance (H30) | Long-term |

**MVP:** LMS/HSS signing with index-reserving multi-HSM custody and an audit log, integrated with MCUboot and wolfBoot, plus a hybrid manifest. **Pilot:** one OEM line plus one HSM partner, 6–9 months. **Sales cycles:** OEMs 6–12 months; utilities 18–36 months.

---

# PART X — Outlook

- **To 2031:**
  - hybrid TLS/IKE standard in OT gateways;
  - new MCUs with LMS/ML-DSA in ROM;
  - FN-DSA and HQC published;
  - deprecation records drive audits;
  - the DNS root moves only to ECDSA (~2029).
- **To 2036:**
  - disallowance;
  - brownfield devices isolated or replaced;
  - a public CRQC is plausible *if* the 2026 estimates and the 2029–2030 vendor roadmaps hold (a forecast, not a fact).
- **15–20 years:** a second transition (hybrid → pure PQ, or lattice → backup family). Devices designed in 2027 must survive two transitions, which argues for multi-family ROMs, epoch validity and DICE identity.
- **Geopolitics:** SM2/3/4, GOST and KpqC divergence requires pluggable families and regional anchors (OpenSSL providers, PSA drivers).

# PART XI — Learning Roadmap

1. **Mathematics:** modular arithmetic and polynomial rings, then Regev's LWE paper and FIPS 203/204/205.
2. **Quantum:** Nielsen & Chuang (QFT, Shor), Gidney 2025, and Google's 2026 ECDLP white paper.
3. **Embedded:** wolfBoot LMS/ML-DSA on STM32/nRF, timed with and without the hardware SHA engine; pqm4 on a NUCLEO-L4R5ZI; port the MCUboot LMS PR to real hardware.
4. **Protocols:**
   - Wireshark DNP3 SAv5 and GOOSE/MMS traces.
   - An open62541 OpenSecureChannel.
   - An X25519MLKEM768 handshake through an industrial firewall.
5. **Root of trust:** the Uptane standard, RFC 5011, ICANN's 2019 review, and the OpenTitan ownership docs; model two-family succession in Tamarin.
6. **CBOM:** generate a CycloneDX CBOM for a firmware image.
7. **Policy:** IR 8547, M-26-15, EO 14412.

# PART XII — Twenty Unanswered Questions

1. Will assessors accept crypto partitions as freedom-from-interference elements? (Pilot certifications; IEC TC65 proposal.)
2. Is there a formally verified multi-family ROM succession state machine? (Tamarin/ProVerif.)
3. How can partial-verification devices handle revocation? (Protocol plus counters.)
4. How do offline devices get trustworthy time, or avoid needing it? (H25.)
5. What are measured LMS/XMSS verify cycles and stack across M0+/M4/M33/M55/RISC-V? (Open benchmark.)
6. What share of fielded OT devices verify any signature? (Passive study.)
7. Which firewalls and DPI engines break on split ClientHellos or fragmented IKE? (H34.)
8. When will OPC UA, IEC 62351, DNP3 and CIP publish PQ profiles?
9. Is HNDL-to-authority exploitable given real key lifetimes? (Field survey.)
10. How do you do multi-site stateful-HBS custody safely? (H31 prototype.)
11. Is masking needed on verify-only devices? (Verification uses no secrets, so likely not.)
12. Who governs anchors for orphaned devices? (H33.)
13. Will lattice margins last 30 years? (Family diversity.)
14. Can passive CBOM reach high precision?
15. What will insurers and auditors demand under the 2031 "deprecated" status?
16. When is replacement cheaper than migration? (TCO models.)
17. What does an adversary actually gain from breaking OT crypto, compared with weak controls?
18. How should SM2 or GOST anchors coexist with NIST anchors?
19. When will DNSSEC, UEFI and WebPKI roots move to post-quantum?
20. Is there a venture-scale standalone business here? (H5/H31 pilots.)

## Recommendations

**Operators:**
- Write headroom (≥16 KB signatures, ≥4 KB keys), hybrid key exchange, post-quantum firmware verification and CBOM delivery into every OT RFP now.
- Move HNDL-to-authority paths to hybrid by 2030.
- Log negotiated groups and alarm on downgrade.
- Tag spares by crypto capability.
- Document "contract clauses only" for low-consequence, tiny-budget assets.

**OEMs:**
- Specify at least two algorithm-family ROM verifiers plus a SHA engine in new silicon.
- Sign firmware with LMS/HSS under index-reserving custody.
- Migrate TLS 1.2 to 1.3 before PQC.

**Builders:** pursue stateful-HBS custody and secure-boot tooling, or passive posture as a component; expect an acquisition exit.

## Caveats

- Secondary-sourced items: FAR and CBOM dates, composite RFC status, UEFI post-June outcomes, and the outcome of the 11 Oct 2026 DNSSEC rollover (scheduled; outcome not verified).
- Quantum estimates are preprints or white papers; roadmaps are forward-looking statements.
- Members-only drafts may hold unseen PQC work.
- LMS cycle figures are constructed estimates.
- Market tiering and spend levels are my assumptions.
- The Thales OTCEP deck could not be obtained.

## Running Glossary

- **AMP:** DNP3 authorisation protocol.
- **BRSKI:** zero-touch bootstrap.
- **CBOM:** cryptographic bill of materials.
- **CMP/EST/SCEP:** certificate enrolment protocols.
- **CRQC:** cryptanalytically relevant quantum computer.
- **Deprecated/Disallowed:** NIST statuses meaning "usable with risk" vs "not allowed".
- **DICE:** measurement-derived device identity.
- **FO transform:** CCA conversion with a re-encryption check.
- **GDOI:** group key distribution.
- **GOOSE/SV:** IEC 61850 multicast messages.
- **HNDL:** harvest now, decrypt later.
- **HNDL-to-authority:** harvesting the transport of long-lived authority keys.
- **Hold-down:** waiting period before a new anchor is trusted.
- **IDevID:** factory device identity certificate.
- **KEM:** key encapsulation mechanism.
- **KMC:** ETCS key management centre.
- **LMS/XMSS:** stateful hash-based signatures.
- **Logical qubit:** an error-corrected qubit.
- **Magic state:** distilled non-Clifford resource.
- **Module-LWE/SIS:** lattice hardness assumptions.
- **NTT:** number-theoretic transform.
- **OTP/eFuse:** one-time-programmable memory.
- **Partial-verification ECU:** an Uptane device that skips Root metadata.
- **Rejection sampling:** aborting signatures that would leak the key.
- **SecOC:** AUTOSAR message authentication.
- **SEooC:** safety element out of context.
- **Surface code:** 2D quantum error-correcting code.
- **TUF/Uptane:** thresholded secure-update frameworks.
- **Winternitz W:** the size/speed trade-off parameter.

I've published **Post-Quantum Trust Lab**: 51 step-through and interactive scenes covering the OT and embedded post-quantum research. It's private to you until you share it. I tested every scene in a headless browser (no render errors), but I haven't looked at the layout in a real browser, so check how it looks on your device.

Each scene has a tag for how far to trust its numbers: published spec, cited measurement, computed in the page, illustrative, constructed estimate, proposed design or hypothesis. The page runs 20 live checks on its own figures in the last scene, and all 20 pass. I also re-computed the main sizes, frame counts, toy examples and arithmetic separately in Python.

The scenes run from foundations to future:
- **Foundations:** a qubit, Shor's algorithm, Diffie–Hellman, lattice maths, ML-KEM, ML-DSA, hash-based signatures.
- **Hardware and boot:** the microcontroller, secure boot, flash and RAM limits, side channels, fault injection.
- **Protocols:** the OT protocol survey, the real-time budget, hybrid TLS, IKE, tiny radios.
- **Roots of trust:** root succession, pre-commitment, offline devices, DICE, anti-rollback counters.
- **Real settings:** the Purdue model, a substation relay, remote assets, sectors.
- **Proposals and economics:** the proposed designs, a fleet simulator, the hypothesis board, timelines and market arithmetic.

Things to know before relying on it:
- **Not photoreal:** the visuals are 2D engineering schematics, plus one isometric exploded microcontroller. They show the mechanisms and the real arithmetic, not circuit-level behaviour.
- **Mismatches with the research report, shown on-page:**
  - The masking overhead appears as both 3.5× and 4.2×, and only 4.2× reproduces from the quoted cycle counts.
  - The US market low end is $0.11B when recomputed, against $0.13B in the report.
  - A simple surface-code qubit count exceeds Google's quoted physical-qubit figure.
  - IKE's first exchange can't be fragmented, which corrects the earlier text.
  - TLS 1.3 downgrade protection is only as strong as the handshake's own primitives.
  - I also caught and fixed an arithmetic error of mine (a root-metadata size) via the live checks.
- **Not re-measured:** I took the published benchmarks, vendor ROM capabilities, quantum resource estimates, acquisition values and some regulatory dates as reported and labelled them as such. Two items rest on my memory and should be confirmed: the IANA group codepoints and the ML-DSA average-attempts figure. The 1–5 scores in the architecture comparison are my own judgement.
- **Proposals are labelled:** the proposed designs and hypotheses are shown as hypotheses, not validated products. The hypothesis board lists only the 14 ideas visualised, not all 42, and omits the audit's per-idea verdicts.

# MISSION 3: THE SOLUTION FORGE
## A Radical Systems-Invention Programme for OT & Embedded Post-Quantum Security

### THE GRAND CHALLENGE

I am providing you with the latest research report:

**"Post-Quantum Migration for OT and Embedded Infrastructure, Re-examined: Audit, Foundations and a Revised Thesis."**

This report represents our current understanding of the domain. It explores the technical foundations, quantum threats, cryptographic algorithms, embedded hardware, industrial protocols, migration barriers, root-of-trust architectures, existing solutions, commercial opportunities and 42 innovation hypotheses.

But I believe we have only scratched the surface.

Now I want to move beyond understanding the problem and begin inventing its solutions.

I want you to conduct an extremely deep, multidisciplinary research and invention exercise to discover **what we could build to fundamentally solve, eliminate, bypass or radically improve the problems throughout the entire OT and embedded post-quantum security ecosystem.**

Do not approach this as another research report. Approach it as a combination of an advanced technology invention laboratory, a multidisciplinary research institute, an elite engineering design team, a scientific discovery programme and a frontier-technology startup laboratory.

Think like the combined intelligence of:

- A world-class cryptographer.
- A quantum computing researcher.
- A semiconductor and computer architecture engineer.
- An embedded systems architect.
- An industrial automation and OT specialist.
- A distributed-systems researcher.
- A mathematician and information theorist.
- A hardware security researcher.
- A formal verification specialist.
- A physicist.
- A systems engineer.
- A strategic technology inventor.
- A venture builder who understands how to turn a difficult technical discovery into an essential global product.

I want you to challenge the existing industry, question the foundations of its architectures, find hidden relationships between seemingly unrelated problems and explore solutions that are not currently part of conventional thinking.

Do not simply optimise the existing system.

**Imagine that we have the opportunity to redesign how industrial infrastructure establishes, maintains, transfers and renews cryptographic trust for the next 50 years. What would we create?**

This is not permission to invent fictional science or ignore fundamental constraints. It is a requirement to explore the full design space, including ideas that are unfamiliar, unconventional, technically ambitious or not yet demonstrated.

The ambition should be extraordinary. The technical discipline should be equally extraordinary.

---

# STAGE 1 — REBUILD THE PROBLEM UNIVERSE BEFORE INVENTING SOLUTIONS

First, carefully examine the entire attached report.

Review the previous research, including:

- The original problem analysis.
- The technical foundations.
- The industrial ecosystem.
- Embedded hardware constraints.
- Protocol limitations.
- Cryptographic migration mechanisms.
- Root-of-trust architectures.
- Existing prior art.
- The H1–H42 innovation hypotheses.
- The commercial analysis.
- The unanswered technical questions.

Do not simply accept its findings.

Independent verification is required. The attachment contains a mixture of established facts, measured benchmarks, constructed estimates, interpretations, hypotheses and predictions. Distinguish between them.

Re-examine the entire domain and construct a new, more comprehensive problem universe.

Look for problems at every level:

**Mathematical and cryptographic:** Cryptographic assumptions, key establishment, signatures, algorithm diversity, state management, hybrid security, randomness, side channels and future cryptographic transitions.

**Silicon and hardware:** Immutable ROM, OTP/eFuse limitations, secure elements, TPMs, microcontrollers, hardware roots of trust, physical access, fault resistance, memory, processing and hardware lifecycle.

**Firmware and software:** Bootloaders, firmware verification, signed configurations, engineering project files, secure updates, software supply chains, cryptographic libraries, parsers and software dependencies.

**Embedded infrastructure:** Resource-constrained devices, disconnected devices, devices without reliable clocks, weak processors, inaccessible equipment, devices that cannot receive updates and equipment that cannot be replaced economically.

**Communication and networks:** Industrial protocols, gateways, key distribution, remote access, bandwidth, latency, fragmentation, interoperability and trust relationships.

**Fleet and lifecycle management:** Thousands of devices, multiple manufacturers, different hardware generations, expired certificates, orphaned products, manufacturer exits, spare equipment, recovery and long-term maintenance.

**Safety and certification:** Functional safety, safety cases, recertification, watchdog constraints, availability, fail-safe behaviour and hardware-software isolation.

**Organisational and economic:** Vendor lock-in, procurement cycles, incompatible incentives, responsibility gaps, standards fragmentation, customer adoption and total cost of ownership.

Also investigate areas that have not received meaningful attention in the previous work.

For every problem, determine:

1. What is the exact problem?
2. What causes it at the deepest technical level?
3. Why does the current architecture produce it?
4. What other problems does it create?
5. Which existing solutions address it?
6. Why do those solutions remain insufficient?
7. What assumptions prevent alternative solutions?
8. What would happen if the problem were completely eliminated?

Build a comprehensive problem graph.

Do not assume that every visible problem requires a separate product. Several problems may have one underlying cause. One architectural change might eliminate multiple difficulties simultaneously.

**Find the smallest number of fundamental causes that explain the largest number of difficulties.**

# STAGE 2 — DISCOVER THE HIDDEN STRUCTURE OF THE ENTIRE SYSTEM

I want you to model this domain as a complex interconnected system.

Do not look at cryptographic algorithms, embedded devices, industrial protocols and security management as isolated subjects.

Understand how information, identity, authority, trust, commands, firmware, configuration, keys and verification move through the complete system.

Build maps of:

- Cryptographic authority.
- Device identity.
- Trust dependencies.
- Firmware and configuration supply chains.
- Key and certificate lifecycles.
- Industrial communication paths.
- Security boundaries.
- Hardware dependencies.
- Failure propagation.
- Stakeholder responsibilities.
- Operational and economic incentives.

Use multiple systems-analysis approaches:

**Causal loop analysis:** Identify reinforcing and balancing feedback loops.

**Dependency graph analysis:** Discover components whose failure prevents the entire migration process.

**Network and graph theory:** Identify central nodes, single points of failure, bottlenecks and alternative paths.

**Reliability engineering:** Investigate failure modes, fault tolerance, recovery and graceful degradation.

**Control theory:** Explore whether cryptographic trust and device security can be continuously controlled through feedback mechanisms.

**Information theory:** Investigate information requirements, entropy, key distribution, communication efficiency and fundamental limits.

**Lifecycle systems engineering:** Understand how security decisions made during chip design influence decades of operation.

**Economic systems thinking:** Understand why participants fail to coordinate even when all recognise the problem.

A leverage point is a place where a relatively small change could eliminate several problems, unlock a new architecture or significantly reduce the cost and complexity of migration.

For every leverage point, explain what changes if we intervene there.

Ask:

- What is the actual bottleneck?
- What is merely a symptom?
- Which components could be eliminated?
- Which responsibilities could be redistributed?
- Which operations could be moved to another layer?
- Which trust relationships could be simplified?
- What would happen if we redesigned the system around a completely different principle?

# STAGE 3 — RUN MULTIPLE INDEPENDENT INVENTION ENGINES

This is the core of the mission.

I do not want you to perform one brainstorming session and present its results.

I want multiple independent invention processes, each using a different approach to discovering solutions.

Run them separately before combining their outputs.

Do not simply describe these frameworks. Apply them rigorously to the actual problems discovered in Stage 1.

## ENGINE A — FIRST-PRINCIPLES REINVENTION

Strip away the current industry architectures.

Temporarily forget PKI, conventional secure boot, existing certificate infrastructure, current firmware-signing approaches and the usual assumptions about industrial trust.

Start only with the fundamental requirements.

A device must know what it can trust. It must establish authority. It must reject unauthorised changes. It must communicate securely. It must survive failures. It must potentially evolve across several generations of cryptographic technology.

Now independently design solutions from the ground up.

- We designed it today without inheriting legacy cryptographic architectures.
- We could change the hardware-software boundary.
- We could introduce entirely new trust primitives.

## ENGINE B — TRIZ, CONTRADICTION AND INVENTIVE PRINCIPLES

Identify the technical contradictions throughout the domain.

For example:

- A secure root should be immutable, but cryptographic technology must evolve.
- A disconnected device must remain secure, but it cannot receive revocation information.
- Strong signatures require larger data structures, but embedded hardware has limited memory.
- Frequent rotation improves security, but industrial systems cannot tolerate disruption.
- Centralised trust simplifies governance, but concentrates risk.
- Hardware verification improves security, but changing silicon takes years.
- Safety certification promotes reliability, but security updates can trigger costly reassessment.

Use contradiction analysis and TRIZ-inspired inventive principles to find solutions.

## ENGINE C — MORPHOLOGICAL DESIGN SPACE EXPLORATION

Break the entire system into independent design dimensions.

For example:

- Location of the trust anchor.
- Location of cryptographic computation.
- Type of trust primitive.
- Authority distribution.

- Revocation mechanism.

- Offline operation model.

## ENGINE D — INVERSION AND ADVERSARIAL RECONSTRUCTION

Imagine the existing security assumptions have failed.

Consider situations in which:

- The original OEM disappears.
- All classical signing keys are compromised.
- A quantum algorithm breaks a cryptographic family earlier than expected.
- A device remains offline for a decade.
- Its trusted clock is incorrect.
- The device cannot process new cryptographic algorithms.
- The device's secure element cannot be updated.
- The gateway becomes compromised.
- A signing authority is unavailable.
- An authorised organisation becomes malicious.

For each situation, invent defensive architectures that preserve security and operational continuity.

Investigate alternative paths, independent verification, bounded trust, recovery mechanisms, quorum designs, hardware diversity and other possibilities.

## ENGINE E — CROSS-DOMAIN TECHNOLOGY TRANSFER

Investigate how other industries and scientific disciplines solve analogous problems.

This is particularly important.

Look at:

- Aerospace fault-tolerant computing.
- Satellite systems and long-duration disconnected operation.
- Automotive secure-update architectures.

- TUF and Uptane.

Look for the underlying principles rather than copying existing products.

Ask:

What lessons from spacecraft that operate without human intervention for years could help protect disconnected industrial devices?

What can distributed systems teach us about authority renewal?

Can error-correcting mechanisms inspire more resilient trust systems?

Can evolutionary or redundancy-based principles help infrastructure survive cryptographic obsolescence?

Explore connections that are scientifically and technically meaningful.

Generate new solutions using these transfers.

## ENGINE F — CONSTRAINT REMOVAL AND ARCHITECTURAL LIBERATION

For each major problem, temporarily remove one constraint and investigate what becomes possible.

Examples:

- What if the device had almost unlimited memory?
- What if communication bandwidth were free?
- What if every device could perform PQC efficiently?
- What if all devices were offline?

## ENGINE G — MATHEMATICAL AND PHYSICAL INSPIRATION

**3. Cryptographic self-awareness:** Could each device maintain an understandable, continuously updated representation of its own trust state, algorithms, dependencies and vulnerabilities?

**4. Distributed trust without a permanent central authority:** Could industrial infrastructure establish and renew trust across several independent organisations without relying on one manufacturer?

**5. Offline-first security:** Could an industrial device safely manage identity, authority changes, revocation and recovery without depending on continuous Internet connectivity or reliable wall-clock time?

**6. Cryptographic abstraction:** Could industrial applications become completely independent of the underlying cryptographic algorithms?

**7. Autonomous migration:** Could an infrastructure layer discover cryptographic dependencies, simulate upgrades, prepare migration plans, verify compatibility and safely coordinate transitions?

**8. Security designed around failure:** Could we develop architectures where a compromised manufacturer, gateway, signing system or cryptographic family does not destroy the entire trust ecosystem?

**9. New physical trust mechanisms:** Could new silicon architectures, hardware primitives or physically rooted verification methods provide capabilities not achievable through conventional software?

**10. Infrastructure-level cryptographic resilience:** Could we design an entire industrial ecosystem to survive several cryptographic generations, supplier failures and future scientific breakthroughs?

# RESEARCH AND REASONING PROTOCOL

Follow this process rigorously.

### 1. Independent solution generation

Generate ideas independently using each invention engine before combining them. This reduces the likelihood that one early concept dominates the investigation.

### 2. Search before claiming novelty

Investigate whether similar systems, patents, research papers or products already exist.

### 3. Multiple solution paths

Never assume there is one correct answer. Generate different approaches for every major problem.

### 4. Cross-pollination

Combine the strongest ideas from different disciplines and invention methods.

### 5. Adversarial review

Challenge every major invention and identify what could make it fail.

### 6. Iterative refinement

Take the strongest ideas through multiple rounds:

- Initial concept.
- Technical examination.
- Contradiction analysis.
- Alternative architecture.
- Adversarial criticism.
- Redesign.
- Feasibility assessment.
- Prototype proposal.

Do not accept the first version as the final solution.

### 7. Evidence discipline

Clearly separate:

- Established scientific facts.
- Existing commercial capabilities.
- Demonstrated engineering results.
- Unverified vendor claims.
- Technical estimates.
- Expert judgment.
- Your own analytical inferences.
- Original invention hypotheses.
- Speculative future possibilities.

### 8. Explicit uncertainty

Where an idea depends on an unproven assumption, identify it. Where fundamental constraints make something impossible under stated conditions, explain why.

### 9. Avoid generic AI output

Do not fill pages with standard phrases such as "AI-powered platform", "blockchain-based security", "intelligent monitoring" or "unified dashboard".

Every proposal must identify a real mechanism and explain exactly what changes technically.

### 10. Continuous self-evaluation

At the end of each major stage, identify what remains unexplored and what the next stage should investigate.

Do not expose private internal reasoning. Instead, provide the research evidence, structured analysis, competing hypotheses, technical rationale and conclusions needed to evaluate your work.

I've finished the report on post-quantum security for OT and embedded devices. Its main conclusion is that migrating to new algorithms isn't the core problem. The real problem is that industrial devices can't accept a new authority when the algorithm family, the owner or the trust epoch changes. Seven root causes explain almost all of the 35 problems I mapped. The four that matter most are trust anchors locked to one algorithm family in silicon, validity that depends on wall-clock time, authority tied to the manufacturer alone, and public-key work forced onto every device.

The top-ranked inventions, scored out of 100:
- **Multi-family root commitment (84):** a 32-byte commitment stored in one-time-programmable memory, covering keys from several algorithm families, plus a one-way "classical sunset" switch and an escrow key.
- **Symmetric-first operation (80):** devices use symmetric keys day to day and only use post-quantum public-key crypto (ML-KEM) once, at enrolment.
- **Hash-only boot verifier (79):** a minimal ROM verifier that needs only hash functions, using about 1 KB of stack.
- **Counter-based validity (77):** expiry based on counters and epochs instead of time, for devices without reliable clocks.
- **Threshold succession escrow (77):** authority shared across organisations so a fleet survives the manufacturer going away.

Signing-key custody for hash-based signatures (LMS) ranks only 16th (65). HSM vendors are likely to bundle it, and NIST's planned revision of SP 800-208 would weaken its value further.

There is one hard limit. A device whose immutable verifier only understands classical algorithms, and which stays offline until a quantum computer can break them, can't be saved by cryptography. Only physical access, network isolation or replacement helps. So every device shipped from now on should carry a post-quantum-capable commitment.

The report also includes:
- a weighted ranking of 20 inventions;
- 11 architectures;
- blueprints;
- 10 lab experiment plans, starting with power-loss testing on a Cortex-M33 board and a TLA+ model of succession;
- a 0–36-month roadmap.

Two caveats on the evidence:
- The report rests on about 60 sources, not the 500–600 you invited. I put verification effort into the claims the conclusions depend on.
- Vendor claims about post-quantum ROM support (Microchip, NXP, ST) haven't been checked at parameter level.

**The Invention Atlas: Authority Continuity for OT and Embedded Post-Quantum Security**

# The Invention Atlas: Authority Continuity for OT and Embedded Post-Quantum Security

Post-quantum migration is not itself the root problem. The root problem is that industrial devices cannot accept a new authority under the failures they actually face: a new algorithm family, a new owner, or a new epoch of trust. The highest-leverage invention is a small authority-continuity kernel committed into silicon at manufacture. It uses only hash functions, commits to several algorithm families, and is paired with symmetric-first operation and threshold succession. A new PKI or a migration SaaS would be lower leverage.

## TL;DR

- **The root causes.** Seven root causes explain almost all of the 35 named problems. Four dominate:
  - trust anchors are bound to one algorithm family in immutable silicon;
  - validity depends on wall-clock time that offline devices lack;
  - authority is bound to one organisation (the OEM), with no succession;
  - public-key operations are pushed into every endpoint even where symmetric cryptography suffices.
  Fixing these four removes most migration complexity rather than managing it.
- **The best inventions.** The weighted ranking (scores out of 100) puts four ideas on top:
  - a multi-family root manifest pre-committed in OTP (84);
  - symmetric-first operation with ML-KEM-only enrolment (80);
  - a minimal ROM verifier that uses only hash functions (79);
  - time-free epoch/counter validity, tied with threshold succession escrow for OEM exit (77 each).
  LMS custody, the Mission 2 favourite, ranks 16th (65). It is useful but commoditising. NIST SP 800-208 currently requires signing in non-exporting hardware modules at FIPS 140 Level 3 or higher, so HSM vendors will bundle custody. NIST's CSRC project page also says it is "working on a revision of SP 800-208 that would enable key export, but also mitigate security concerns", which would erode the custody moat further.
- **The hard boundary.** Some devices cannot be rescued by cryptography: those whose immutable verifier speaks only a classical family and that stay offline until a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC) exists. Only physical presence, network compensation or replacement helps them. So every device shipped from now on should carry a PQ-capable, multi-family commitment. The first experiments are:
  - a hash-only first stage plus a multi-family manifest on a Cortex-M33 board, tested under power-loss fuzzing;
  - a TLA+ model of threshold succession across OEM exit.

## Evidence legend and research process log

Labels used throughout:

| Label | Meaning |
|---|---|
| [F] | Fact or primary-source statement |
| [V] | Unverified vendor claim |
| [D] | Demonstrated engineering result |
| [E] | Estimate, with arithmetic shown |
| [I] | Own inference |
| [H] | Invention hypothesis |
| [S] | Speculation |

**Step 1 — Re-verify Mission 2's foundations.**
- [F] NIST IR 8547 final was published on 27 October 2026. It deprecates all quantum-vulnerable public-key algorithms after 2030, regardless of key size, and disallows them after 2035. NIST's Andrew Regenscheid said the 2035 disallowance was not intended to apply to hybrid modes.
- [F] OMB M-26-15 implements the 22 June 2026 Executive Order "Securing the Nation Against Advanced Cryptographic Attacks". It sets a 2031 phase for migrating signatures on high-value assets, and agencies must submit PQC Migration Plans within 120 days. I did not independently verify the EO number "14412" that Mission 2 cited.
- [F] Gidney (arXiv 2505.15917, 21 May 2025): RSA-2048 can be factored in under a week with fewer than one million noisy qubits. This assumes "a gate error rate of 0.1%, a surface code cycle time of 1 microsecond, and a control system reaction time of 10 microseconds". The 2019 Gidney+Ekerå estimate was 20 million qubits.
- [F] Google Quantum AI (30 March 2026): ECDLP-256 needs ≤1,200 logical qubits with 90M Toffolis, or ≤1,450 with 70M. This runs on fewer than 500,000 physical qubits in minutes.
- [F] Alice & Bob (IACR ePrint 2026/2665; press release 29 October 2026):
  - RSA-2048 needs 22,593 physical cat qubits for 3.14 days. LDPC-cat codes "more than halve the qubit count, from 49,649 to 22,593".
  - The same paper gives ≤29,588 cat qubits for 61.8 minutes for ECDLP-256.
  - The headline figures "assume no bit flips during the computation". With bit flips about once an hour, "RSA 2048 needs about 45,000 qubits".
  - Marin Ivezic (PostQuantum.com) notes that the demonstrated error correction "is a distance-3 repetition code against phase flips on three data qubits, not yet below threshold".
- [D] On the RP2040, ML-DSA-44 signing has a p99 of 584.2 ms and a maximum of 1,156.8 ms over 10,000 signatures (arXiv 2603.19340).
- Corrections to Mission 2:
  - The market components sum to $0.11B, not $0.13B.
  - Post-quantum IKEv2 needs IKE_INTERMEDIATE (RFC 9242) plus RFC 9370.

**Step 2 — Check what root-of-trust silicon can actually verify.** This turned out to be the decisive discovery.
- [F] TI's AM263P4 ROM cannot verify PQC. TI plans to put it in a secondary bootloader instead.
- [F] OpenTitan's ROM verifies ECDSA P-256 plus optional SPHINCS+-128s. RFC #30765 flags this as non-compliant with CNSA 2.0.
- [V] Vendors claim the following, unverified at parameter level:
  - Microchip MEC175xB: "embedded immutable post-quantum cryptography", supporting ML-KEM, ML-DSA and LMS.
  - NXP MCX A5: PQC hybrid secure boot in ROM.
  - ST STM32G5 (announced 3 November 2026): a hardware core accelerating LMS, ML-KEM, ML-DSA and SLH-DSA.

**Step 3 — Look at how other domains handle the same problem.**
- [F] Microsoft Support (KB5062710) lists the UEFI Secure Boot 2011 expiries:
  - Microsoft Corporation KEK CA 2011 on 24 June 2026;
  - Microsoft UEFI CA 2011 on 27 June 2026;
  - Microsoft Windows Production PCA 2011 on 19 October 2026.
  The 2023 CAs replace them. Devices without the 2023 certificates still boot, but can no longer receive boot-level security updates.
- [F] CCSDS SDLS Extended Procedures use only symmetric master keys.
- [F] Roughtime became RFC 10049 (Experimental, October 2026).
- [F] Threshold ML-DSA became practical for small groups during 2025–2026.

**Step 4 — Prior-art checks.** I checked:
- Intel patents US 11405213 and US 12137169 (fast XMSS/LMS verification for secure boot);
- the OCP Hardware Secure Boot specification, which already allows a "table of hashes" of public keys in eFuses;
- KEMTLS-PDK;
- the Aquaman transparent PQ proxy.

These downgraded several "novel" ideas to "existing technology, new application".

**Step 5 — Subagent gap-fill on hash-based verification cost.**
- [D] Campos et al. (AFRICACRYPT 2020): LMS verification (SHA-256, h=10, w=16) takes 2,658,884 cycles on a Cortex-M4 and about 1,044 B of stack. XMSS takes 13.07M cycles.
- [D] Banegas et al. (arXiv 2106.05577): LMS verification takes 123 ms on an nRF52840 at 64 MHz, using 1,580 B of stack.

These figures made the hash-only verifier the strongest silicon-level idea.

**Scope note.** This work rests on about 60 sources (18 searches, several fetches, one subagent), not the 500–600 sources invited. I prioritised verifying the claims the conclusions depend on. Section 13 lists where more sourcing would most change the conclusions.

## 1. The complete problem universe

**Mathematical and cryptographic**
- **P1 — Algorithm-family monoculture in anchors.** Hybrid signatures and agile libraries do not help at the immutable layer [I].
- **P2 — HNDL-to-authority.** Once classical keys can be forged, an attacker can inject a fake "successor root" into any device that has not yet moved to a PQ root. This problem has a hard deadline [I].
- **P3 — LMS/XMSS state reuse.** [F] SP 800-208 requires signing in non-exporting hardware modules at FIPS 140 Level 3 or higher, and calls these schemes "not suitable for general use". NIST says it is working on a revision that "would enable key export, but also mitigate security concerns".
- **P4 — ML-DSA rejection-sampling latency and leakage.** [D] The RP2040 study recovered iteration counts from wall-clock time alone. The authors claim no key recovery, but warn that the history of attacks on BLISS counsels caution.
- **P5 — Hybrid downgrade.** [F] CVE-2026-2673: OpenSSL TLS 1.3 servers using DEFAULT may negotiate a weaker group than configured.
- **P6 — Side-channel certification of PQ hardware.** [F] Independent analyses (Saarinen; Karabulut and Azarderakhsh) found leakage in the masking of the first Adams Bridge ML-DSA accelerator.
- **P7 — Certified entropy on cold-booting MCUs.**

**Silicon and hardware**
- **P8 — Immutable ROM verifiers that cannot learn new families.**
- **P9 — Scarce OTP that competes between revocation and anti-rollback.** [F] OCP says key revocation "shall not be mixed with anti-rollback revocation OTP".
- **P10 — RAM ceilings.** [D] In reference C, ML-DSA-44 signing needs 50.6 KB of stack, ML-DSA-87 needs 119.6 KB, and ML-KEM-512 decapsulation needs 9.4 KB.
- **P11 — Silicon generations last 3–5 years; assets last 20–40 years.**

**Firmware and software**
- **P12 — Single-format boot chains.** An ML-DSA-87 header grows by 2,592 + 4,627 = 7,219 B.
- **P13 — Engineering project files and configurations are unsigned,** and the authority to change them sits on engineering workstations.
- **P14 — Each vendor's crypto library has its own PQ readiness.**

**Embedded infrastructure**
- **P15 — Devices without trustworthy time.**
- **P16 — Devices that can never be updated.**
- **P17 — Weak processors.** [D] On an RP2040, ECDSA P-256 verification takes 321 ms, versus 44.0 ms for ML-DSA-44. PQ verification is often cheaper than classical; signing and RAM are the real constraints.

**Communication**
- **P18 — MTU fragmentation.** An X25519MLKEM768 ClientHello exceeds 1,500 B. On LoRaWAN EU868, an ML-KEM-768 key needs 24 frames.
- **P19 — Process-bus frames must stay symmetric.** A Sampled Values frame leaves only 208–250 µs.
- **P20 — Group keys.** [F] IEC 62351-9:2023 distributes GOOSE/SV/PTP group keys via GDOI. It discusses post-quantum crypto in Annex B.11 but mandates no PQ mechanism.
- **P21 — OPC UA has no PQ security policy yet.** This comes from a practitioner source and is unconfirmed.

**Fleet and lifecycle**
- **P22 — Catch-up cost.** In a TUF-style scheme, each rotation of an ML-DSA-65 root (5 keys, threshold 3) costs about 29.8 KB [E].
- **P23 — Orphaned fleets when the OEM exits.**
- **P24 — Spares sitting on shelves with stale roots.**
- **P25 — Backups that restore old crypto state.**
- **P26 — Revocation on offline devices.**
- **P27 — Certificate expiry as an authority cliff.** [F] Microsoft-trusting devices left without the 2023 certificates "will no longer be able to receive new security protections for the early boot process".

**Safety and certification**
- **P28 — Crypto changes trigger safety recertification.**
- **P29 — Watchdog conflicts.** A signing p99 of 584 ms is far beyond a 100 ms deadline.
- **P30 — Fail-safe versus fail-secure at boot.**

**Organisational and economic**
- **P31 — A responsibility gap between OEM, integrator and operator.**
- **P32 — Regulatory clocks.** [F] Under the EU CRA, reporting obligations apply from 11 September 2026 and full obligations from 11 December 2027. Support periods must be at least five years, and updates must remain available for ten. Products placed on the market before December 2027 stay outside most of the law.
- **P33 — No standard of evidence that migration is complete.**
- **P34 — A small standalone market.** Mission 2's estimate, corrected: $0.4–1.9B globally [E].
- **P35 — Devices have no machine-readable self-description of their cryptography.**

**Root-cause compression**

| Root cause | Problems explained |
|---|---|
| R1 Algorithm-bound anchors in immutable silicon | P1, P2, P8, P9, P11, P12, P16 |
| R2 Time-dependent validity | P15, P22, P24, P26, P27 |
| R3 Organisation-bound authority | P13, P23, P27, P31 |
| R4 Public-key work at every endpoint | P4, P10, P17, P18, P19, P20 |
| R5 Crypto co-located with safety | P28, P29, P30 |
| R6 Copyable or rewindable state | P3, P24, P25 |
| R7 Invisible state and misaligned incentives | P31, P32, P33, P34, P35 |

**Still unexplored:** outage cost per substation, and the fraction of fleets that can be updated remotely. No primary data was found for either.

## 2. The interconnected system and its hidden leverage points

**How authority flows [I].** Silicon ROM key → OEM root → release key → firmware → device identity → operator PKI/KDC → session keys → commands. Configuration authority bypasses this chain entirely through engineering workstations.

**Central nodes.** The central nodes are the ROM verifier, the OEM release key, the workstation, the KDC/GDOI server and the time sources. Of these, only the ROM verifier cannot be re-issued, so it is the true bottleneck. Certificate expiry, catch-up size and revocation latency are symptoms of it.

**Feedback loops.**
- Reinforcing — deferral: uncertainty leads to deferral, which grows the classical installed base, which raises cost, which reinforces deferral.
- Reinforcing — OEM exit: small OEMs cannot fund PQ roots, customers distrust them, and exit becomes more likely.
- Balancing — regulation (CRA, IR 8547).
- Missing — there is no feedback from device crypto state back to the operator (R7). An unobservable system cannot be controlled.

**Information-theoretic limits.**
- An offline device cannot tell "no update exists" from "updates are being withheld". A freeze attack is undetectable without an external freshness signal.
- Succession needs at least one authenticated channel that outlives the predecessor family. That is either a pre-committed key from an independent family or physical presence.
- A hash commitment to a whole key set costs 32 B of OTP.

**30 leverage points**
1. A hash-only ROM verifier.
2. OTP that commits to a Merkle root over several family roots.
3. A one-way "classical sunset" latch.
4. Counters and epochs instead of time.
5. Pre-rotation: commit the hash of the next key.
6. Threshold across organisations, not just across keys.
7. Public-key work at enrolment only.
8. A KDC with ML-KEM key transport.
9. A separate crypto core.
10. A workstation quorum for configuration signing.
11. Witness cosignatures shipped with images.
12. Device crypto self-description.
13. Passive CBOM from observed handshakes.
14. A pinned hybrid-strict policy.
15. Anti-rollback hold-down.
16. A physical-presence channel.
17. A spares activation ceremony.
18. Monotonic counters outside restorable storage.
19. LMS index reservation.
20. Capability-scoped signing keys.
21. Rate-limited authority.
22. A neutral escrow custodian.
23. Procurement clauses.
24. A regulator evidence format.
25. Gateway termination for unpatchable devices.
26. A GDOI-PQ profile.
27. KEMTLS-PDK.
28. Moving signing out of devices.
29. A migration digital twin.
30. Insurance pricing on attestations.

Leverage points 1, 2, 4 and 6 act on the bottlenecks. Points 7–30 mostly reduce cost or improve observability.

## 3. Outputs of the independent invention engines

**Engine A — First principles**
- A1, the "commitment-first device": the only immutable knowledge is a hash commitment to a set of authority descriptors [H].
- A2, the "symmetric-only endpoint": the endpoint holds only symmetric keys and a hash verifier, and an enrolment authority does all public-key work, as spacecraft SDLS does [H].
- A3, the "owner-sovereign device": the operator holds the root, and the OEM is one signer in a threshold [H].

**Engine B — TRIZ contradictions**

| Contradiction | Resolution |
|---|---|
| Immutable vs evolvable | Separate by abstraction: the immutable part is a hash plus a commitment ("preliminary action"). |
| Offline vs revocable | Separate in time: use counter-bounded authority and counter floors. |
| Large signatures vs small RAM | Stream LMS verification (about 1 KB stack) and move signing off-device. |
| Rotation vs availability | Make-before-break with hold-down, as in RFC 5011. |
| Central governance vs concentrated risk | Threshold across organisations. |
| Certification vs updates | Separate by partition: a crypto cell. |

**Engine C — Morphological design space.** Dimensions:
- anchor: ROM, OTP commitment, secure element or token;
- primitive: ECC, lattice, hash or MAC;
- authority: single, threshold of keys, or threshold of organisations;
- validity: time, counter, epoch or event;
- revocation: CRL, short-lived certificates, counter floor or rate limit;
- recovery: signed, physical presence or escrow.

The best unexplored combination is OTP commitment + hash verifier + threshold of organisations + counter validity + escrow, which became Architecture 1. Ledger authority plus time-based validity was rejected because of the liveness it demands.

**Engine D — Inversion**

| Failure | Defence |
|---|---|
| OEM disappears | Escrow plus threshold |
| All classical keys forged | Sunset latch, effective only if the device has already moved to PQ |
| Wrong clock | Counters |
| Gateway compromised | End-to-end authority |
| Power loss during renewal | A/B slots with commit-after-verify |
| Backup restore | Non-restorable counters |
| Malicious organisation | Threshold, rate limits and transparency |
| Isolated islands | Bounded-scope site quorum |

**Limit:** collusion by a full threshold of parties cannot be prevented, only detected afterwards.

**Engine E — Cross-domain transfer**
- Spacecraft SDLS-EP: symmetric master keys and over-the-air rekeying. The SDLS-DR proposal adds double-ratchet post-compromise recovery [F].
- DNSSEC RFC 5011: hold-down periods.
- UEFI's 2026 expiry: never tie device authority to an X.509 notAfter date.
- KERI: pre-rotation.
- Immune systems: diverse recognisers, i.e. multi-family verifiers.
- Error-correcting codes: k-of-n reconstruction.
- Certificate Transparency and Sigstore: witness cosigning, made usable offline.

**Engine F — Remove a constraint, then restore it**
- Unlimited memory: ship every family's verifier. Once memory is restored, only hash verifiers remain small enough.
- 100-year security: only hash-based and symmetric assumptions are conservative enough.
- No trusted party: a threshold of three or more independent organisations.
- Contact once every six months: size the epochs to the contact interval.

**Engine G — Mathematics and physics**
- Hash functions are the only primitive shared by every NIST PQ family (SHA-3/SHAKE in ML-KEM and ML-DSA; SHA-256 in LMS). That makes hash engines the universal accelerator.
- Verification is cheaper than signing for every scheme, so all signing should move off-device.
- PUFs store keys; they do not provide algorithm agility.
- Small anchor state machines can be formally verified in TLA+ or Tamarin.

**Engine H — Missing categories**
- An authority-continuity kernel.
- A trust-anchor escrow utility.
- A crypto-state attestation format.
- A migration rehearsal twin.

## 4. Multi-solution matrix

| Problem | Algorithm | Hardware | Software | Protocol/infra | Governance | Boundary |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P1/P8 ROM | Multi-family Merkle commitment | Hash-only ROM; PQ accelerator [V] | Second-stage PQ bootloader (wolfBoot LMS/XMSS/ML-DSA [F]) | Signed family-activation manifests | Procurement mandate | Classical-only ROM with no second stage cannot be fixed cryptographically |
| P2 HNDL-to-authority | Sunset latch | Monotonic fuse | Walk campaign tooling | Transparency of root changes | Deadline before a credible CRQC | Devices not yet moved: physical presence only |
| P15 time | Counter/epoch | Drift-bounded uptime counter | Max-staleness policy | Roughtime RFC 10049 | Contact SLAs | Freeze undetectable offline |
| P23 OEM exit | Threshold of organisations | Escrow leaf in OTP | Escrow release tooling | Custodian service | Contractual triggers; CRA support end | Escrow not committed at manufacture cannot be added without the OEM |
| P3/P25 state | SLH-DSA for roots | HSM index reservation | Persist-then-sign | Signer log | Clone/backup audits | Detect via transparency |
| P10/P17 weak devices | Symmetric-first; KEM-only | Hash accelerator | Stack-reduced libraries | GDOI-PQ, KEMTLS-PDK | Device-class policy | Class 0: hardware or gateway only |
| P22 catch-up | Pre-rotation chain | Metadata buffer | Skip lists | Checkpoints | Rotation caps | History must be retained |
| P28/P29 safety | Verify-only on device | Crypto core | Bounded-time scheduler | Offloaded signing | Separate security case | Shared-core devices need recertification |
| P35 invisibility | Signed crypto claims | Attestation key | CBOM agent | Passive CBOM | Evidence format | Silent devices: passive inference only |

**Comparisons**
- A second-stage bootloader is available now, but it inherits the classical ROM's HNDL exposure: whoever can forge the ROM signature can replace the second stage. Only a commitment at the ROM or OTP level closes that gap [I].
- A KDC wins on process buses; KEMTLS-PDK wins on point-to-point IP links.

## 7. Eleven architectural proposals

**Architecture 1 — Authority Continuity Kernel (ACK)**
- **Thesis:** the only immutable things are a hash engine and a 32-byte commitment to authorities in several families.
- **Components:**
  - a ROM SHA engine;
  - an LMS verifier using about 1 KB of stack [D];
  - an OTP Merkle root over {classical root, LMS root, ML-DSA root, escrow root, policy};
  - a monotonic sunset latch;
  - A/B anchor slots.
- **Flow:** the boot stage verifies an LMS-signed second stage, which carries the ML-DSA, hybrid and protocol stacks. Activating or retiring a family needs signatures from two committed families, or from the escrow leaf plus one family.
- **Failures:**
  - a lattice break leaves LMS plus escrow;
  - an LMS signer compromise leaves ML-DSA plus the threshold;
  - power loss is handled by the A/B slots.
- **Offline operation:** complete. The design uses no time.
- **Resources:**
  - [E] boot verification of 2.66M cycles is about 42 ms at 64 MHz and about 17 ms at 160 MHz;
  - [D] verifier code of 12,864 B of flash, including the library;
  - [E] 32 B of OTP plus a few fuses.
- **What is new:** each element has prior art (OCP hash tables, wolfBoot hybrid boot, OpenTitan SPHINCS+). What I found no prior art for is the combination in one commitment: cross-family activation rules, an escrow leaf and a sunset latch. That makes it a meaningful combination, not a patent-safe novelty.
- **Limitation:** native support needs new ROMs, roughly from 2028 onward [E].

**Architecture 2 — Symmetric-First Industrial Fabric**
- ML-KEM-768 enrolment delivers a key-encryption key. A GDOI-PQ KDC then distributes group and session keys. IEC 62351-6 MACs on the data path are unchanged.
- [D] ML-KEM-768 decapsulation takes 22.0 ms on a Cortex-M0+ with 14.2 KB of stack, once per enrolment.
- **Risk:** the KDC is central. It is replicated and needs a threshold to unlock.
- **Prior art:** CCSDS SDLS-EP; arXiv 2608.22123 (ML-KEM for Sampled Values).

**Architecture 3 — Threshold Succession Network**
- Fleet authority is k-of-n across the OEM, the operator and neutral custodians.
- **Conservative mode:** independent multi-signatures.
- **Advanced mode:** threshold ML-DSA, now practical for small groups [F]:
  - Celi et al.: up to 6 parties;
  - Mithril: USENIX Security 2026;
  - TALUS: one online round, 0.62–1.94 ms in its TEE variant.

**Architecture 4 — Time-Free Validity Layer**
- Authority objects carry epoch counters, not notAfter dates. Revocation raises a counter floor.
- Staleness restricts only privileged operations.
- Roughtime is an optional accelerator when connected.

**Architecture 5 — Pre-Rotation Catch-up Chain**
- Each root commits to SHA-256(next root).
- [E] Catching up after 10 missed rotations costs 10 × 32 B + 29.8 KB ≈ 30.1 KB, versus 10 × 29.8 KB = 298 KB for a sequential TUF walk. That is a ratio of about 0.10.

**Architecture 6 — Crypto Cell**
- A separate crypto core with its own "no interference" safety argument.
- The control core receives verified images and keys over a fixed-latency interface.

**Architecture 7 — Offline Transparency Bundle**
- Images ship with witness cosignatures, so offline devices can detect targeted malicious releases.

**Architecture 8 — Physical-Presence Authority Bridge**
- A sealed commissioning token plus on-site presence authorises a one-time move to a PQ second stage on classical-only devices.

**Architecture 9 — Crypto Passport and Observable Fleet**
- Devices sign a description of their families, roots, latch, counters and library versions. Silent devices are inferred passively.
- This turns migration into a control loop.

**Architecture 10 — Trust Renewal Utility**
- A managed bundle of custodian, escrow, threshold signer, checkpoint server and witness, for OEMs and orphan-fleet operators.

**Architecture 11 — Hash-Only 100-Year Device**
- Only hash-based signatures and symmetric keys. Enrolment happens through physical commissioning.

## 8. Red-team analysis and eliminated ideas

**Killed**

| Idea | Reason |
|---|---|
| Blockchain authority | Needs a liveness that offline devices lack; transparency logs give its benefits more cheaply. |
| QKD for field devices | Needs optical links, and gives no authentication without pre-shared keys. |
| Per-frame PQ signatures on process buses | 208–250 µs per frame. |
| TESLA on offline devices | Needs loose clock synchronisation. |
| VDF time evidence | Too costly, and depends on assumptions about attacker speed. |
| PUF as algorithm agility | A PUF stores keys; it cannot change algorithms. |
| Fully autonomous AI migration | Safety cases require human authorisation; kept only as rehearsal (L8). |

**Modified**
- **S20 ROM VM → S11, plug-ins running from flash.** This keeps the immutable attack surface small.
- **G2 custodian.** At least two custodians in different jurisdictions inside the threshold, so the custodian does not become a new single point of failure.
- **ACK.** ROM masks freeze 18–36 months before volume production [E]. So the commitment can instead live in OTP programmed at OEM provisioning, on existing ROMs that check a hash-of-key table, behind a first stage that uses only hash functions.
- **LMS custody.** HSM vendors will bundle reservation, so it is retained as a feature of the utility.

**Assumptions to test**
- Mainstream MCUs can host a 32-byte manifest root in OEM-provisioned OTP.
- Operators accept custodians.
- Assessors accept the crypto-cell argument.
- Hash-only first stages meet boot budgets on low-clock parts.

**Is replacement better?** Yes for Class 0 devices, and for classical-ROM assets with under about 10 years of remaining life: replace them, with gateway isolation in the interim [I].

| 19 | Anti-rollback hold-down | 2,5,3,3,4,4,2,3,2,2 | 63 | Medium | Delays urgent fixes |
| 20 | PQ gateway proxy | 3,5,2,1,5,4,4,2,1,2 | 63 | High | Prior art; last hop stays classical |

Check for rank 1: 15 + 12 + 15 + 6 + 8 + 8 + 8 + 5 + 3 + 4 = 84.

## 10. Engineering blueprints

**Blueprint 1 — ACK**

**A. What it is.** Silicon/firmware IP plus a provisioning specification.

**B. Architecture.**
- OTP holds `ACK_ROOT` (32 B), `SUNSET` (1 bit) and `FLOOR` (counter fuses).
- Each manifest leaf is `{family_id, pubkey_hash, activation_policy}`.
- The transitions are `ADD_FAMILY`, `RETIRE_FAMILY`, `SET_SUNSET`, `RAISE_FLOOR` and `ESCROW_RELEASE`. Each needs two active families, or the escrow leaf plus one family.

**C. Workflow.**
1. The OEM provisions `ACK_ROOT`.
2. Normal boot: LMS stage 1, then a hybrid ML-DSA+ECDSA application.
3. Before a CRQC, `SET_SUNSET` is signed by LMS + ML-DSA. After that, classical-only signatures are rejected permanently.
4. If lattices break, `RETIRE_FAMILY(ML-DSA)` is signed by LMS + escrow.

**D. Security.**
- No single-family compromise gives control.
- The latch is monotonic.
- Anchor updates are atomic under power loss.
- Validation: a TLA+ model plus latch fault injection.

**E. Resources.**
- [D] 2.66M cycles to verify.
- LMS SHA-256 (n=32, w=8, h=10) signatures are 1,452 B; w=4 gives 2,508 B. Computed from RFC 8554 as 4 + (4 + 32 + p·32) + 4 + 10·32, with p = 34 or 67.

**F. Roadmap.**
- Emulate now in a second stage (wolfBoot already supports LMS/XMSS/ML-DSA [F]).
- Build provisioning tools.
- ROM support from about 2028.
- An OCP/GlobalPlatform profile.

**H. Commercialisation.**
- Licence the kernel to MCU vendors and sell an OEM provisioning service.
- Competitors: Caliptra and OpenTitan, plus vendor ROMs [V].

**I. Defensibility.** Moderate. Standards participation and the reference implementation matter more than patents, given the OCP and Intel prior art.

**Blueprint 2 — Symmetric-First Fabric**
- **What it is:** a KDC/GDOI-PQ product plus a device SDK.
- **Key sizes:** ML-KEM-768 encapsulation key 1,184 B; ciphertext 1,088 B [F].
- **Recovery and islands:** missed rekeys are recovered through the counter floor; islands run KDC replicas.
- **Main risk:** compromise of the KDC. Mitigations are HSM-held key-encryption keys and scoping per substation.
- **Cost:** [V] wolfSSL reports 8.3 ms for ML-KEM-768 decapsulation on a newer STM32 (clock unspecified). The data path is unchanged.
- **First customers:** utilities already deploying IEC 62351-9. Standards route: IEC TC57 WG15.
- **Defensibility:** depth of integration with protection relays.

**Blueprint 3 — Threshold Succession and Custodian Utility**
- **Healthy OEM:** the OEM plus one other party signs.
- **On a trigger** (end of CRA support, or insolvency): the custodians plus the operator sign.
- **Metadata size:** about 3 × 3,309 B ≈ 9.9 KB using ML-DSA-65 multi-signatures [E].
- **Timeline:** contracts now, the protocol in 1–2 years, threshold ML-DSA once NIST's threshold call matures.
- **Pricing:** per device-year.

**Blueprints 4–7, in brief**
- **Time-free validity:** a library and metadata profile. Withheld updates remain undetectable offline, and the safety case must say so.
- **Pre-rotation:** a TUF/Uptane extension. Next keys are held offline under a threshold.
- **Crypto cell:** a reference board plus a safety template. The ML-DSA-44 signing p99 of 584 ms is why signing stays out of control partitions.
- **Passive CBOM:** a parser for OPC UA OpenSecureChannel policies, TLS/DTLS groups, IKE proposals and GDOI, producing CycloneDX CBOM plus signed passport claims.

## 12. Three future visions

**Vision 1 — Evolutionary (2027–2032).**
- Components:
  - PQ second-stage bootloaders on existing MCUs;
  - a symmetric-first fabric;
  - counter-based validity;
  - passive CBOM;
  - gateways for legacy devices;
  - escrow contracts tied to CRA support periods.
- **Weakness:** classical ROMs keep the HNDL-to-authority exposure until devices are moved to PQ.

**Vision 2 — Transformational (2028–2040).**
- Every new device carries an ACK.
- Fleet authority is a threshold across organisations, with custodians.
- Validity is time-free.
- Crypto passports are accepted as regulatory evidence.
- **Weakness:** governance and custodian liability.

**Vision 3 — Radical (2035 onward).**
- Trust renewal is a utility, like time service.
- Subfleets are deliberately diverse in algorithm.
- The longest-lived assets use hash-only devices.
- Applications name security properties, not algorithms.
- **Requires:** fleet-diversity theory, a property-level protocol layer and international custodian governance.

## 13. Biggest unanswered questions

1. Neutral, current benchmarks for LMS/XMSS verification on Cortex-M0+/M33 with hardware SHA. The best figures found date from 2020–2021.
2. Which shipping ROMs verify which PQ families, at parameter level [V].
3. Formal security definitions for cross-family activation rules.
4. Whether ML-DSA iteration-count leakage can aid key recovery.
5. Masking margins for PQ hardware.
6. Whether assessors accept crypto-cell partitioning under IEC 61508/62443.
7. How escrow works legally across jurisdictions.
8. What fraction of fleets can be updated remotely, and what outages cost.
9. PQ roadmaps for OPC UA, DNP3 and IEC 62351.
10. How realistic the falling quantum resource estimates are in hardware.
11. How secret a pre-rotated next key stays over long periods.
12. How middleboxes behave with large hybrid handshakes.

## 14. Prioritised roadmap

- **0–6 months:**
  - run E1, E2, E6 and E7;
  - publish an open ACK reference second stage and the TLA+ model;
  - draft an escrow contract template;
  - engage IEC TC57 WG15 on GDOI-PQ.
- **6–18 months:**
  - run E3, E4, E5 and E8;
  - pilot the symmetric-first fabric with one utility;
  - pilot the custodian with two small OEMs;
  - submit an ACK profile to OCP/GlobalPlatform.
- **18–36 months:**
  - ROM integration with a silicon partner;
  - threshold ML-DSA in the custodian service;
  - a regulator evidence format.
- **Continuously:** move every updatable classical-root device to PQ before any credible CRQC, starting with those that have the longest remaining life.

**Strategic verdict**
- **Defensible businesses:** the custodian/escrow utility and the symmetric-first fabric.
- **Standards and IP play:** the ACK, licensed to MCU vendors.
- **Features that incumbents will absorb:** passive CBOM, pinned hybrid policy, LMS custody and PQ gateways.

## Final coverage table

● = solves; ◐ = partially solves; ○ = does not address.

**The combination that solves the most.** Five inventions fully address R1–R4 and R7 and partly address R5 and R6:
1. the ACK manifest;
2. the symmetric-first fabric;
3. time-free validity;
4. threshold succession with a custodian;
5. crypto passport and passive CBOM.

Adding the crypto cell closes R5, and backup-aware state completes R6. This seven-part minimal portfolio is the recommended direction. It supersedes the original five-plane Industrial Trust Plane while absorbing its missing provisioning, time, revocation and governance layers.

## Beyond the Solution Forge: Discover What We Missed, Challenge What We Believe, and Find More Efficient Ways to Solve the Problem

# THE NEXT LEVEL OF THE INVESTIGATION

We have already conducted an extensive investigation into OT and embedded post-quantum security. We have examined the problem universe, explored multiple invention methodologies, generated unconventional solutions, combined technologies, proposed architectures, investigated commercial opportunities and considered the future of industrial cryptographic infrastructure.

But I do not want to stop there.

I want another level of investigation.

The previous work is a foundation, not a boundary. Treat the research and document already generated in this chat as the current body of work. Go through it carefully, including the earlier analysis, conclusions, technical proposals, architectural ideas, invention hypotheses, comparisons, unanswered questions and final recommendations.

Now challenge the entire body of work.

**What did we overlook? What did we misunderstand? What did we assume without sufficient evidence? What important connections did we fail to make? What solutions are hiding outside the boundaries of the way we have been thinking?**

Perhaps some of our proposed solutions are unnecessarily complicated. Perhaps we are trying to solve problems at the wrong layer. Perhaps a problem that appears to require a new cryptographic mechanism can be eliminated through a change in hardware architecture, operational procedure, network topology or trust governance.

Perhaps the most valuable invention is not another security product, but an entirely different way of organising the system.

I want you to approach this as a second, independent research expedition.

Do not merely expand the previous document by adding more pages, more technologies or more ideas. Re-examine the intellectual foundations of the work. Search for omissions, contradictions, incorrect priorities, hidden constraints, overlooked industries, unconventional mechanisms and substantially better approaches.

Think like a combination of:

- A scientist trying to discover what everyone else has overlooked.
- An engineer trying to eliminate unnecessary complexity.
- A cryptographer questioning the foundations of existing trust models.
- An industrial operator who has to keep real infrastructure running.
- A systems architect redesigning an ecosystem rather than a component.
- An adversary searching for the weakest assumptions.
- An economist investigating why technically superior solutions fail commercially.
- A researcher who is willing to reject an attractive theory when evidence contradicts it.
- An inventor looking for a completely different way to solve an established problem.

The objective is to go deeper, think differently, learn more and discover how to solve more of the problem with fewer dependencies, fewer moving parts, lower cost, better assurance and greater operational practicality.

Do not assume that the previous conclusions are correct simply because they are detailed.

**The previous work should be challenged with the same intensity with which it was created.**

# STAGE 1 — CONDUCT A COMPLETE INTELLECTUAL AUDIT OF THE PREVIOUS WORK

1. Problems already identified.
2. Root causes already proposed.
