4. Existing technologies and standards examined.
5. Proposed solutions.
6. Architectural proposals.
7. Technology combinations.
8. Commercial hypotheses.
9. Experiments and validation plans.
10. Questions that remain unanswered.
11. Areas where evidence is weak or incomplete.
12. Areas that received disproportionately little attention.

Create a traceability structure connecting:

**Problem → Root cause → Assumption → Proposed solution → Technical mechanism → Evidence → Unresolved risk.**

For every major conclusion, investigate whether the reasoning is actually strong.

Look for:

- Conclusions that rely on a single source.
- Claims that have not been independently verified.
- Assumptions that were treated as facts.
- Solutions that address symptoms rather than root causes.
- Technologies that were included because they are fashionable rather than necessary.
- Architectures that introduce more complexity than they remove.
- Opportunities that were identified without a convincing customer problem.
- Missing comparisons with simpler alternatives.
- Important dependencies that were not recognised.
- Areas where the solution is theoretically sound but practically difficult.
- Ideas that are technically interesting but unlikely to be adopted.
- Important research questions that were prematurely treated as solved.

Create a **Previous Work Challenge Register**.

Classify every important finding as:

- Strongly supported.
- Supported but incomplete.
- Plausible but not sufficiently validated.
- Based on a questionable assumption.
- In conflict with other evidence.
- Potentially incorrect.
- Missing a critical dimension.
- Worth expanding substantially.
- Should be discarded or redesigned.

Do not protect the previous work from criticism.

I would rather discover that a major part of our current direction is wrong than build a sophisticated solution around a flawed assumption.

At the same time, do not discard valid findings simply to appear unconventional. Preserve what survives scrutiny.

# STAGE 2 — SEARCH FOR THE PROBLEMS WE DO NOT KNOW WE HAVE

This is one of the most important parts of the mission.

The previous work explored a large problem universe, but that universe was necessarily shaped by the questions we initially asked.

Now investigate the domain from outside that framing.

Do not begin with the assumption that the known list of problems is complete.

Search for problems that are:

- Hidden behind existing terminology.
- Distributed across several organisations.
- Created by interactions between otherwise functioning components.
- Invisible during laboratory testing.
- Only encountered after years of deployment.
- Triggered by uncommon combinations of failures.
- Caused by procurement or governance rather than technology.
- Created by the transition between cryptographic generations.
- Created by the existence of multiple competing trust authorities.
- Not recognised as security problems by the organisations experiencing them.
- Economically ignored because their future cost is difficult to quantify.
- Solved informally through undocumented operational workarounds.

Investigate the **negative space** of the industry: the things that existing products, standards, research papers and vendor architectures rarely discuss.

Explore questions such as:

### A. The invisible lifecycle problems

What happens to the cryptographic identity of a device when its manufacturer disappears?

What happens when the people who originally provisioned the device leave the organisation?

What happens when a facility changes ownership?

What happens when an old device is moved to another plant with different trust authorities?

What happens when equipment is stored as spare inventory for years before deployment?

What happens when a replacement device is installed but inherits an inappropriate identity or security configuration?

What happens when a device is decommissioned but its certificates, credentials or signing relationships remain active?

What happens when an organisation cannot determine whether an old device is still trusted?

Investigate the complete lifecycle: design, manufacture, provisioning, shipment, installation, commissioning, operation, maintenance, repair, replacement, ownership transfer, incident recovery and decommissioning.

### B. The hidden state problems

Investigate what happens when devices disagree about:

- Current cryptographic epochs.
- Trusted authorities.
- Revocation information.
- Firmware versions.
- Configuration versions.
- Key generations.
- Validity policies.
- Security state.
- Recovery state.
- Accepted algorithm sets.

Explore problems involving rollback, stale state, replay, interrupted updates, inconsistent backups, conflicting authority changes and recovery from partially completed transitions.

Can a device be cryptographically valid according to one authority but operationally unacceptable according to another?

Can two individually secure components produce an insecure system because their state has diverged?

Can the system detect when its view of trust is incomplete?

### C. The human and organisational problems

Investigate the undocumented work performed by technicians, plant engineers, integrators, vendors, service teams and administrators.

What decisions are currently made manually because the software cannot make them safely?

What happens when the person with the necessary technical knowledge is unavailable?

Where does security depend on institutional memory rather than enforceable mechanisms?

Where do operational teams bypass security because the approved process is too difficult?

Where do procurement teams unintentionally create long-term security liabilities?

Where are responsibilities divided so poorly that no party owns the complete security lifecycle?

### D. The economic blind spots

Investigate hidden costs such as:

- Engineering labour.
- Site visits.
- Downtime.
- Recertification.
- Compatibility testing.
- Vendor support contracts.
- Long-term software maintenance.
- Spare-parts replacement.
- Hardware redesign.
- Operational training.
- Compliance evidence.
- Incident recovery.
- Maintaining obsolete infrastructure.

Which costs are currently assigned to the wrong organisation?

Which costs are not measured at all?

Which future liabilities are being transferred to operators without being explicitly acknowledged?

### E. The security problems created by security solutions

This deserves particular attention.

Can adding cryptographic agility create downgrade risks?

Can introducing more trust authorities increase the attack surface?

Can a central migration gateway become a critical dependency?

Can excessive monitoring create new attack surfaces?

Can automated cryptographic updates create dangerous failure modes?

Can stronger firmware verification create recovery problems?

Can hardware roots of trust create irreversible lifecycle dependencies?

Can hybrid cryptography create complexity that undermines the benefits it was meant to deliver?

Investigate how security mechanisms create second-order security and operational problems.

**Produce at least 50 additional problem hypotheses that were not adequately addressed in the previous work.** They must be meaningfully distinct, evidence-backed where possible, and clearly separated from previously identified problems.

Do not force the number. If fewer than 50 survive serious scrutiny, explain why. If you uncover substantially more, include them.

# STAGE 3 — CHANGE THE WAY WE LOOK AT THE PROBLEM

I want you to investigate the entire subject through perspectives that were not sufficiently developed in the previous exercise.

Do not merely assign different professional titles to the same conventional analysis. Each perspective should produce its own questions, problem definitions, solution possibilities and criticisms.

## Perspective 1 — The industrial operator

Ignore the architecture diagram for a moment.

Imagine you are responsible for an oil refinery, pipeline, power station, water treatment plant, railway system or manufacturing facility.

You have old equipment, multiple vendors, maintenance windows, safety requirements, disconnected networks and limited engineering staff.

You do not care whether a cryptographic architecture is elegant.

You care whether:

- The facility remains operational.
- A maintenance task can be completed.
- A device can be replaced safely.
- An emergency response works.
- A security update does not disrupt production.
- The system can be understood by the people responsible for it.
- The organisation is not permanently dependent on one vendor.

What would you demand from the technology?

What would make you reject it?

What capabilities would save you the most time and money?

What would you pay to never have to deal with again?

## Perspective 2 — The embedded engineer

Imagine you are implementing the technology inside a small microcontroller with limited RAM, flash, processing power, energy and connectivity.

Investigate what happens at the instruction, memory, hardware acceleration, boot-time and communication levels.

Identify opportunities involving:

- Smaller implementations.
- More efficient memory layouts.
- Incremental verification.
- Streaming verification.
- Precomputed values.
- Batching.
- Hardware acceleration.
- Algorithm-specific optimisation.
- Asynchronous processing.
- Reduced protocol overhead.
- Delegated operations.
- Reduced persistent state.
- More efficient error recovery.

Do not assume that every endpoint needs to implement every operation independently.

Investigate where computation could safely happen, where trust must remain local and where moving a function creates a new security boundary.

## Perspective 3 — The cryptographer

Challenge the cryptographic foundations.

Which security properties are truly necessary at each layer?

Which operations require asymmetric cryptography?

Which can use symmetric mechanisms?

Where are signatures necessary, and where would message authentication suffice?

Where does confidentiality actually matter?

Where is integrity more important than confidentiality?

Where is authenticity being confused with authorisation?

Where do systems rely on unnecessary cryptographic operations?

Investigate the limits and consequences of alternative trust and verification models.

Do not suggest replacing cryptographic guarantees with vague assumptions of trust.

## Perspective 4 — The attacker

Assume the adversary is patient, technically sophisticated and familiar with industrial systems.

Investigate:

- Compromised manufacturing.
- Malicious firmware.
- Signing infrastructure compromise.
- Insider threats.
- Compromised suppliers.
- Supply-chain substitution.
- Fault injection.
- Side-channel attacks.
- Physical extraction.
- Credential theft.
- Protocol downgrade.
- Recovery manipulation.
- Malicious maintenance tools.
- Compromised engineering workstations.
- Long-term persistence.
- Attacks against migration infrastructure itself.

Ask which proposed inventions give attackers new opportunities.

Can an attacker exploit the transition between two cryptographic generations?

Can the migration mechanism become more vulnerable than the system it protects?

Can an attacker force the device into a weaker compatibility mode?

What would a truly resilient design do?

## Perspective 5 — The manufacturer and OEM

Imagine being an equipment manufacturer responsible for a product that must remain supportable for 20–40 years.

You have cost targets, semiconductor procurement challenges, product certification, firmware maintenance, customer contracts and changing security standards.

What would make a new security architecture economically attractive?

What would require changes to chip selection, manufacturing, factory provisioning, firmware and service operations?

Could a new technology reduce long-term support liability?

Could it create interoperability problems or expose the manufacturer to new obligations?

What would encourage multiple OEMs to adopt it?

## Perspective 6 — The independent system integrator

The integrator connects equipment from different manufacturers.

Explore what happens when:

- Two OEMs use different identity systems.
- Devices follow different cryptographic policies.
- One vendor supports an algorithm that another does not.
- An installation contains equipment from several hardware generations.
- The customer owns the infrastructure but not the software.
- The integrator is responsible for commissioning but not long-term maintenance.

Could an independent layer reconcile these differences without becoming a dangerous central authority?

## Perspective 7 — The safety engineer

Study cryptographic migration as a safety-relevant change.

Investigate fault containment, independence, common-cause failure, safety integrity, deterministic behaviour, diagnostic coverage, failure recovery and change control.

Could cryptographic transitions be designed so that the security subsystem cannot unpredictably disrupt the control subsystem?

Could a safety case be maintained across cryptographic upgrades?

Where does the assurance argument need to be rebuilt?

Do not confuse security validation with functional safety validation.

## Perspective 8 — The economist and procurement strategist

Treat this as an ecosystem coordination problem.

Investigate who pays, who benefits, who bears risk, who controls the budget and who has the authority to adopt the technology.

Why might an operator delay investment even when a long-term risk is recognised?

Why might an OEM resist a solution that benefits the customer?

Could insurers, regulators, certification bodies or infrastructure owners create new incentives?

What commercial structures would reduce adoption friction?

Could migration become a shared service rather than a project repeatedly funded by individual facilities?

## Perspective 9 — The standards and governance researcher

Study the problem of long-lived technical systems operating under changing standards and institutional authority.

Who decides which algorithms are acceptable?

Who can authorise a transition?

How can multiple organisations coordinate a change?

What happens when standards evolve faster than industrial procurement cycles?

Can an architecture remain adaptable without making its security policy infinitely configurable?

## Perspective 10 — The future infrastructure designer

Ignore the current commercial categories.

Imagine industrial equipment designed today to operate through multiple generations of cryptographic technology.

What should be standard in every new device?

Which responsibilities should be handled by the silicon?

Which should belong to firmware?

Which should belong to the network?

Which should belong to a fleet infrastructure layer?

Which should be managed by the operator?

What should become an industry-wide capability rather than a proprietary feature?

For every perspective, identify its unique discoveries and then compare where its priorities conflict with other perspectives.

# STAGE 4 — INVESTIGATE WHETHER WE ARE SOLVING THE PROBLEM AT THE WRONG LAYER

This is a major new investigation.

Many engineering problems persist because solutions are applied at the layer where the problem becomes visible rather than the layer where it originates.

Investigate every important problem from multiple intervention levels.

For example, a large post-quantum signature may appear to be a memory and bandwidth problem. But perhaps the deeper issue is that every endpoint is expected to perform the same operation independently.

A difficult offline revocation problem may appear to require a better communication mechanism. But perhaps the deeper issue is that the system relies on a central, continuously available authority.

A hardware immutability problem may appear to require a replaceable root of trust. But perhaps the deeper issue is that long-term authority is bound too tightly to a specific manufacturer.

For each major problem, investigate whether it can be solved through:

1. A change to the mathematical or cryptographic mechanism.
2. A change to the silicon or hardware architecture.
3. A change to the firmware or software architecture.
4. A change to the protocol or communication model.
5. A change to the network topology.
6. A change to trust and authority distribution.
7. A change to device lifecycle management.
8. A change to operational procedures.
9. A change to organisational responsibility.
10. A change to procurement or commercial incentives.
11. A change to standards or interoperability arrangements.
12. Elimination of the requirement that creates the problem.

Do not assume the visible technical layer is the correct intervention point.

Create a **Problem-to-Intervention-Level Matrix**.

Identify where a change produces the largest benefit with the smallest implementation burden.

Look for cases where one intervention at a higher architectural level eliminates the need for many lower-level workarounds.

# STAGE 5 — FIND WAYS TO SOLVE MORE WITH LESS

This is the central theme of this second pass.

I want you to go beyond generating better solutions and investigate how to achieve the same or better security outcomes using dramatically fewer components, operations, dependencies, costs and maintenance activities.

Think about efficiency as a complete system property.

## A. Eliminate before optimising

For every major mechanism in the proposed architectures, ask:

- Is this component actually necessary?
- What would happen if we removed it?
- Does another component already provide the same capability?
- Is this mechanism compensating for a design flaw elsewhere?
- Can the underlying requirement be removed?
- Can multiple mechanisms be replaced by one simpler mechanism?
- Can we avoid creating this state in the first place?

Do not optimise unnecessary machinery.

## B. Reduce the number of trust relationships

Investigate how many independent trust relationships a device needs throughout its life.

Can they be simplified?

Can the number of authorities be reduced without creating concentration risk?

Can authority delegation be made more explicit?

Can a trust relationship be established once and safely evolve through controlled transitions?

Can trust become more composable and less dependent on vendor-specific arrangements?

## C. Reduce the amount of cryptographic work

Investigate:

- Whether all operations require post-quantum cryptography.
- Whether some devices need only verification rather than signing.
- Whether operations can be performed at commissioning instead of repeatedly.
- Whether cryptographic computation can be safely shared or delegated.
- Whether batch verification or aggregation is appropriate.
- Whether streaming or incremental verification can reduce memory use.
- Whether a protocol redesign could reduce the number of cryptographic messages.
- Whether symmetric mechanisms can safely handle particular use cases.
- Whether cryptographic operations are repeated unnecessarily.

Every efficiency proposal must preserve the required security properties.

Do not recommend a computational shortcut without analysing what guarantee is lost or transferred.

## D. Reduce the system's persistent state

Investigate how much state a device must preserve:

- Keys.
- Counters.
- Epochs.
- Firmware versions.
- Authority information.
- Revocation information.
- Configuration history.
- Recovery metadata.
- Monotonic values.
- Trust policies.

Can the required state be compressed, reconstructed, synchronised or managed differently?

Can unnecessary state be eliminated?

What is the trade-off between local storage, external services, recovery complexity and availability?

Pay special attention to devices that lose power, have unreliable clocks or remain disconnected for long periods.

## E. Reduce operational burden

Investigate whether technicians and operators can stop performing repetitive cryptographic administration.

Could commissioning, renewal, validation, recovery and decommissioning become more automated?

Which operations should remain human-controlled?

Which operations are safe to automate?

How can the system explain its decisions and provide evidence that the operator can verify?

## F. Reduce the number of migrations

Is it necessary for every system component to migrate independently?

Could some changes be performed transparently at a gateway, trust layer or common infrastructure component?

Could new equipment provide backward-compatible security boundaries for legacy devices?

Could a device remain operational while its cryptographic authority changes independently?

Investigate when abstraction actually reduces lifecycle complexity and when it only hides the problem.

## G. Reduce long-term maintenance

Investigate what would make a solution maintainable for 20–40 years.

Could the architecture minimise dependence on proprietary tools, vendor cloud services, external connectivity, expiring credentials and specialised personnel?

Could it use stable interfaces while allowing replaceable implementations underneath?

What would happen when the original developer, supplier or integrator is no longer available?

**For every strong invention, calculate what complexity it removes and what complexity it introduces.**

Create a comparison of the original approach, the improved approach and the simplest viable approach.

I want to identify solutions that are not merely more secure, but structurally simpler and cheaper to operate.

# STAGE 6 — INVESTIGATE THE SECOND- AND THIRD-ORDER CONSEQUENCES

The previous work focused heavily on solving visible problems.

Now analyse what happens after the proposed solutions are deployed.

A solution can successfully eliminate one problem and create another five years later.

Investigate the long-term consequences of:

- Centralised cryptographic management.
- Distributed authority.
- Hardware roots of trust.
- Cryptographic agility.
- Algorithm replacement.
- Hybrid cryptography.
- Automated firmware updates.
- Remote attestation.
- Hardware acceleration.
- Gateway-based security.
- Delegated signing.
- Threshold authority.
- Offline operation.
- Cryptographic transparency.
- Long-lived device identities.
- Continuous verification.
- AI-assisted security management.

For each major architecture, ask:

**What does this design make easier today, and what could it make harder tomorrow?**

Explore:

### 1. Complexity transfer

Does the architecture eliminate complexity or simply move it from endpoints to gateways, central services, OEMs, integrators or operators?

Who inherits the new burden?

### 2. Concentration risk

Does the system introduce a new infrastructure component whose compromise affects thousands or millions of devices?

Can authority be concentrated operationally even when it is distributed technically?

### 3. Long-term lock-in

Could a technology designed to provide cryptographic agility become difficult to replace itself?

Can a new abstraction become another permanent dependency?

### 4. Security monoculture

Could a single migration platform, algorithm policy or hardware design create a new systemic vulnerability?

When is diversity useful, and when does it create unacceptable complexity?

### 5. Recovery complexity

Does the architecture handle the initial security transition but fail to provide a credible recovery mechanism when authority, state or keys are compromised?

### 6. Operational uncertainty

Can operators understand which security state the system is actually in?

Can they distinguish a valid transition from a compromised transition?

### 7. Future obsolescence

Could today's proposed solution become tomorrow's legacy problem?

How do we avoid building another generation of devices whose security depends on assumptions that cannot be changed?

Build a second-order consequence graph for the most promising architectures.

# STAGE 7 — INVESTIGATE NEW AND OVERLOOKED TECHNOLOGIES

I want a dedicated technology-discovery exercise.

Do not simply repeat the technologies already discussed.

Search the broader scientific and engineering landscape for methods that could be useful, even when their original applications are unrelated to post-quantum security.

Investigate new possibilities across at least the following areas.

### A. Advanced hardware and semiconductor design

Explore:

- Reconfigurable hardware.
- Secure microarchitecture.
- Hardware-assisted cryptographic agility.
- Trusted execution.
- Programmable logic.
- Hardware isolation.
- Memory protection.
- Secure boot alternatives.
- Immutable versus mutable trust anchors.
- Hardware state protection.
- Low-power accelerators.
- Secure hardware lifecycle mechanisms.

Investigate whether hardware can be designed to support future cryptographic change without requiring the silicon itself to be replaced.

### B. Distributed systems and asynchronous computing

Explore:

- Asynchronous consensus.
- Eventual consistency.
- Quorum-based authority.
- Conflict-free state management.
- Distributed logs.
- Replicated state machines.
- Byzantine fault tolerance.
- Threshold mechanisms.
- Epoch-based coordination.
- Intermittent connectivity.
- Store-and-forward communication.

Investigate how these mechanisms could support trustworthy operation across isolated facilities and disconnected devices.

### C. Formal methods and high-assurance engineering

Explore:

- Model checking.
- Theorem proving.
- State-machine verification.
- Protocol verification.
- Refinement types.
- Runtime assurance.
- Proof-carrying code.
- Verified boot chains.
- Safety cases.
- Compositional verification.
- Automated invariant checking.

Could we make cryptographic transitions verifiable as system-level state changes rather than treating them as a sequence of independent update operations?

### D. Information theory and resource efficiency

Explore:

- Succinct proofs.
- Compact representations.
- Error-correcting mechanisms.
- Information-theoretic limits.
- Efficient commitments.
- Data structures for authenticated state.
- Communication-efficient verification.
- Memory-efficient computation.
- Precomputation.
- Incremental validation.

Investigate whether any of these principles can meaningfully reduce embedded resource requirements.

### E. Manufacturing and supply-chain assurance

Explore:

- Secure provisioning at manufacturing.
- Independent component authentication.
- Reproducible builds.
- Software bills of materials.
- Hardware provenance.
- Firmware transparency.
- Supplier verification.
- Manufacturing audit trails.
- Component lifecycle records.
- Secure factory-to-field transitions.

Could trust be established and maintained through stronger evidence about how a device was built, configured and updated?

### F. Physical and environmental systems

Investigate:

- Physical unclonable functions.
- Hardware entropy.
- Environmental influence on physical security.
- Tamper evidence.
- Physical access controls.
- Secure key destruction.
- Fault-resistant design.
- Power-loss-resistant state changes.
- Long-term storage reliability.

Do not assume that physical properties automatically provide cryptographic security. Examine the actual threat model and practical limitations.

### G. Autonomous infrastructure management

Explore:

- Automated dependency discovery.
- Policy-driven configuration.
- Digital twins.
- Simulation-based migration.
- Automated compatibility analysis.
- Runtime anomaly detection.
- Evidence collection.
- Predictive lifecycle management.
- Human-supervised autonomous operations.

Investigate whether the migration process itself can become a continuously managed engineering capability.

### H. Technologies from unrelated industries

Search outside conventional cybersecurity.

Look at medical devices, aviation, railways, spacecraft, nuclear engineering, industrial process control, distributed energy, telecommunications, automotive electronics, banking infrastructure, logistics, high-assurance computing and disaster recovery.

Find technologies and methodologies that solve comparable problems of long lifecycles, distributed trust, constrained hardware, failure recovery and interoperability.

For every new technology, identify its actual transferable mechanism, not simply its name.

Produce a **New Technology Discovery Register** showing:

- Technology or research area.
- Original application.
- Underlying mechanism.
- Potential relevance to OT and embedded security.
- What problem it could solve.
- Existing evidence.
- Adaptation required.
- Possible limitations.
- Research or implementation opportunity.

# STAGE 8 — FIND THE ALTERNATIVE TO THE ALTERNATIVE

I want you to challenge the assumption that the most obvious technical direction is necessarily the best one.

For every major solution category in the previous work, investigate at least one fundamentally different way of achieving the same objective.

Examples:

**Instead of upgrading every endpoint:** Could we redesign the surrounding architecture so that constrained endpoints require fewer new capabilities?

**Instead of relying on continuous revocation:** Could we design stronger bounded authority, offline policies, expiration mechanisms or other methods that do not require constant connectivity?

**Instead of giving every device a complex cryptographic stack:** Could different device classes have different security responsibilities while maintaining end-to-end guarantees?

**Instead of permanently trusting a manufacturer:** Could trust depend on independently verifiable evidence that evolves through the device lifecycle?

**Instead of replacing a complete hardware root of trust:** Could we safely separate immutable measurement from replaceable authority and verification policy?

**Instead of building a new central management platform:** Could we create interoperable, distributed mechanisms that allow different management systems to cooperate?

**Instead of performing a large fleet-wide migration:** Could we use progressive capability discovery, risk-based transition and coexistence mechanisms?

These are directions to investigate, not predefined solutions.

For each major problem, produce:

- The conventional approach.
- The best alternative approach.
- The underlying difference.
- The advantages.
- The disadvantages.
- The security consequences.
- The implementation consequences.
- The circumstances where each approach is superior.

Look especially for solutions that eliminate the need for an entire category of infrastructure.

# STAGE 9 — DESIGN A MORE INTELLIGENT WAY TO ALLOCATE SECURITY

Not every industrial device has the same criticality, exposure, lifetime, resource budget or operational role.

Investigate whether one universal security architecture is inherently inefficient.

Develop a risk- and capability-based model that considers:

- Device criticality.
- Expected service life.
- Physical accessibility.
- Network exposure.
- Communication frequency.
- Computational capacity.
- Memory availability.
- Energy budget.
- Data sensitivity.
- Command authority.
- Safety consequences.
- Recovery options.
- Vendor support status.
- Connectivity.
- Environmental conditions.

Investigate whether devices should be grouped into different security classes.

Could a small, isolated monitoring sensor use a fundamentally different architecture from a safety-critical controller or a high-value industrial gateway?

Could security capabilities be allocated based on actual risk rather than forcing every device to implement every capability?

Could the overall system still provide coherent, verifiable security guarantees?

Develop at least three models:

1. A universal architecture.
2. A tiered, device-class architecture.
3. A dynamically adaptable architecture.

Compare their costs, complexity, security boundaries and long-term maintenance implications.

Investigate how to avoid creating weak classes that become easy targets or hidden paths into critical systems.

The objective is not to lower security arbitrarily. It is to apply the right security mechanisms at the right locations, with explicit and defensible guarantees.

# STAGE 10 — CONDUCT A SERIOUS MULTI-PERSPECTIVE DESIGN DEBATE

I want an actual confrontation between competing architectural philosophies.

Do not simply produce a list of opinions.

Create a structured design debate between at least these positions:

### Position A — The cryptographic purist

Argues that security guarantees must be based on rigorous cryptographic mechanisms and that architectural convenience must never weaken them.

### Position B — The embedded minimalist

Argues that the strongest architecture may be the one that requires the least computation, storage, connectivity and device-side complexity.

### Position C — The industrial pragmatist

Argues that availability, maintainability, interoperability and deployment practicality are as important as theoretical security.

### Position D — The distributed-systems architect

Argues that authority and trust should be designed around failures, partial connectivity, decentralisation and inconsistent state.

### Position E — The hardware architect

Argues that software cannot compensate indefinitely for hardware limitations and that some problems need to be solved at the silicon and manufacturing level.

### Position F — The safety engineer

Argues that security changes must preserve predictable behaviour, fault containment and the safety case.

### Position G — The independent security auditor

Argues that every proposal must survive adversarial examination, implementation review and evidence-based validation.

### Position H — The technology entrepreneur

Argues that the solution must fit an actual customer problem, have a credible deployment path and deliver measurable economic value.

Make each position:

1. Identify the most important problem.
2. Challenge the assumptions of the other positions.
3. Propose a preferred architecture.
4. Explain its technical advantages.
5. Acknowledge its weaknesses.
6. Respond to criticism.
7. Identify evidence that would change its position.

Then conduct a convergence session.

Find:

- Where multiple perspectives agree.
- Where trade-offs are unavoidable.
- Where one architecture is clearly superior.
- Where different device classes require different solutions.
- Where the best approach is a hybrid.
- Where no responsible conclusion can yet be reached.

Do not artificially manufacture agreement.

I want genuine intellectual disagreement that leads to better engineering decisions.

# STAGE 11 — REBUILD THE ARCHITECTURES FOR MAXIMUM EFFICIENCY

Return to the strongest architectural proposals from the previous work.

Do not simply rank them again.

Redesign them using everything discovered in this second pass.

For each architecture, investigate:

- Which components can be removed?
- Which interfaces can be simplified?
- Which operations can be eliminated?
- Which trust dependencies can be reduced?
- Which functions can be combined?
- Which functions should be separated?
- Which operations should be local?
- Which can be delegated?
- What must remain independently verifiable?
- What should be standardised?
- What should remain implementation-specific?
- Where can state be reduced?
- What should happen during loss of connectivity?
- How should recovery work?
- What is the smallest viable hardware requirement?
- What is the smallest viable software footprint?

Produce two redesigned versions of each promising architecture:

**Version 1 — Minimal viable architecture**

The simplest design that achieves the required security properties under a clearly defined threat model.

**Version 2 — High-assurance architecture**

A stronger architecture with additional defence in depth, independent verification, resilience and recovery capabilities.

Compare them.

Then identify which features are truly essential and which are optional enhancements.

Create a system diagram for each redesigned architecture. Include trust boundaries, data flows, control flows, authority relationships, failure modes and recovery paths.

# STAGE 12 — INVESTIGATE REAL-WORLD DEPLOYMENT FROM START TO FINISH

A technically successful system can fail because its deployment process is unrealistic.

I want a much more detailed examination of how these technologies would actually enter industrial infrastructure.

Build several realistic deployment scenarios.

Include:

### Scenario A — A new industrial device

A manufacturer is designing a new embedded product today.

How should cryptographic agility, identity, manufacturing provisioning, firmware verification and future trust transitions be designed from the beginning?

### Scenario B — An old device that cannot be modified

The installed equipment cannot be updated, its cryptographic capabilities are limited and replacing it is expensive.

What can realistically be done around it?

What security guarantees can still be achieved, and what guarantees are impossible?

### Scenario C — A mixed-vendor facility

The installation contains devices from several manufacturers, multiple generations of equipment, different protocols and different security capabilities.

How can an architecture create coherent trust without forcing a complete replacement?

### Scenario D — A long-term disconnected installation

The device may remain offline for years.

How should its authority, state, credentials, firmware and recovery mechanisms operate?

### Scenario E — OEM disappearance

The original manufacturer is no longer available.

How can the asset owner continue maintaining the device without creating uncontrolled trust?

### Scenario F — An emergency security event

A cryptographic algorithm, signing authority or critical credential is compromised.

How can the system recover without creating unacceptable operational disruption?

### Scenario G — A new technology transition

An organisation needs to move from an existing cryptographic configuration to a future one across thousands of devices.

How should the change be planned, tested, coordinated and verified?

For each scenario, provide:

- Initial conditions.
- Existing constraints.
- Proposed architecture.
- Stakeholders.
- Step-by-step deployment.
- Required tools.
- Failure points.
- Recovery process.
- Validation evidence.
- Cost drivers.
- Expected operational impact.
- Remaining risks.

Identify which ideas survive actual deployment conditions and which only work on paper.

# STAGE 13 — BUILD A MUCH BETTER ECONOMIC AND COMMERCIAL MODEL

I want a more rigorous economic investigation than simply identifying products that might be commercially interesting.

Study how value is created and where the money actually moves.

Investigate:

1. Who experiences the problem?
2. Who owns the affected asset?
3. Who controls the technical decision?
4. Who controls the budget?
5. Who bears the liability?
6. Who performs the implementation?
7. Who maintains the solution?
8. Who benefits from reduced risk?
9. Who could block procurement?
10. Who would become dependent on the technology?

Analyse possible business models:

- Per-device licensing.
- Per-site licensing.
- Fleet-wide subscriptions.
- Managed cryptographic lifecycle services.
- Embedded software licensing.
- OEM licensing.
- Gateway or appliance sales.
- Hardware modules.
- Certification and assurance services.
- Migration engineering.
- Long-term support contracts.
- Open-source core with commercial assurance services.
- Industry consortium infrastructure.
- Standards-based interoperability services.

For each model, investigate the actual costs of delivering and supporting it.

Do not use invented market sizes or unsupported financial projections.

Develop realistic economic models with explicit assumptions and sensitivity analysis.

Investigate whether the commercial opportunity is:

- A product.
- A protocol.
- A specialised hardware component.
- An infrastructure layer.
- A managed service.
- A developer tool.
- A certification capability.
- A shared industry utility.
- A combination of these.

Ask an important question:

**What would make a customer adopt this before a major cryptographic failure forces them to?**

Find a credible economic reason to act early.

Also investigate whether a solution has value even for customers who are not immediately concerned about quantum computing. For example, could it improve firmware governance, asset visibility, supplier independence, device lifecycle management or operational resilience?

# STAGE 14 — SEARCH FOR THE BEST STARTING POINT, NOT JUST THE BIGGEST VISION

A revolutionary architecture is worthless if there is no credible way to begin building and deploying it.

Identify the smallest practical entry points that could eventually lead to a much larger system.

For each promising architecture, investigate:

- The smallest prototype that demonstrates its central mechanism.
- The smallest customer problem it could solve independently.
- The minimum hardware requirements.
- The minimum software components.
- The dependencies that can be postponed.
- The standards that can be reused.
- The first organisations that could test it.
- The evidence required to establish credibility.
- The next stage after a successful pilot.

Investigate whether we should begin with:

- Embedded verification libraries.
- Cryptographic dependency discovery.
- Device identity lifecycle tools.
- Offline authority-management mechanisms.
- Firmware trust infrastructure.
- Automated migration planning.
- Hardware modules.
- Gateway-based security mediation.
- Fleet security evidence.
- A new interoperability layer.
- Another opportunity discovered through the research.

Do not favour a starting point simply because it is easy to build.

Look for the intersection of:

- A real and painful problem.
- A technically defensible solution.
- A manageable initial implementation.
- A customer who can adopt it.
- A meaningful path to a larger infrastructure capability.

Create three roadmaps:

**Roadmap A — Practical entry:** What could be built and validated first?

**Roadmap B — Strategic expansion:** How does the initial capability become a larger product or infrastructure layer?

**Roadmap C — Long-term transformation:** What would be required to change the wider industrial ecosystem?

Include decision gates that determine whether to continue, change direction or abandon an approach.

# STAGE 15 — BUILD A RESEARCH AND VALIDATION PROGRAMME THAT KEEPS DISCOVERING

Do not treat this investigation as a final answer.

Identify the most important unanswered scientific, technical, operational and commercial questions.

For each question, specify:

- What we currently believe.
- Why that belief may be wrong.
- What evidence is missing.
- What experiment could resolve it.
- What the experiment would cost or require.
- What result would support the hypothesis.
- What result would disprove it.
- What decision depends on the result.

Prioritise the experiments by information value, not just implementation difficulty.

Investigate which small experiment could eliminate the largest amount of uncertainty.

Use an experimental sequence in which early tests invalidate weak architectures before expensive engineering begins.

For promising ideas, propose:

1. A software simulation.
2. A cryptographic prototype.
3. An embedded implementation.
4. A hardware-in-the-loop test.
5. A failure-injection test.
6. A formal verification exercise.
7. A controlled industrial pilot.
8. An independent security review.

Only include the stages that are relevant to the specific invention.

All validation must take place in controlled, owned or authorised environments. Do not conduct experiments against live critical infrastructure.

# STAGE 16 — CONDUCT A FINAL INDEPENDENT CHALLENGE

After completing all the research, act as an independent review board that has not participated in the invention process.

Its job is to challenge the conclusions, not endorse them.

Ask:

- What is the most important thing the previous work misunderstood?
- What is the most important problem still unsolved?
- Which invention is technically impressive but commercially irrelevant?
- Which commercial opportunity is attractive but technically weak?
- Which architecture has unnecessary components?
- Which solution creates dangerous concentration of trust?
- Which problem is being overemphasised?
- Which important problem is underemphasised?
- Which existing technology already solves more of the problem than we recognised?
- What is the strongest argument against the leading proposal?
- What evidence would cause us to abandon it?
- Which assumptions are still doing too much work?
- What would a highly experienced industrial operator disagree with?
- What would an independent cryptographer reject?
- What will become an operational liability after 15 years?
- What will happen if the original vendor disappears?
- What happens if the proposed technology itself becomes obsolete?

Then rank the strongest solutions again.

Do not preserve previous rankings automatically.

A solution should rise or fall based on what has been discovered.

---

# THE FINAL DELIVERABLE — THE SECOND-PASS DISCOVERY ATLAS

Produce a substantial, deeply researched report organised as follows:

1. Executive synthesis: What changed in our understanding.
2. Complete audit of the previous work.
3. Previous Work Challenge Register.
4. At least 50 additional problem hypotheses, where supported by research.
5. Hidden lifecycle, state, human and economic problems.
6. The findings from all independent stakeholder perspectives.
7. The Problem-to-Intervention-Level Matrix.
8. The most important opportunities for eliminating complexity.
9. Second- and third-order consequence analysis.
10. New technology discoveries from adjacent disciplines.
11. Alternative solutions to the existing leading approaches.
12. The competing architecture debate.
13. The redesigned minimal and high-assurance architectures.
14. Real-world deployment scenarios.
15. Economic and commercial analysis.
16. The strongest practical starting points.
17. Prioritised experiments and validation programme.
18. The independent challenge-board findings.
19. Revised rankings of the strongest inventions.
20. The most important unanswered questions.

I've finished the second-pass review of the OT and embedded post-quantum security work. The main finding is that the earlier work was aimed at the wrong bottleneck. The biggest problems are industrial fleets not installing updates that already exist, and classical "side doors" that get around secure boot: debug unlock, ownership transfer, factory provisioning and engineering workstations. A new mask ROM isn't the main gap, because Nordic's NSIB and ST's OEMiRoT show that many current chips can already lock a first-stage bootloader in flash.

The pass also corrected three numbers that change the design:
- **Boot time:** the ≈42 ms figure only holds for the larger 2,508-byte LMS signature (W4). The 1,452-byte signature (W8) takes about 363 ms in software at 64 MHz.
- **Pre-rotation savings:** linear pre-rotation saves 1.5–3×, not 10×.
- **Root signing:** NIST's draft SP 800-230 adds limited-use SLH-DSA parameter sets for firmware signing. That removes most of the reason to keep stateful LMS keys for root signing, except where NSA's CNSA 2.0 still names LMS/XMSS.

The new build order is:
1. An open first stage locked in flash, with key slots across at least two cryptographic families.
2. Post-quantum debug credentials and FIDO Device Onboard ownership vouchers, plus turning off field debug.
3. Symmetric keying for the process bus.
4. Update staging that fits existing maintenance windows.
5. Isolated enclaves and a replacement schedule for devices that can't be updated.

The new-ROM kernel, the neutral custodian, threshold signing and the time-free layer are now research items only.

The report also includes a full audit of the earlier claims, 55 new problem hypotheses, a cost model for a 10,000-device fleet and a ranked list of experiments. The first experiment to run is a two-week desk survey of how many industrial microcontroller families can lock a first stage in flash. If fewer than half can, the plan shifts back toward asking chip makers for new silicon.

Some limits on the evidence:
- Only about 70 sources were checked, far fewer than the 500–600 asked for.
- The final NIST IR 8547 and Executive Order 14412 are based on secondary reports.
- The downtime and patching figures come from vendors.
- SP 800-230 is still a draft and could change.

**OT and Embedded Post-Quantum Security: Second-Pass Discovery Atlas**

# Second-Pass Discovery Atlas: OT and Embedded Post-Quantum Security, Re-examined

The previous work bet on new silicon and a new kernel to carry trust through algorithm changes. This pass finds that bet aimed at the wrong bottleneck. Industrial fleets mostly fail to install updates that already exist. Many shipping MCUs can already host a hash-based, PQ-capable immutable first stage in locked flash. And the most dangerous unaddressed exposures are classical "side doors" around secure boot: debug unlock, ownership transfer, factory provisioning and engineering workstations. Build first: an open continuity first stage for existing MCUs, PQ-capable ownership vouchers and debug credentials, and update staging aligned to maintenance windows — not an Authority Continuity Kernel in new ROM.

## TL;DR

- **The thesis survives but moves layer.** The real bottleneck is update adoption plus classical authorisation paths that bypass secure boot, not the immutable ROM verifier. In "The Wretched State of OT Firmware Patching", aDolus CTO Eric Byres analysed Microsoft customer PLC data: "60% were running ancient firmware versions with 8 or more exploitable CVEs… updates have been available for over 10 years!" Silicon Labs, NXP and STM32 debug-unlock credentials are classical signatures granting full device access.
- **Several load-bearing numbers in the previous work were wrong, and the corrections change the architecture.**
  - The ≈42 ms boot estimate paired the LM-OTS W4 cycle count with the 1,452 B W8 signature. A W8 verify is ≈23 M cycles, ≈363 ms at 64 MHz in software.
  - Linear pre-rotation saves 1.5–3×, not 10×.
  - NIST draft SP 800-230 (Quynh Dang and Dustin Moody; comments 13 Apr–12 Jun 2026) adds six SLH-DSA sets for firmware signing with "a strict limit of 2^24 signatures per signing key". That removes most of the case for stateful LMS root custody.
- **Re-ranked recommendation, built on existing standards:**
  1. An open locked-flash continuity first stage with dual-class key slots.
  2. PQ-capable FIDO Device Onboard ownership vouchers and debug credentials.
  3. Symmetric-first process-bus keying.
  4. Fleet crypto-state evidence tied to the CRA and India's DST timelines.

  New-ROM licensing, the custodian utility and the time-free layer drop to research status.

## 1. Executive synthesis: what changed in our understanding

**What survives [I]:**
- PQ migration in OT is an authority-continuity problem.
- Hash functions are the conservative common denominator.
- Verification, not signing, belongs on constrained endpoints.
- The process bus stays symmetric.
- The OEM/integrator/operator responsibility gap is real.

**What changed, in order of consequence:**

1. **The binding constraint is update adoption.**
   - Eric Byres of aDolus (vendor, Oct 2023) analysed Microsoft customer PLC data: "60% were running ancient firmware versions with 8 or more exploitable CVEs… updates have been available for over 10 years!" Updating would raise CVE-free devices "from 4% to 40%" and cut devices with more than eight CVEs "down to only 18%".
   - SANS ICS/OT Cybersecurity Survey: 2023's Challenges (700+ responses): "Fewer than 30% of facilities pre-test and apply vendor-validated patches on a defined schedule, and only 15% apply patches during routine maintenance windows."
   - The previous work modelled the update as an attack surface, never as the thing that does not happen.
2. **Maker-defined immutable first stages already exist.**
   - Nordic's nRF Secure Immutable Bootloader (NSIB) runs from write-protected flash. It stores only hashes of provisioned public keys (in OTP on nRF91/nRF5340). Using a higher-index key permanently invalidates lower ones — deployed prior art for a monotonic sunset.
   - ST's OEMiRoT combines unique boot entry, write protection, RDP Level 2 and HDP so that user-flash code is immutable.
   - A PQ-capable hash-only first stage can therefore ship on many existing parts without a new mask ROM.
3. **Classical side doors were under-weighted.** Each of the following gives total control to a quantum-capable forger without touching the boot chain:
   - Silicon Labs Series 2 debug unlock signs with ECDSA-P256-SHA256.
   - NXP RW61x debug credentials are signed by RoT keys.
   - OpenTitan's ownership unlock/activate keys are ECDSA P-256, with inline signatures "ECDSA-only".
   - FIDO Device Onboard (FDO) ownership vouchers use SECP256R1/SECP384R1.
4. **Stateful hash signatures are no longer the only conservative firmware option.**
   - NIST SP 800-230 ipd (Quynh Dang and Dustin Moody; comments open 13 Apr–12 Jun 2026) adds six SLH-DSA sets at levels 1, 3 and 5. They carry "a strict limit of 2^24 signatures per signing key" and are "not approved for general-purpose use". They are tailored to software, firmware and certificate signing.
   - For comparison, the smallest FIPS 205 signatures are 7,856, 16,224 and 29,792 B.
   - Caveat: NSA CNSA 2.0 still names LMS/XMSS for national-security firmware, and its v2.1 excludes HSS and XMSS^MT.
5. **Arithmetic corrections** (Section 3) change rankings.
6. **India makes the pressure concrete.**
   - The DST Task Force roadmap (draft Feb 2026; final May 2026 per secondary sources) sets critical information infrastructure (CII) milestones: foundations 2027, priority migration 2028, completion 2029.
   - It names power, telecom and ONGC as urgent adopters.
   - It recommends containing "classical-only systems within controlled enclaves where immediate migration is not feasible". Against 15–30-year OT assets, enclave containment becomes the default for most installed OT.

**The hidden assumption that changed most:** that the cryptographic root is the scarcest resource. The scarcest resources are maintenance windows, people who understand the device, and authorisation paths nobody inventoried.

### Corrected arithmetic

**(a) Boot time [E].**
- Campos measured LMS SHA-256 verification at:
  - 2,658,884 cycles with Winternitz 16 (W4), h=10;
  - 23,252,036 cycles with Winternitz 256 (W8), h=5.
- At 64 MHz that is 2.66M/64M = 41.5 ms (W4, 2,508 B signature) and 23.25M/64M = 363 ms (W8, 1,452 B).
- With hardware SHA-256 at an assumed ~100 cycles per compression instead of ≈3,000, W8 becomes ≈23.25M × 100/3,000 ≈ 0.8M cycles ≈ 12 ms. This assumes hashing dominates the cost.
- **Rule:** use W4 without a hash engine; W8 with one.

**(b) Catch-up after N=10 missed rotations [E].**
- A key set is 5 × 1,952 + 200 = 9,960 B.
- A t=3 signature bundle is 3 × 3,309 = 9,927 B.

| Scheme | Bytes/step | N=10 total | vs TUF (298,140 B) |
|---|---|---|---|
| TUF walk | 29,814 | 298,140 | 1.0× |
| KERI-style pre-rotation | 19,887 | 198,870 | 1.5× |
| Pure hash-chain | 9,960 | 99,600 | 3.0× |
| Merkle over K=64 future sets | 9,960 + 6×32 | 10,152 | 29.4× |

- Only the Merkle variant gives a large saving. It requires keeping 64 pre-generated key sets secret for decades, and fixes their families at genesis.
- The previous E6 pass threshold (≥5×) **fails** for the scheme as specified.

**(c) Family independence [I].**
- Let A(k) be the set of assumptions key k depends on:
  - ML-DSA → {MLWE/MSIS, SHAKE}
  - SLH-DSA-SHAKE → {Keccak}
  - LMS-SHA256 → {SHA-256}
  - ECDSA → {ECDLP, SHA-2}
- A transition rule is **single-assumption-safe** if no single assumption's failure leaves an authorising quorum, unless that quorum spans at least two disjoint assumption sets.
- "LMS + SHA-256 escrow" fails this test after a SHA-256 break.
- **Minimum robust set:** {ML-DSA, SLH-DSA-SHAKE, LMS-SHA-256}, held by at least two organisations.

---

## 5. Hidden lifecycle, state, human and economic problems

**Lifecycle.** At least five classical side doors exist outside secure boot:
- debug (N1–N2);
- RMA (N3);
- ownership (N5–N7);
- provisioning (N8);
- configuration (N17).

A CRQC attacker uses whichever is cheapest.

**State.** Monotonic floors already ship. The overlooked risk is that they can be burned against the owner (N22). So raising a floor must need stronger authorisation than booting.

**Human.**
- Patching happens in annual or biannual windows (aDolus). Only 15% of facilities patch in routine windows, per SANS's 2023 survey of 700+ respondents.
- Key knowledge leaves with integrator staff.
- "Don't update" is rational given the downtime cost. ABB's "Value of Reliability" survey (Sapio Research, July 2023, 3,215 respondents) reports a median of $124,669 per hour of unplanned downtime (about $103,000 in the US). "More than two-thirds" of respondents have an unplanned outage at least monthly [V].

**Economic.** Visit costs are small. S&C Electric's GridTalk (March 2017) reports that "Many utilities take an ultra-conservative guess, claiming truck rolls each average $250 to $500" [V]. TSIA's figure of ~$1,000 is quoted by a vendor blog. The problem is schedule risk, not labour.

---

## 6. Findings from the ten stakeholder perspectives

| Perspective | Unique discovery | Would reject | Main conflict |
|---|---|---|---|
| Operator | Crypto must ride existing windows; "explain what changed" | New outages, vendor cloud, new skills | Cryptographer's fast deprecation |
| Embedded engineer | Hash hardware decides feasibility (M4 SHA2 ≈3,000; Keccak ≈12,000 cycles/call) | ML-DSA signing on M0+ | Purist |
| Cryptographer | Single-assumption-safe rule; composite is EUF-CMA only | SHA-256 monoculture | Minimalist |
| Attacker | Cheapest path is debug or voucher; floors can be burned | — | Operator convenience |
| OEM | First stage is a firmware deliverable; CRA 5 years caps promises | Kernel tied to one MCU vendor | Operator's 30-year wish |
| Integrator | FDO is the only standard ownership chain | Competitor-owned root-of-roots | Custodian model |
| Safety engineer | Crypto failure must keep last-known-good running | Fail-secure boot in SIS | Auditor |
| Economist | Value = avoided unplanned downtime + compliance evidence | Per-device subscriptions | Entrepreneur |
| Standards researcher | Reuse FDO, RFC 9881/9909, TCG v1.85, SP 800-230 | New proprietary formats | Inventor |
| Future designer | Every device: SHA-2+SHA-3, locked stage with ≥3 key-hash slots over ≥2 classes, PQ debug and vouchers | Single-algorithm ROMs | — |

---

## 8. Opportunities for eliminating complexity

1. **Eliminate new ROM.** Use a locked-flash first stage.
   - Removes: the mask-ROM dependency and licensing.
   - Adds: reliance on lock integrity.
2. **Eliminate stateful root custody.** Use SP 800-230 SLH-DSA once final, keeping LMS only under CNSA 2.0.
   - Removes: index reservation and catastrophic state reuse.
   - Adds: larger signatures and slower verification without a hash engine. A post on X by @conduition_io, not NIST, puts the smallest draft set at 3,856 B. bc-java issue #2402 lists the 192-24 set at 7,752 B and the 256-24 set at 14,944 B.
3. **Eliminate threshold cryptography.** Use multisignatures (≈9.9 KB for three ML-DSA-65 signatures).
4. **Eliminate the time-free layer as a product.** Index floors already ship.
5. **Eliminate field debug.** Disable it after commissioning and handle RMA by board swap. This removes N1–N3.
6. **Eliminate per-device PQ for un-updatable assets.** Use enclave containment instead.
7. **Eliminate a custom passport format.** Use CycloneDX CBOM plus TCG/IETF attestation.

---

## 9. Second- and third-order consequences

```
Locked first stage ──► no silicon dependency
   ├─► first-stage bug unfixable (N20) ──► formal verification of ~13 KB
   └─► RDP2 blocks RMA (N4) ──► weaker lock shipped ──► side door reopens
                                  └─► mitigation: board-swap RMA + PQ debug cert
FDO-PQ vouchers ──► orphan recovery without custodian
   ├─► voucher files become crown jewels (N7) ──► escrow vouchers, not keys
   └─► M&A diligence must include vouchers (N52)
Enclave containment ──► fast compliance (India 2029)
   ├─► gateway concentration (N43)
   └─► "temporary" enclaves become permanent; inside never migrates
Symmetric fabric ──► process bus untouched
   └─► KDC compromise fleet-wide ──► per-device key diversification (N10)
Window staging ──► adoption without downtime
   └─► attackers learn windows ──► staged artefacts signed and attested
```

- **Complexity transfer:** the redesign moves complexity into contracts and existing standards that already have owners. The custodian would have created a new organisation.
- **Lock-in:** a locked stage is lock-in by design. Mitigate with ≥3 slots across ≥2 assumption classes and an open reference.
- **Monoculture:** a shared open stage spreads one bug everywhere. Mitigate with formal verification and two independent implementations.

---

## 11. Alternatives to the leading approaches

| Objective | Previous | Alternative | Alternative wins when | Previous wins when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Boot survives algorithm break | ACK in new ROM/OTP | Locked first stage, ≥3 slots, ≥2 classes | Lockable MCUs exist | No lockable flash; high physical threat |
| Root signing | Stateful LMS + custody | SP 800-230 SLH-DSA | Commercial OT | CNSA 2.0 systems |
| Ownership | Threshold + custodian | FDO-PQ + contractual voucher escrow | Multi-OEM fleets | Single strong operator |
| Offline revocation | Counter layer | Index floor + enclave | Disconnected assets | — |
| Legacy | Gateway + passport | Enclave + replacement schedule | Un-updatable assets | — |
| Catch-up | Linear pre-rotation | Merkle future sets, or plain TUF | Long-offline | Future-key secrecy untenable |
| Debug | — | Disable; PQ debug certs | Production units | Lab/RMA units |
| Adoption | — | Window-aligned signed staging | Everywhere | — |

---

## 12. The competing architecture debate

- **A — Purist.**
  - Problem: side doors.
  - Prefers: slots for ML-DSA + SLH-DSA-SHAKE + LMS-SHA-256; the single-assumption rule; PQ debug and vouchers.
  - Attacks B: "one hash is a monoculture."
  - Weakness: cost on M0+.
  - Changes mind if SP 800-230 verify on a hash-engine MCU exceeds 100 ms.
- **B — Minimalist.**
  - Problem: RAM and boot time.
  - Prefers: one hash verifier in the first stage; ML-DSA only in the updatable second stage.
  - Attacks A: "three verifiers are three bug surfaces."
  - Changes mind on a structural attack on SHA-256 or SHAKE.
- **C — Pragmatist.**
  - Problem: non-adoption.
  - Prefers: window staging, enclaves, replacement plans.
  - Attacks all: "nothing helps if updates sit uninstalled."
  - Weakness: leaves HNDL-to-authority open.
  - Changes mind if pilots show >80% remote adoption without downtime.
- **D — Distributed systems.**
  - Problem: divergent trust state.
  - Prefers: floors, signed state digests, gossip of the highest floor.
  - Attacks C: "enclaves diverge silently."
  - Weakness: protocol surface.
- **E — Hardware.**
  - Problem: flash locks are weaker than ROM; no hash engine.
  - Prefers: SHA-2+SHA-3 engines and OTP key-hash slots via procurement.
  - Attacks B: "software W8 takes 363 ms."
  - Changes mind if fault tests show locks hold.
- **F — Safety.**
  - Problem: recertification bundling.
  - Prefers: crypto confined to boot/update; failures degrade privileges, never safety functions; last-known-good retained.
  - Attacks A: fail-closed in an SIS is a hazard.
- **G — Auditor.**
  - Problem: unverifiable claims.
  - Prefers: independent labs (India Tier-1/2), TVLA requirements, benchmark methodology.
- **H — Entrepreneur.**
  - Problem: no quantum budget line.
  - Prefers: sell compliance evidence and adoption tooling; PQ comes as a by-product.
  - Changes mind on a paid pilot.

**Convergence:**
- **Agreement:** a hash-based locked first stage, closed side doors, and a symmetric process bus.
- **Unavoidable trade-off:** diversity (A) versus surface (B). Resolve with diversity in slots and assumption classes, one formally verified verifier per class.
- **Clearly superior point:** C's adoption argument.
- **Class-dependent:** hash engines for new designs, enclaves for legacy.
- **Unresolved:** custodian acceptance and assessor acceptance.

---

## 13. Redesigned minimal and high-assurance architectures

### 13.1 Continuity First Stage (replaces ACK)

**Minimal (MV-1):**
- A locked first stage of ≈13 KB (inherited verifier figure 12,864 B).
- One hash verifier: LMS W4 (2,508 B; ≈42 ms at 64 MHz in software), or SLH-DSA-few once final.
- Three key-hash slots (96 B) and an index floor.
- Advancing the floor needs a signature from a different slot.
- No time, no network.

**High-assurance (HA-1):**
- Hardware SHA-2/SHA-3.
- Slots spanning {LMS-SHA-256, SLH-DSA-SHAKE, ML-DSA-65 verified in the second stage}.
- Single-assumption-safe rule.
- A/B second stages plus last-known-good.
- TLA+-verified state machine and a lock fault-injection campaign.
- UUID-bound PQ debug credentials, with debug disabled after commissioning.

```
[Reset] → [Locked first stage: hash verifier + 3 key-hash slots + floor]
             │ verify (LMS/SLH-DSA)        trust boundary: write-locked flash
             ▼
       [Second stage A | B] ─ ML-DSA/hybrid, protocol PQ
             │ fail → other slot → fail → last-known-good (keep running)
             ▼
       [Application]
Authority: slot1 OEM, slot2 OEM alt-class, slot3 operator/escrow
Floor advance: signature from slot ≠ current
Recovery: board swap (debug disabled)
```

**Compared with the ACK:**
- Removes: new ROM, the sunset fuse, the OTP Merkle manifest and licensing.
- Adds: reliance on lock integrity.

### 13.2 Ownership Continuity (replaces threshold custodian)

- **Minimal:** FDO vouchers with an ML-DSA or SLH-DSA profile, escrowed by contract with the operator. Slot 3 holds the operator's key hash.
- **High-assurance:**
  - Dual-class voucher signatures.
  - An OEM-exit trigger in the contract (end of CRA support, or insolvency).
  - A transfer log witnessed by two parties.

```
OEM ─signs─► Voucher ─► Distributor ─► Integrator ─► Operator  (off-device)
OEM exit: voucher chain + escrow release ─► rewrite slot3 via floor rule
```

### 13.3 Symmetric Process-Bus Fabric (kept)

- **Minimal:**
  - ML-KEM-768 enrolment at commissioning.
  - KDC inside the enclave.
  - Unchanged IEC 62351-6 MACs.
  - Per-device key diversification.
- **High-assurance:** replicated KDC with t-of-n unlock, TPM v1.85 identity, and a GDOI-PQ profile.

### 13.4 Enclave Containment (new)

- **Minimal:** a PQ-terminating gateway, a passive inventory, and a dated replacement plan.
- **High-assurance:** two gateways from different vendors, TPM identity, signed configuration and witnessed logs.

**Essential vs optional:**
- **Essential:** the locked hash first stage, ≥3 slots, the floor rule, closed debug, PQ vouchers, symmetric bus and enclaves.
- **Optional:** threshold crypto, custodians, pre-rotation, a time layer, insurance pricing.

---

## 14. Real-world deployment scenarios

| Scenario | Architecture | Key steps | Failure points | Remaining risk |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A New device | HA-1 + FDO-PQ + PQ debug | Choose lockable MCU with hash engine; provision 3 key hashes; disable debug at commissioning; ship voucher | CM key injection (N8) | Lock faults |
| B Unmodifiable device | Enclave | Inventory; segment; PQ gateway; schedule replacement | Gateway concentration | No device-level guarantee |
| C Mixed-vendor | Zone enclaves + CBOM + FDO where supported | Map authority incl. debug and EWS; per-class controls | Integrator handover | Divergent state |
| D Long-disconnected | MV-1 W4; Merkle future keys if needed | Pre-commit; update at visits | Freeze attack | Future-key secrecy |
| E OEM gone | Voucher escrow → slot 3 | Present chain; advance floor | Voucher lost | Without slot 3: replace |
| F Emergency break | Floor advance via other-class slot | Sign with SLH-DSA/LMS; stage next window or emergency window | $124,669/h median downtime (ABB 2023 survey [V]) | Single-class assets |
| G Fleet transition | Window staging | Inventory → lab rehearsal → staged → signed state reports | Non-adoption | 1–2 window cycles |

**Survives deployment:** locked stage, enclaves, staging, debug closure, vouchers.

**Paper-only:** custodian, threshold ML-DSA, time layer, insurance pricing.

---

## 15. Economic and commercial analysis

**Who is who:**
- The operator experiences the problem and owns the asset.
- The OEM controls the technical decision.
- The operator's maintenance budget pays.
- Liability is split by the CRA: OEM during support, operator after.
- The integrator implements.
- Plant engineering and procurement can block.

**Model for a 10,000-device fleet [E].** Assumptions:
- 20 devices per visit.
- Full visit $1,000; planned-window marginal cost 25% of that.
- 33% of emergency visits need a second trip (Aberdeen, via a vendor blog).
- r = remotely updatable share. No source measures r, so it is varied.

| r | Visits | Planned ($250/visit) | Emergency ($1,000 × 1.33) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.1 | 450 | $112,500 | $598,500 |
| 0.4 | 300 | $75,000 | $399,000 |
| 0.7 | 150 | $37,500 | $199,500 |

**Downtime sensitivity:**
- If each emergency visit at r = 0.1 causes one hour at ABB's survey median ($124,669, rounded here to $125,000): 450 × $125,000 = $56.25M.
- If only 5% of visits do: $2.81M.

**Reason to act early:** converting emergency work into planned-window work is worth roughly $2.8M–$56M per 10,000 devices, against $0.04M–$0.11M for planned visits. Downtime figures are vendor-sponsored and plant-oriented.

**Market (inherited, corrected):** $0.38B–$1.88B global; $38M–$188M for a 10% vendor. Not for fundraising.

**Business models, ranked by fit:**
1. Open first-stage reference plus a paid porting and assurance service.
2. Compliance-evidence product (CRA Article 14, India CII inventories).
3. Window-aligned migration engineering.
4. Procurement-clause libraries and test-lab services.

Weak fits: per-device subscriptions and a custodian utility.

**Value without quantum concern:** firmware governance, update adoption, OEM-exit protection, compliance evidence.

---

## 16. The strongest practical starting points

| Entry | Smallest prototype | Standalone value | Credibility evidence | Next |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Continuity first stage | wolfBoot/MCUboot-derived locked stage on STM32U5/H5 and nRF5340, LMS W4, 3 slots | OEM "PQ-ready boot" claim | Boot time, fault tests, TLA+ | 5 families; SLH-DSA-few |
| Side-door audit kit | Checklist + scripts mapping debug, lifecycle, ownership, provisioning per part | Real authority map | Audit of 10 MCU families | PQ debug profile |
| FDO-PQ voucher | PQ entries in open FDO | OEM-exit continuity | 2-OEM interop | FIDO contribution |
| Window staging | Signed staging + state report on lab fleet | Adoption | Pilot adoption rate | Product |

**Roadmap A (0–9 months):**
- Build the audit kit and first-stage prototype; run E-A1 to E-A4.
- **Gate:** if fewer than 50% of MCU families are lockable, pivot to silicon procurement asks.

**Roadmap B (9–24 months):**
- Open release with formal verification.
- FDO-PQ contribution.
- Evidence product.
- Indian power/telecom CII pilots.
- **Gate:** one paid pilot. Abandon the evidence product if incumbents ship it first.

**Roadmap C (2–10 years):**
- Procurement standard text: hash engine, ≥3 slots over ≥2 classes, PQ debug and vouchers, no field debug.
- Submissions to IEC 62443-4-2 and national lab criteria.
- **Gate:** two OEM adopters.

---

## 17. Prioritised experiments

| ID | Question | Experiment | Cost | Support / disprove | Decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E-A1 | Share of industrial MCU families with lockable first stage | Desk survey: ST, NXP, Renesas, Microchip, TI, Infineon, Nordic, Silicon Labs, Espressif, GigaDevice | 2 weeks | ≥50% / <30% | New ROM or not |
| E-A2 | Verify cost of LMS W4/W8, SP 800-230, ML-DSA-44 on M0+/M33 ± HW SHA | Owned boards | 4 weeks | ≤100 ms / >300 ms | Algorithm |
| E-A3 | Which authorisation paths are classical | Docs + lab | 3 weeks | — | Clauses |
| E-A4 | Can option-byte locks be faulted | Voltage/EM glitching on owned parts | 6 weeks | Hold / fail | Flash vs ROM |
| E-B1 | Real remote-update adoption | Read-only measurement at consenting operator | 3 months | — | Priority |
| E-B2 | Single-assumption rule | TLA+/Tamarin | 6 weeks | No path / counterexample | Slots |
| E-B3 | FDO-PQ interop | Open FDO + PQ | 8 weeks | Works / size breaks | Standard |
| E-B4 | Floor-burn attack | NSIB-style lab demo | 2 weeks | Exploitable / not | Floor rule |
| E-C1 | Assessor acceptance | Interviews with two bodies | 2 months | Accept / reject | Safety |
| E-C2 | Independent review | Third-party audit | Budget | — | Release |

**Highest information value:** E-A1. It is cheap and decides the whole silicon-ROM strand.

All experiments run on owned or authorised equipment only.

---

## 18. Independent challenge-board findings

- **Main misunderstanding:** that the root is the bottleneck. Adoption and side doors are.
- **Main unsolved problem:** installed devices with classical-only immutable verifiers and no lock. Only containment and replacement help them.
- **Impressive but commercially irrelevant:** ACK ROM licensing; threshold ML-DSA succession.
- **Attractive but technically weak:** unverified "PQ-ready" labels; attestation-priced insurance.
- **Unnecessary components:** OTP Merkle manifest, sunset latch, custodian, time layer.
- **Concentration risks:** the KDC, enclave gateways, and any root-of-roots.
- **Overemphasised:** offline revocation, time, pre-rotation.
- **Underemphasised:** debug/RMA, vouchers, factory provisioning, adoption.
- **Existing tech doing more than recognised:** NSIB/OEMiRoT, FDO, TPM v1.85, SP 800-230.
- **Strongest argument against the lead:** fault injection can bypass flash locks, and first-stage bugs are permanent.
- **Abandonment evidence:** E-A4 shows a reliable bypass, or E-A1 shows few lockable families.
- **Overworked assumptions:** that operators update when it is cheap; that SP 800-230 finalises as drafted.
- **Operator objection:** any new outage or cloud dependency.
- **Cryptographer objection:** treating SHA-2 and SHA-3 as fully independent.
- **15-year liability:** the open first stage needs a maintainer foundation.
- **Vendor disappears:** survivable only if slot 3 is provisioned at manufacture.
- **Obsolescence:** three slots give one generation of headroom, not unlimited headroom.

---

## 19. Revised rankings

**Criteria:** security gain, deployability on existing silicon, burden removed, evidence and pull. Tested under security-heavy (40/20/15/15/10) and deployability-heavy (20/35/20/15/10) weightings.

| Rank | Invention | Prior rank | Robust to weights? |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Locked continuity first stage (dual-class slots, floor rule) | 1, 3 | Yes |
| 2 | Side-door closure: PQ debug, field-debug disable, lifecycle audit | new | Yes |

## Caveats

- **Coverage:** far below the 500–600 sources invited. Unrechecked items include quantum estimates beyond Google's, threshold ML-DSA results, TI/NXP/ST PQ ROM claims, IEC 62351 details and the OPC UA roadmap.
- **Secondary sourcing:** IR 8547 final and EO 14412 rest on secondary reports.
- **Vendor-sponsored data:** the economic figures come from Siemens, ABB, Dragos, Claroty, Armis and aDolus. No survey measures remote updatability.
- **Draft standard:** SP 800-230 may change before it is final.
- **Estimate:** hardware-SHA boot scaling assumes hash-dominated cost.

I've written **OT-Embedded-PQC-Unified-Atlas.md**, about 18,000 words. It rewrites all the research passes, the verification lab and both challenge rounds into one argument instead of stacking them. Where a later pass overturned an earlier belief, only the belief that survived is in the main text. The overturned ones are kept in a corrections ledger, so you can see how the thinking changed. I re-ran all the arithmetic in the document and it checks out.

The conclusion it reaches:
- **The real problem.** The quantum computer isn't the deepest issue. The deeper one is that long-lived industrial devices can't safely accept change: a new algorithm, a new owner, new software, or an authority that outlives the company that built them.
- **What to build first, in order:**
  1. A small post-quantum boot stage stored in locked flash. Many existing chips can do this today without new silicon.
  2. Closing the "side doors" around secure boot: debug unlock, ownership transfer, factory key loading and engineering workstations. These still rely on classical signatures, so a quantum attacker could use them without touching the boot chain.
  3. Keeping real-time plant traffic on symmetric cryptography, with public-key work done once at commissioning.
  4. Post-quantum ownership vouchers, with the voucher files held in escrow, so the fleet survives if the manufacturer disappears.
  5. Scheduling cryptographic updates into maintenance windows that already exist.
  6. Walling off devices that can't be changed, with a firm replacement date.
- **The hard limit.** Some installed devices can't be rescued by cryptography. Every device shipped from now on should be able to accept a post-quantum authority later.

The document also adds new material from reading everything together:
- **Two new root causes:** updates exist but don't get installed, and authorisation paths nobody has mapped.
- **A map of six separate authority chains** reaching each device, not just secure boot.
- **A device-class model** with rules that stop a weak device from becoming a path into a critical one.
- **Twelve new invention ideas, labelled as untested**, for example an "authorisation surface map" for every product and spare parts that only take on ownership when installed.

Three things to keep in mind:
- **Thin sourcing.** The two most recent passes drew on about 130 sources, far fewer than the 500–600 you invited. The source lists from the earlier passes aren't reproduced.
- **Weak inputs.** Several key inputs are vendor claims, draft standards or secondary reports, and each is labelled in the text.
- **Leftovers.** The earliest broad opportunity scans appear only as the starting point. Two benchmark conflicts remain open and are listed: the memory needed for one signing algorithm, and the size of the new draft signature sets.

The cheapest next step is a two-week desk survey of how many industrial microcontroller families can lock a first boot stage in flash. That result decides whether new chips are needed at all.
